Favard length and quantitative rectifiability Thanks: D.D. is supported by the Research Council of Finland postdoctoral grant Quantitative rectifiability and harmonic measure beyond the Ahlfors-David-regular setting , grant No. 347123.
Damian Dąbrowski

Abstract
. The Favard length of a Borel set E ⊂ ℝ 2 E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} is the average length of its orthogonal projections. We prove that if E E is Ahlfors 1-regular and it has large Favard length, then it contains a big piece of a Lipschitz graph. This gives a quantitative version of the Besicovitch projection theorem. As a corollary, we answer questions of David and Semmes and of Peres and Solomyak. We also make progress on Vitushkin’s conjecture.



1.
Introduction

Favard length of a Borel set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the average length of its orthogonal projections. More precisely,

\Fav(E)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))\,d\theta.

This quantity is also referred to as
Buffon’s needle probability
.

The Besicovitch projection theorem
[
Bes39
]
, which is one of the foundational results of geometric measure theory, asserts that if a Borel set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfies
0<\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)<\infty
and
\Fav(E)>0
, then there exists a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
such that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap\Gamma)>0.

It is a purely qualitative result: it gives no estimates on
\lip(\Gamma)
or
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap\Gamma)
.

The
Favard length problem
consists of quantifying Besicovitch’s theorem. Different versions of the problem have been proposed by many authors, see e.g.
[
PS02
, Section 7.3]
,
[
Mat04
, Problem 4]
,
[
DS93b
, p. 857]
,
[
DS93a
, p. 29]
,
[
Orp21
, Question 1]
. Our main theorem solves the Favard length problem in the class of Ahlfors regular sets.

Definition
.

We say that
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
Ahlfors regular with constant

A\geq 1
if
E
is closed and for all
x\in E,

0<r<\diam(E),
we have

A^{-1}r\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap B(x,r))\leq Ar.

If the constant
A
does not matter, we simply say that
E
is Ahlfors regular.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
0<\kappa<1
and
A\geq 1
. Suppose that
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is Ahlfors regular with constant
A
, and that
\Fav(E)\geq\kappa\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
.

Then, there exists a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
with
\lip(\Gamma)\lesssim_{\kappa,A}1
such that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap\Gamma)\gtrsim_{\kappa,A}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

Remark 1.2
.

We get explicit estimates for
\lip(\Gamma)
and
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap\Gamma)
in terms of
A
and
\kappa
, see (
4.10
) and (
4.11
).

Remark 1.3
.

It is not difficult to reduce the proof of Theorem
1.1
to the case of finite unions of parallel segments, see Appendix
A
. Consequently, we do not need to use the classical Besicovitch projection theorem in our proof.

As a corollary, we get positive answers to a question of David and Semmes
[
DS93a
, p. 29]
, a question of Orponen
[
Orp21
, Question 1]
, and a problem posed by Peres and Solomyak
[
PS02
, Question 7.5]
and Mattila
[
Mat04
, Problem 4]
. Finally, we make progress on Vitushkin’s conjecture by showing that sets with uniformly large Favard length have large analytic capacity. We describe these corollaries in the following subsections.

Remark 1.4
.

All the problems mentioned in this introduction have natural counterparts for
n
-dimensional projections in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. It seems plausible the proof of Theorem
1.1
can be generalized to that setting, and we hope to address this in a later version of this preprint.

1.1.
Quantitative rectifiability

The field of quantitative rectifiability was born in the 1990s after the breakthrough works of Jones
[
Jon91
]
and David and Semmes
[
DS91
,
DS93a
]
, motivated by the study of singular integrals and analytic capacity. The central objects of interest in this area are
uniformly rectifiable sets
, which are quantitative counterparts of the usual rectifiable sets. An important subclass of uniformly rectifiable sets are the sets with big pieces of Lipschitz graphs.

Definition
.

A set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
has
big pieces of Lipschitz graphs
(BPLG) if
E
is Ahlfors regular and there exist constants
L>0
and
\theta\in(0,1)
such that for every
x\in E
and
0<r<\diam(E)
there exists a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma_{x,r}
such that
\lip(\Gamma_{x,r})\leq L
and

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap B(x,r)\cap\Gamma_{x,r})\geq\theta r.

In
[
DS93a
, p. 29]
David and Semmes asked the following.

Question 1.5

(David-Semmes)
.

Suppose that
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is an Ahlfors regular set with
uniformly large Favard length
(ULFL), i.e. it satisfies
\Fav(E\cap B(x,r))\gtrsim r
for all
x\in E
and
0<r<\diam(E)
. Is
E
uniformly rectifiable?

A positive answer follows immediately from Theorem
1.1
.

Corollary 1.6
.

Suppose that
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is Ahlfors regular and satisfies ULFL. Then,
E
has BPLG.

Remark 1.7
.

It is easy to check that the converse implication also holds: sets with BPLG satisfy ULFL, simply because large subsets of Lipschitz graphs have many big projections, see
[
MO18
, Proposition 1.4]
. Thus, ULFL is equivalent to BPLG in the class of Ahlfors regular sets.

Proof.

Let
x\in E
and
0<r<\diam(E)
. By
[
BHH+22
, Lemma 2.2]
there exists a compact set
E_{x,r}
which is Ahlfors regular with constant
\lesssim 1
, and such that
E\cap B(x,r/3)\subset E_{x,r}\subset E\cap B(x,r)
. Since

\Fav(E_{x,r})\geq\Fav(E\cap B(x,r/3))\gtrsim r\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{1}(E_{x,r}),

it follows from Theorem
1.1
that there exists a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma_{x,r}
with
\lip(\Gamma_{x,r})\lesssim 1
and

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap B(x,r)\cap\Gamma_{x,r})\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(E_{x,r}\cap\Gamma_{x,r})\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{1}(E_{x,r})\gtrsim r.

∎

Related work

Theorem
1.1
implies the (planar versions of) results from three recent papers marking progress on the Favard length problem
[
MO18
,
Orp21
,
Dąb22b
]
. We discuss these results below.

In
[
DS93b
, p. 857]
David and Semmes asked a question similar to Question
1.5
. The only difference was that the ULFL condition was strengthened to the
plenty of big projections
(PBP) condition: for every
x\in E
,
0<r<\diam(E)
there exists
\theta_{x,r}\in[0,1]
such that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E\cap B(x,r)))\geq\delta r\quad\text{for all }|\theta-\theta_{x,r}|\leq\delta.

This conjecture (and its higher dimensional version) was recently confirmed in a breakthrough article of Orponen
[
Orp21
]
. In the same paper
[
Orp21
, Question 1]
it was asked whether a single-scale version of PBP, i.e.

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E\cap B(0,1)))\geq\delta\quad\text{for all }|\theta-\theta_{0}|\leq\delta,

(1.1)

already implies the existance of a big piece of a Lipschitz graph, with controlled constants. Since (
1.1
) implies
\Fav(E\cap B(0,1))\gtrsim\delta^{2}
, this follows readily from Theorem
1.1
.

We remark that the proof of Theorem
1.1
is very different from that in
[
Orp21
]
, and we do not use Orponen’s result. On the other hand, we crucially use the methods and results from two other papers where progress on the Favard length problem was made, namely
[
MO18
]
and
[
Dąb22b
]
.

In
[
MO18
]
it is shown that if
E
is Ahlfors regular, and
F\subset E
is large and satisfies

\int_{I}\|\pi_{\theta}\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{F}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,d\theta\lesssim 1

(1.2)

for a large interval
I\subset[0,1]
, then
F
contains a big piece of a Lipschitz graph. Just like the PBP condition, assumption (
1.2
) should be thought of as a strengthening of
\Fav(E)\gtrsim 1
: it follows from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

1\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)}\int_{I}\pi_{\theta}\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{F}(\pi_{\theta}(F))\,d\theta\lesssim\int_{I}\|\pi_{\theta}\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{F}\|_{L^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(F))^{1/2}\,d\theta\\
\leq\left(\int_{I}\|\pi_{\theta}\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{F}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,d\theta\right)^{1/2}\Fav(F)^{1/2}\overset{\eqref{eq:MOasssump}}{\lesssim}\Fav(E)^{1/2}.

In the proof of Theorem
1.1
we use the main technical proposition from
[
MO18
]
as a black-box, see Theorem
3.17
.

In
[
Dąb22b
]
it was shown that if
E
is Ahlfors regular and

\|\pi_{\theta}\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{E}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1\quad\text{for }\theta\in G,

where
G\subset[0,1]
satisfies
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)\sim 1
, then
E
contains a big piece of a Lipschitz graph. Note that this condition implies
\Fav(E)\gtrsim 1
. Compared to (
1.2
), the gain is that the set of good directions
G
is only assumed to have large measure, but does not have to be an interval. This came at a price of strengthening the
L^{2}
estimate on projections to an
L^{\infty}
estimate. The proof of Theorem
1.1
uses many of the ideas and tools from
[
Dąb22b
]
, and we discuss this in detail in Section
2
.

Regarding other work on the Favard length problem, in
[
DS93b
]
David and Semmes showed that a single big projection together with
weak geometric lemma
gives a big piece of a Lipschitz graph. In
[
CDOV24
]
a version of Theorem
1.1
was proven with assumption
\Fav(E)\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
replaced by “almost maximality of Favard length”, and with a correspondingly stronger conclusion.

Regarding the classical Besicovitch projection theorem, its higher dimensional generalization is due to Federer
[
Fed47
]
, see also
[
Whi98
]
. A version for metric spaces is due to Bate
[
Bat20
]
. Different variants of the Besicovitch projection theorem have also been shown for
generalized projections

[
HJJL12
,
BV11
,
CDT20
,
BT23
,
DT22
]
.

1.2.
Vitushkin’s conjecture

In 1967 Vitushkin conjectured
[
Vit67
]
that a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
has positive
analytic capacity
if and only if
\Fav(E)>0
. Instead of defining analytic capacity, which is denoted by
\gamma(E)
, let us only say that
\gamma(E)=0
if and only if
E
is
removable for bounded analytic functions
: all bounded analytic function defined on
\mathbb{C}\setminus E
are constant.

Over the last 50 years plenty of work has been done towards the solution of Vitushkin’s conjecture, and we only scratch the surface by saying that it was shown to be true for sets
E
with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)<\infty
in
[
Cal77
,
Dav98
]
and false for sets
E
with infinite length
[
Mat86
,
JM88
]
. See
[
Tol14
,
Ver21
]
for the full historical account.

There is one outstanding open question remaining from the Vitushkin’s conjecture.

Question 1.8
.

Suppose that
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is compact. Is it true that

\Fav(E)>0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\gamma(E)>0?

(1.3)

One can also ask whether a stronger, quantitative result is true.

Question 1.9
.

Suppose that
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is compact and
\Fav(E)\geq\kappa\diam(E)
for some
\kappa>0
. Is it true that

\gamma(E)\gtrsim_{\kappa}\diam(E)?

(1.4)

Remark 1.10
.

In (
1.4
) the best dependence on
\kappa
one can hope for is linear, which would be equivalent to an estimate
\gamma(E)\gtrsim\Fav(E)
. This is because
\gamma(E)\sim\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)\sim\Fav(E)
for subsets of lines (see
[
Tol14
, Proposition 1.22]
or
[
Pom60
]
) and circles
[
Mur90
]
.

The answer to Question
1.8
is known to be positive for sets with
\sigma
-finite length by
[
Cal77
,
Tol03
]
, but is open otherwise. Until now Question
1.9
was open even for Ahlfors regular sets. Thanks to Theorem
1.1
we make progress on Question
1.9
.

Firstly, we answer it in the positive for general compact sets satisfying ULFL. We do not assume
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)<\infty
. Before it was not even known whether such sets have positive analytic capacity.

Corollary 1.11
.

Suppose that a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfies the ULFL condition, i.e. there exists
\kappa>0
such that
\Fav(E\cap B(x,r))\geq\kappa r
for all
x\in E
and
0<r<\diam(E)
. Then,

\gamma(E)\gtrsim_{\kappa}\diam(E).

Proof.

This follows immediately from Corollary
1.6
together with the proof of
[
DV22
, Theorem 1.3]
, see
[
DV22
, Remark 1.9]
. In
[
DV22
, Theorem 1.3]
Villa and the author showed a version of Corollary
1.11
with ULFL replaced by the stronger PBP condition. In the proof we used Orponen’s result
[
Orp21
]
as a black-box, and replacing all references to
[
Orp21
]
by Corollary
1.6
gives Corollary
1.11
.
∎

Secondly, we get a positive answer to Question
1.9
for Ahlfors regular sets.

Corollary 1.12
.

Suppose that a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is Ahlfors regular with constant
A
and satisfies
\Fav(E)\geq\kappa\diam(E)
. Then,

\gamma(E)\gtrsim_{\kappa,A}\diam(E).

(1.5)

Proof.

By Theorem
1.1
there exists a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
with
\lip(\Gamma)\lesssim_{A,\kappa}1
and such that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap\Gamma)\gtrsim_{A,\kappa}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\diam(\Gamma)\lesssim\diam(E)
simply by truncating the graph
\Gamma
.

By Verdera’s quantitative solution to Denjoy’s conjecture
[
Tol14
, Theorem 4.31]

\gamma(E)\gtrsim\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}(E\cap\Gamma)^{3/2}}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)^{1/2}}\gtrsim_{\kappa,A}\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}(E\cap\Gamma)^{3/2}}{\diam(\Gamma)^{1/2}}\gtrsim\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}(E\cap\Gamma)^{3/2}}{\diam(E)^{1/2}}.

Finally, since
\Gamma
is a bilispchitz image of
\mathbb{R}
with bilispchitz constant
\lesssim_{\kappa,A}1
, and the Hausdorff content
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}
restricted to
\mathbb{R}
is equal to
\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{\mathbb{R}}
, we get that

\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}(E\cap\Gamma)\gtrsim_{A,\kappa}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap\Gamma)\gtrsim_{A,\kappa}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)\sim_{A}\diam(E).

Together with the previous estimates this gives
\gamma(E)\gtrsim_{\kappa,A}\diam(E)
.
∎

Related work

Question
1.9
was answered in the positive for sets satisfying PBP in
[
DV22
, Theorem 1.3]
. In
[
CT20
]
Chang and Tolsa showed a lower bound on analytic capacity of the form

\gamma(E)\geq c_{I}\frac{\mu(E)^{2}}{\int_{I}\|\pi_{\theta}\mu\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,d\theta},

(1.6)

whenever
\mu
is a measure on
\mathbb{C}
with
0<\int_{I}\|\pi_{\theta}\mu\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,d\theta<\infty
and
I
is an interval. Since
\Fav(E)
satisfies a very similar lower bound (see estimate (1.5) in
[
CT20
]
) this suggests that the optimal dependence on
\kappa
in (
1.4
) might be linear.

1.3.
Peres-Solomyak question

The following question was posed in
[
PS02
, Question 7.5]
. Given a compact set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
0<\delta<\diam(E)
consider

\ell(E,\delta)=\sup_{\Gamma}\,\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}(\Gamma(\delta)\cap E),

(1.7)

where the supremum is taken over all rectifiable curves
\Gamma
of length
\diam(E)
*
*

*

In
[
PS02
]
in the definition of
\ell(E,\delta)
it is assumed that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)=1
. We changed their definition slightly to get linear scaling, so that
\ell(CE,C\delta)=C\ell(E,\delta)
.
, and
\Gamma(\delta)
denotes the
\delta
-neigborhood of
\Gamma
.

Observe that if
E
is purely unrectifiable (i.e.
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap\Gamma)=0
for all rectifiable curves
\Gamma
) then
\ell(E,\delta)\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
. At the same time, by the Besicovitch projection theorem we have
\Fav(E(\delta))\to 0
for such sets. This inspired the following question.

Question 1.13

(Peres-Solomyak)
.

Is there a quantitative estimate of
\Fav(E(\delta))
in terms of
\ell(E,\delta)
?

See also Problem 4 in
[
Mat04
]
which asked for estimates of
\Fav(E(\delta))
for purely unrectifiable sets, and which can be seen as a variant of this question.

For general Borel sets with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)\sim\diam(E)\sim 1
, say, the answer to Question
1.13
is negative, as shown by the “grid example”, see
[
CDOV24
, Proposition 6.1]
. On the other hand, in the class of Ahlfors regular sets the answer to Question
1.13
turns out to be positive.

Corollary 1.14
.

Suppose that
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a compact Ahlfors regular set. Then, for any
0<\varepsilon<1

\Fav(E(\delta))\leq\frac{C\diam(E)}{\left(\log\log\log(C_{\varepsilon}\diam(E)\ell(E,18\delta)^{-1})\right)^{1/(3+\varepsilon)}},

(1.8)

with
C,C_{\varepsilon}
depending on the Ahlfors regularity constant of
E
, and
C_{\varepsilon}
depending also on
\varepsilon
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality assume that
\delta\in 2^{-\mathbb{N}}
, and set

\kappa\coloneqq\frac{\Fav(E(\delta))}{\diam(E)}.

We will find a lower bound on
\ell(E,9\delta)
in terms of
\kappa
(the change from
18\delta
to
9\delta
comes from the assumption
\delta\in 2^{-\mathbb{N}}
).

Let
\mathfrak{D}(E,\delta)
denote the dyadic cubes of sidelength
\delta
intersecting
E(\delta)
. Set

F\coloneqq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathfrak{D}(E,\delta)}\partial Q.

It is easy to see that
\Fav(F)\geq\Fav(E(\delta))=\kappa\diam(E)\sim\kappa\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{\delta})
, see (
A.2
).
It is also not difficult to show that
F
inherits Ahlfors regularity from the set
E
, see Lemma
A.2
. Thus, Theorem
1.1
together with the explicit bounds (
4.10
) and (
4.11
) gives a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
with
\lip(\Gamma)\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\exp(\exp(C\kappa^{-3-\varepsilon}))
and such that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(F\cap\Gamma)\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}\exp(-\exp(\exp(C\kappa^{-3-\varepsilon})))\mathcal{H}^{1}(F).

Since
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)\lesssim 1+\lip(\Gamma)\sim\lip(\Gamma)
, there exists a curve
\gamma\subset\Gamma
of length
1
such that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(F\cap\gamma)\gtrsim\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(F\cap\Gamma)}{\lip(\Gamma)}\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}\exp\big(-\exp(\exp(C\kappa^{-3-\varepsilon}))-\exp(C\kappa^{-3-\varepsilon}))\big)\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)\\
\geq\exp(-\exp(\exp(C^{\prime}\kappa^{-3-\varepsilon})))\mathcal{H}^{1}(F).

Remark that
\Gamma
is a bilispchitz image of
\mathbb{R}
with bilispchitz constant
\lesssim\lip(\Gamma)
. Since the Hausdorff content
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}
restricted to
\mathbb{R}
is equal to
\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{\mathbb{R}}
, we get that

\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}(F\cap\gamma)\gtrsim\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(F\cap\gamma)}{\lip(\Gamma)}\gtrsim_{\varepsilon}\exp(-\exp(\exp(C^{\prime\prime}\kappa^{-3-\varepsilon})))\mathcal{H}^{1}(F).

(1.9)

It remains to show that

\ell(E,9\delta)\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}(\gamma(9\delta)\cap E)\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}(F\cap\gamma).

(1.10)

In Appendix
C
we show that if
E
is Ahlfors regular and
\gamma
is arbitrary, then
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}(\gamma(9\delta)\cap E)\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{1}_{\infty}(E(3\delta)\cap\gamma)
. Since
F\subset E(3\delta)
, (
1.10
) follows. Together with (
1.9
) and recalling that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)\sim\diam(E)
, we obtain the desired estimate (
1.8
).
∎

Related work

Suppose that
K\subset[0,1]^{2}
is the classical 4-corners Cantor set, so that
K=C_{1/2}\times C_{1/2}
where
C_{1/2}=\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}4^{-i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}a_{i}\in\{0,3\}\}
. It is well-known (and it can be shown e.g. using the Analyst’s Travelling Salesman Theorem
[
Jon91
]
) that

\ell(K,4^{-n})\sim\frac{1}{n}.

Thus, Corollary
1.14
implies for
n
large enough

\Fav(K(4^{-n}))\lesssim\frac{1}{\left(\log\log\log n\right)^{1/(3+\varepsilon)}}.

(1.11)

The rate of decay of
\Fav(E(\delta))
, with
E
either the 4-corners Cantor set or more general self-similar and random sets, has been an area of intense study over the last two decades, see
[
Mat90
,
PS02
,
Tao09
,
ŁZ10
,
BV10a
,
BV10b
,
NPV11
,
BŁV14
,
Łab14
,
Wil17
,
Bon19
,
ŁM22
,
VV24
]
. The problem of obtaining such bounds is called by many authors the Favard length problem, or the Buffon’s needle problem.

With the exception of
[
PS02
]
and
[
Tao09
]
, the upper bounds on
\Fav(E(\delta))
obtained in aforementioned articles are vastly better than (
1.11
). For example, the best known estimate for the 4-corners Cantor set is

\Fav(K(4^{-n}))\lesssim\frac{1}{n^{c}}

(1.12)

for some
0<c<1/6
, see
[
NPV11
]
. It has been conjectured that (
1.12
) should hold for every
0<c<1
(with implicit constant depending on
c
). The estimate fails for
c=1
by
[
BV10a
]
.

Thus, Corollary
1.14
is of little use when restricting attention to sets with plenty of extra structure offered by self-similarity or randomness. The novelty of our estimate is that it holds without any additional assumptions beyond Ahlfors regularity.

It remains an interesting open question to find out optimal dependence on
\ell(E,\delta)
in such estimates (we imagine (
1.8
) is far from optimal). Other than improving (
1.8
), one can also seek lower bounds for
\Fav(E(\delta))
. By
[
BV10a
]
for the 4-corners Cantor set we have

\Fav(K(\delta))\gtrsim\ell(K,\delta)\log(\ell(K,\delta)^{-1}).

(1.13)

Problem 1.15
.

Find examples of Ahlfors regular sets
E
improving the lower bound (
1.13
).

In
[
Wil17
]
purely unrectifiable sets with arbitrarily slow decay of
\Fav(E(\delta))
have been constructed. It is not clear to us whether these examples imply a better lower bound of the type (
1.13
) than the 4-corners Cantor set.

In
[
Tao09
]
an estimate on
\Fav(E(\delta))
has been shown that in some regards is even more general than Corollary
1.14
. Instead of Ahlfors regularity the set is only assumed to be roughly Ahlfors regular along a fixed sequence of scales. However, to apply Tao’s result one also needs to estimate
rectifiability constants
of
E
along that scale sequence. It is not clear to us whether it is possible to obtain an estimate on
\Fav(E(\delta))
in terms of
\ell(E,\delta)
using Tao’s result. In the case of the 4-corners Cantor set, Tao’s result implies an estimate of
\Fav(K(4^{-n}))
in terms of the inverse tower function
\log_{*}(n)
, which is substantially weaker than (
1.11
).

2.
Outline of the proof

2.1.
Conical energies

Before outlining the proof of Theorem
1.1
, we describe our key tool: the
conical energies
. They appeared implicitly for the first time in
[
MO18
]
, were explicitly introduced in
[
CT20
]
, and further explored in
[
Dąb22a
,
Dąb22c
]
. Similar quantities have also been used in
[
BN21
]
to characterize measures carried by Lipschitz graphs.

The conical energy of measure
\mu
at point
x
with respect to a set of angles
I\subset[0,1]
is the quantity

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x,I)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu(X(x,I,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r},

where
X(x,I,r)
is the cone centered at
x
associated to angles from
I
and truncated at scale
r
. We do not assume
a priori
that
I
is an interval. There are three main reasons why conical energies are an excellent tool for finding big pieces of Lipschitz graphs.

Firstly, if the set of angles
I
is an interval, and
\mu
is Ahlfors regular with
E=\supp\mu
, then one can use
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x,I)
to estimate the number of “bad scales”: the scales
j\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the double truncated cone
X(x,0.9I,2^{-j-1},2^{-j})
intersects
E
:

\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.9I)=\#\{j\in\mathbb{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X(x,0.9I,2^{-j-1},2^{-j})\cap E\neq\varnothing\}\\
\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x,I).

(2.1)

See Lemma
3.19
for a more precise statement. This was observed in
[
MO18
, Lemma 1.9]
, and used extensively in
[
CT20
,
Dąb22a
,
Dąb22b
]
.

Estimating
\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,I)
is relevant to us for the following reason: suppose that
F\subset E
is such that
\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,I)=0
for all
x\in F
. Then,
F\cap X(x,I)=\varnothing
for all
x\in F
, and consequently
F\subset\Gamma
for some Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
with
\lip(\Gamma)\sim\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)^{-1}
. Moreover, in
[
MO18
]
it was shown that as soon as
\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,I)\leq M_{0}
for all
x\in F\subset E
, then
F
contains a big piece of a Lipschitz graph. See Theorem
3.17
for a precise statement. Thus, in order to find a big piece of a Lipschitz graph in
E
, it suffices to find a large interval
I\subset[0,1]
and a big piece
F\subset E
with uniformly bounded
\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,I)
(or uniformly bounded
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x,I)
, by (
2.1
)).

The second reason conical energies are very useful is that they are “additive in angles”: observe that if
I_{1},\dots I_{n}\subset[0,1]
are disjoint and
J\coloneqq\bigcup_{j}I_{j}
then

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x,J)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x,I_{j}).

(2.2)

Thus, if we are able to show
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x,I_{j})\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(I_{j})
, and
J=\bigcup_{j}I_{j}
happens to be an interval, then by (
2.1
)

\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.9J)\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x,J)=\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x,I_{j})\\
\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I_{j})=1.

This idea was used in
[
Dąb22b
]
. It is not clear to us whether one can directly estimate
\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.9J)\lesssim 1
knowing that
\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,I_{j})\lesssim 1
for all
j
, and so bounding conical energies presents an advantage over estimating
\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,I_{j})
directly.

Finally, conical energies are very well suited for stopping-time arguments and multi-scale decompositions in the spirit of David and Semmes, which was exploited in
[
CT20
,
Dąb22a
,
Dąb22b
]
.

2.2.
Sketch of the proof of Theorem
1.1

For the sake of simplicity, we make some claims below which are not literally true (e.g. we omit error terms, assumptions, we claim estimates that in reality look different). We only concentrate on communicating the key ideas and difficulties.

Suppose that
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is Ahlfors regular and satisfies
\Fav(E)\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
. Let
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{E}
, and suppose that
\diam(E)=1
. We want to find a big piece of a Lipschitz graph inside
E
.

As mentioned in the previous subsection, by Theorem
3.17
and Lemma
3.19
it suffices to find
F\subset E
with
\mu(F)\gtrsim\mu(E)
and a large interval
J_{0}\subset[0,1]
such that
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x,J_{0})\lesssim 1
for all
x\in F
. By Chebyshev’s inequality, this is equivalent to finding
E^{\prime}\subset E
with
\mu(E^{\prime})\gtrsim\mu(E)
and such that

\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,J_{0},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\lesssim\mu(E).

(2.3)

Using the assumption
\Fav(E)\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
it is not too difficult to prove the following: there exist an interval
J_{0}\subset[0,1]
and a set
E^{\prime}\subset E
with
\mu(E^{\prime})\gtrsim\mu(E)
such that for every
x\in E^{\prime}
there exists a set of good directions
G(x)\subset J_{0}
satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\gtrsim 1
and

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(x,G(x))=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim 1.

(2.4)

This is done in Lemma
3.5
. There are two big problems preventing us from concluding (
2.3
):

(a)

G(x)
need not be an interval. The best we can get is that it is a finite union of intervals, but with no bound on the number of intervals nor their size.

(b)

G(x)
may vary wildly between different points.

Let us ignore the second issue for a moment, and assume that for some fixed set
G_{0}\subset J_{0}
we have
G(x)=G_{0}
for all
x\in E^{\prime}
. Assume also that
E^{\prime}=E
. This is essentially the setup we were able to deal with in
[
Dąb22b
]
. In
[
Dąb22b
, Corollary 4.3]
we showed that in this situation
the good directions propagate
: we can find a new good set
G_{1}
such that
G_{0}\subset G_{1}\subset J_{0}
,
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{1})\geq(1+\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0})
with
\varepsilon\sim 1
, and

\int_{E}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{1},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\lesssim\int_{E}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{0},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\overset{\eqref{eq:Gxest}}{\lesssim}\mu(E).

(2.5)

Since the size of the new good set has increased by
\sim\varepsilon
, after at most
k\sim\varepsilon^{-1}
iterative applications of
[
Dąb22b
, Corollary 4.3]
we get that
G_{k}=J_{0}
and so

\int_{E}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,J_{0},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1}\mu(E),

which was our goal (
2.3
).

Since it is relevant to the proof of Theorem
1.1
, we describe briefly the proof of
[
Dąb22b
, Corollary 4.3]
. Firstly, the new good set
G_{1}
is defined as follows. Start by covering
G_{0}
with a family of maximal dyadic intervals
\mathcal{G}_{1}
such that for
I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I\cap G_{0})\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)
. Note that such intervals may be arbitrarily short. Then, denoting by
I^{1}
the dyadic parent of
I
, we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{1}\cap G_{0})\leq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{1})
, and it is easy to see that
G_{1}\coloneqq\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}}I^{1}
satisfies
G_{0}\subset G_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{1})\geq(1+\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0})
.

What remains to show is the energy estimate (
2.5
). Using the additivity of conical energies (
2.2
) and the fact that for every
I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
we have
I^{1}\subset 3I
, to prove (
2.5
) it suffices to show for every
I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}

\int_{E}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,3I,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\lesssim\int_{E}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,I\cap G_{0},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x).

(2.6)

This is achieved in
[
Dąb22b
, Proposition 4.1]
, and proving it occupies most of that paper. In the proof of (
2.6
) it is crucial to work with a generalized dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}(E,I)
consisting of (generalized) rectangles adapted to the interval
I
. As usual,
\mathcal{D}(E,I)=\bigcup_{k}\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,I)
where each
\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,I)
is a partition of
E
. Each rectangle
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,I)
has dimensions
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)2^{-k}\times 2^{-k}
, with the longer edge pointing in the directions corresponding to
I
. Since it may happen that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\ll 1
, one should think of these rectangles as very narrow tubes. For
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,I)
we set
\ell(Q)=2^{-k}
.

The reason we work with lattices
\mathcal{D}(E,I)
is that we want to create a multiscale decomposition associated with (
2.6
), of the form

\int_{E}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,3I,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\sim\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{D}(E,I)}\int_{Q}\frac{\mu(X(x,3I,\ell(Q),\ell(Q^{1})))}{\ell(Q)}\,d\mu(x).

In order to upper bound the right hand side above, it is crucial that for any choice of
x_{Q}\in Q
the family of double-truncated cones
\{X(x_{Q},3I,\ell(Q),\ell(Q^{1}))\}_{Q\in\mathcal{D}(E,I)}
has bounded intersections. This can only be achieved if we work with the rectangles described before, and not with the usual dyadic squares. In other words, the geometry of the problem forces us to use these rectangles. We discuss this further in Subsection
3.4
.

Going back to (
2.4
), the discussion above indicates how to deal with problem (a) assuming all
G(x)
coincide. The main achievement of this article is that we managed to overcome problem (b).

The key idea is the following. As before, our goal is to prove the propagation of good directions. For every
x\in E^{\prime}
we construct a new good set
G_{*}(x)
in a similar way as
G_{1}
before, but using
G(x)
instead of
G_{0}
. Thus, the new good set may vary from point to point. We do it in such a way that
G(x)\subset G_{*}(x)\subset J_{0}
for some fixed interval
J_{0}
, and for many
x\in E^{\prime}
we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{*}(x))\geq(1+\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))
. Finally, we have a counterpart of (
2.5
):

\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{*}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\lesssim\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x).

(2.7)

See Proposition
4.1
for the precise statement. With our main proposition at hand, an iteration procedure quickly yields Theorem
1.1
, and we describe this in Subsection
4.1
. The proof of Proposition
4.1
occupies Sections
5
–
12
. The definition of new good sets
G_{*}(x)
takes place in Section
5
, and the remainder of the paper is devoted to the proof of (
2.7
).

Since
G_{*}(x)
may differ from point to point, we are not able to reduce (
2.7
) to estimates over single intervals as in (
2.6
), and we are not able to fix a single lattice
\mathcal{D}(E,I)
to work with. Instead, we derive an estimate of the form

\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{*}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{T}}\int_{Q}\frac{\mu(X(x,J_{Q},\ell(Q),\ell(Q^{1})))}{\ell(Q)}\,d\mu(x).

(2.8)

Here
\mathcal{T}
is a tree-like structure consisting of cubes from many different lattices
\mathcal{D}(E,I)
. For every
Q\in\mathcal{T}
we denote by
J_{Q}
the interval of directions corresponding to
Q
, so that
Q\in\mathcal{D}(E,J_{Q})
. The tree structure is understood in the following way: if
Q,P\in\mathcal{T}
and
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(P)
, then either
Q\subset P
and
J_{Q}\subset J_{P}
, or
Q\cap P=\varnothing
, or
J_{Q}\cap J_{P}=\varnothing
. Equivalently, either
Q\times J_{Q}\subset P\times J_{P}
, or
Q\times J_{Q}\cap P\times J_{P}=\varnothing
. Thus,
\mathcal{T}
can be seen as a multiscale decomposition of
E\times[0,1]
, so that there is an additional layer of complexity: we simultaneously work with scales, locations, and directions.

We describe the lattices we work with in Section
6
, and we construct the tree
\mathcal{T}
in Section
7
. The rough idea is that if
x\in E^{\prime}
and
J
is one of the “good intervals for
x
at scale
k
”, then we should add the cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J)
containing
x
to
\mathcal{T}
. Of course, (
2.8
) is only useful if we can estimate the right hand side by the conical energies related to
G(x)
, and this complicates the actual construction. We prove various properties of
\mathcal{T}
in Section
8
, and we get the estimate (
2.8
) (formulated in the language of “bad cubes”) in Section
9
.

One key aspect of
\mathcal{T}
is that we have a decomposition

\mathcal{T}=\bigcup_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}}\mathcal{T}(R)

such that within each
\mathcal{T}(R)
all the cubes belong to a common lattice associated with
J_{R}
, i.e.
\mathcal{T}(R)\subset\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
. Moreover, we are able to prove a packing condition on the family
\mathsf{Roots}
:

\sum_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\mu(R)\lesssim\mu(E).

(2.9)

In our language, the cubes inside
\mathcal{T}

do not shatter too often
: a cube
Q
shatters if its
\mathcal{T}
-children correspond to strict sub-intervals of
J_{Q}
. The family
\mathsf{Roots}
consists precisely of cubes that resulted from shattering. Constructing
\mathcal{T}
in a way that prevents shattering from happening too often was another key difficulty that made the construction rather delicate.

Since each sub-tree
\mathcal{T}(R)
consists of cubes from a single lattice
\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
, we are in a situation resembling
[
Dąb22b
, Proposition 4.1]
. In Sections
10
–
12
we adapt the proof from
[
Dąb22b
]
to our setup. Eventually, we show that for every
R\in\mathsf{Roots}

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(R)}\int_{Q}\frac{\mu(X(x,J_{Q},\ell(Q),\ell(Q^{1})))}{\ell(Q)}\,d\mu(x)\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\mu(R)\cdot\mathcal{E},

where
\mathcal{E}\coloneqq\mu(E^{\prime})^{-1}\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}{\mu(X(x,G(x),r))}/r\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)
. Together with the packing condition (
2.9
) and the estimate (
2.8
), this gives the desired bound (
2.7
). This concludes the proof of Proposition
4.1
, and of Theorem
1.1
.

One important aspect of our proof which we ignored in the discussion above is the role played by some maximal function estimates on projected measures. In
[
Dąb22b
, Proposition 4.1]
it was crucial to have estimates on
\|\pi_{\theta}\mu\|_{L^{\infty}}
for certain directions
\theta
. Here, this is replaced by pointwise estimates on the Hardy-Littlewood maximal function of the projected measure

\mu_{\theta}(x)\coloneqq\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}\mu)(\pi_{\theta}(x)).

The importance of such estimates becomes clear in Lemmas
3.12
,
3.14
, and especially Lemma
3.15
.

3.
Preliminaries

3.1.
Notation

Throughout the article notation
f\lesssim g
stands for
f\leq Cg
for some constant
C>0
. If the constant
C
depends on a parameter
h
, we write
f\lesssim_{h}g
. The estimate
f\lesssim g\lesssim f
is denoted by
f\sim g
, and
f\sim_{h}g
stands for
f\lesssim_{h}g\lesssim_{h}f
. We often use letter
C
to denote absolute constants, and the precise value of
C
may change from line to line. We use
C
for large constants, and
c
for small constants. The absolute constants whose value we wish to preserve for future reference are usually assigned a subscript.

Let
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\simeq[0,1)
. Given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
we set

\displaystyle e_{\theta}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(\cos(2\pi\theta),\sin(2\pi\theta))\in\mathbb{S}^{1},

\displaystyle\ell_{x,\theta}

\displaystyle\coloneqq x+\spn(e_{\theta}),

\displaystyle\ell_{\theta}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ell_{0,\theta}.

We denote by
\pi_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
the orthogonal projection onto
\ell_{\theta}
, so that
\pi_{\theta}(x)=x\cdot e_{\theta}
.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and a measurable set
I\subset\mathbb{T}
we define the cone centered at
x
with directions in
I
as

X(x,I)=\bigcup_{\theta\in I}\ell_{x,\theta}.

Note that we do not require
I
to be an interval. We also set
\theta^{\perp}=\theta+1/4
,
I^{\perp}=I+1/4
,
\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}=\pi_{\theta^{\perp}}
, etc.

For
0<r<R
we define truncated cones as

\displaystyle X(x,I,r)=X(x,I)\cap B(x,r),

\displaystyle X(x,I,r,R)=X(x,I,R)\setminus B(x,r).

In case
I=[\theta-a,\theta+a]
, we have an algebraic characterization of
X(x,I)
:
y\in X(x,I)
if and only if

|\pi^{\perp}_{\theta}(y)-\pi^{\perp}_{\theta}(x)|\leq\sin(2\pi a)|x-y|.

(3.1)

We will denote by
\Delta
the usual family of half-open
triadic
intervals on
[0,1)\simeq\mathbb{T}
, so that

\Delta=\bigcup_{j\in 0}^{\infty}\Delta_{j}=\bigcup_{j\in 0}^{\infty}\{[k3^{-j},(k+1)3^{-j})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}k\in\{0,\dots,3^{j}-1\}\}

Using triadic intervals instead of dyadic intervals bears a minor technical convenience: every interval
I\in\Delta
has a triadic child with the same center as
I
. If
J\in\Delta
, then
\Delta(J)
denotes the collection of triadic intervals contained in
J
. For
I\in\Delta\setminus\{[0,1)\}
, the notation
I^{1}
will be used for the triadic parent of
I
. We also write
\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(I)
to denote the triadic children of
I
.

Given an interval
I\subset\mathbb{T}
and
C>0
, we will write
CI
to denote the interval with the same midpoint as
I
and length
C\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)
.

The closure of a set
A
will be denoted by
\overline{A}
, and its interior by
\mathrm{int}(A)
.

Suppose that
\mu
is a Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We denote by
\pi_{\theta}\mu
the measure on
\mathbb{R}
obtained as a push-forward of
\mu
under
\pi_{\theta}
.

Given a Radon measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}
we denote by
\mathcal{M}\nu
its Hardy-Littlewood maximal function:

\mathcal{M}\nu(t)=\sup_{r>0}\frac{\nu((t-r,t+r))}{2r}.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In the proof of Theorem
1.1
quantities of the form
\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}\mu)(\pi_{\theta}(x))
will play a crucial role. To simplify the notation we set

\displaystyle\mu_{\theta}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}\mu)(\pi_{\theta}(x)),

\displaystyle\mu_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}\mu)(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)).

3.2.
From big projections to bounded projections

Recall that
\mu_{\theta}(x)=\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}\mu)(\pi_{\theta}(x))
. In this subsection we show how to use lower bounds on
\Fav(E)
to find a large subset
E^{\prime}\subset E
such that for
x\in E^{\prime}
we have
\mu_{\theta}(x)\lesssim 1
for many
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

There exists an absolute constant
C\geq 1
such that the following holds. Let
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
, and suppose that
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-measurable with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)<\infty
. Set
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{E}
. If

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))>0

and

M\geq C\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))},

(3.2)

then for
E_{M}\coloneqq\{x\in E\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mu_{\theta}(x)\leq M\}
we have

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E_{M})\geq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))}{2}.

Proof.

By the weak-
(1,1)
estimate for the Hardy-Littlewood maximal function (see
[
Tol14
, Theorem 2.5]
) we have

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\{t\in\mathbb{R}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}\mu)(t)>M\})\leq C^{\prime}\frac{\|\mu\|}{M}=C^{\prime}\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)}{M}.

By (
3.2
) we get

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\{t\in\pi_{\theta}(E)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}\mu)(t)>M\})\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))}{2}

as soon as
C\geq 2C^{\prime}
. Thus,

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E_{M}))=\mathcal{H}^{1}(\{t\in\pi_{\theta}(E)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}\mu)(t)\leq M\})\\
\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))-\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))}{2}=\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))}{2}.

Since
\pi_{\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is
1
-Lipschitz, we have

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E_{M})\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E_{M}))\geq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))}{2}.

∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose that
\kappa>0
,
M=C\kappa^{-1},

G\subset\mathbb{T}
is measurable with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)>0
,
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-measurable with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)<\infty
, and for all
\theta\in G

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))>\kappa\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

Let
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{E}
,
E_{M,\theta}\coloneqq\{x\in E\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mu_{\theta}(x)\leq M\}
, and for
x\in E
let
G(x)\coloneqq\{\theta\in G\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}x\in E_{M,\theta}\}
. Then,

E^{\prime}\coloneqq\left\{x\in E\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ \mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\geq\frac{\kappa}{4}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)\right\}

satisfies

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E^{\prime})\geq\frac{\kappa}{4}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1
, for every
\theta\in G
we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E_{M,\theta})\geq\frac{\kappa}{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).
By Fubini’s theorem

\int_{E}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}(x)=\int_{G}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E_{M,\theta})\,d\theta\geq\frac{\kappa}{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

At the same time,

\int_{E}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}(x)=\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}(x)+\int_{E\setminus E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}(x)\\
\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)\mathcal{H}^{1}(E^{\prime})+\frac{\kappa}{4}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\setminus E^{\prime})\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)\mathcal{H}^{1}(E^{\prime})+\frac{\kappa}{4}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

Comparing the two inequalities we get

\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)\mathcal{H}^{1}(E^{\prime})\geq\frac{\kappa}{4}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

∎

3.3.
From bounded projections to conical energies

In
[
CT20
, Corollary 3.3]
Chang and Tolsa related conical energies
†
†

†

The conical energies in
[
CT20
]
are defined using a different formula, but it is equivalent to our definition, see
[
Dąb22a
, Remark 1.22]
.
and
L^{2}
-norms of projections by proving

\iint_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu(X(x,I^{\perp},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\lesssim\int_{I}\|\pi_{\theta}\mu\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,d\theta.

(3.3)

Their proof uses Fourier analysis and it can be adapted to give the following more general bound.

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose that
\mu
and
\nu
are compactly supported Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For any open
I\subset\mathbb{T}
we have

\iint_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu(X(x,I^{\perp},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\nu(x)\lesssim\int_{I}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\pi_{\theta}\mu(t)\cdot\pi_{\theta}\nu(t)\,dt\,d\theta.

The proof is virtually identical to that of
[
CT20
, Corollary 3.3]
, one just needs to replace one “copy” of
\mu
by
\nu
in all inequalities.

Corollary 3.4
.

Suppose that
\mu
is a compactly supported Borel measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
I\subset\mathbb{T}
be open, and suppose that

\int_{I}\|\pi_{\theta}\mu\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,d\theta<\infty.

(3.4)

Then, for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\supp\mu

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu(X(x,I^{\perp},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\int_{I}\pi_{\theta}\mu(\pi_{\theta}(x))\,d\theta.

(3.5)

Proof.

Denote the quantity on the left hand side of (
3.5
) by
f(x)
, and the quantity from the right hand side by
g(x)
. Note that both
f
and
g
are
\mu
-integrable by (
3.4
) and (
3.3
).

Let
x\in\supp\mu
and
\varepsilon>0
. Applying Lemma
3.3
to
\mu
and
\nu_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\mu|_{B(x,\varepsilon)}/\mu(B(x,\varepsilon))
we get

\frac{1}{\mu(B(x,\varepsilon))}\int_{B(x,\varepsilon)}f(y)\,d\mu(y)\lesssim\int_{I}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\pi_{\theta}\mu\cdot\pi_{\theta}\nu_{\varepsilon}\,dt\,d\theta\\
=\int_{I}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\pi_{\theta}\mu(\pi_{\theta}(y))\,d\nu_{\varepsilon}(y)\,d\theta=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{I}\pi_{\theta}\mu(\pi_{\theta}(y))\,d\theta\,d\nu_{\varepsilon}(y)\\
=\frac{1}{\mu(B(x,\varepsilon))}\int_{B(x,\varepsilon)}g(y)\,d\mu(y).

By the Lebesgue differentiation theorem applied to
f
and
g
, letting
\varepsilon\to 0
implies (
3.5
) for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\supp\mu
.
∎

In Appendix
A
we show that in the proof of Theorem
1.1
we may restrict attention to sets
E
which are finite unions of parallel segments. The following lemma will be the starting point for an iteration procedure described in Section
4
.

Recall that
\mu_{\theta}(x)
stands for
\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}\mu)(\pi_{\theta}(x))
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose that
\kappa>0
,
M=C\kappa^{-1},

J_{0}\in\Delta(\mathbb{T})
,
G\subset J_{0}
is measurable with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)>0
,
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a finite union of parallel segments,
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{E}
, and for all
\theta\in G

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))>\kappa\mu(E).

(3.6)

Then, there exists a measurable set
E^{\prime}\subset E
such that

(1)

\mu(E^{\prime})\geq\tfrac{\kappa}{4}\mu(E)
and

(2)

for every
x\in E^{\prime}
there exists a finite family
\mathcal{G}(x)
of disjoint triadic sub-intervals of
J_{0}
with
G(x)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}(x)}I
satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\geq\tfrac{\kappa}{5}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)
,

(3)

for every
x\in E^{\prime}
and
I\in\mathcal{G}(x)
there exists
\theta_{I}\in I
such that
\mu_{\theta_{I}}(x)\leq M

(4)

for every
x\in E^{\prime}

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu(X(x,G^{\perp}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim M\mathcal{H}^{1}(G).

Proof.

We apply Lemma
3.2
to obtain
E^{\prime}\subset E
with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E^{\prime})\geq\tfrac{\kappa}{4}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
and such that for every
x\in E^{\prime}
we have a set
G^{\prime}(x)\subset G
satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G^{\prime}(x))\geq\tfrac{\kappa}{4}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)
and

\mu_{\theta}(x)\leq M\quad\text{for all $\theta\in G^{\prime}(x)$}.

(3.7)

Suppose that
E
is a union of
N
parallel segments
J_{1},\dots,J_{N}
. Without loss of generality we may assume these segments are pairwise disjoint. Let
\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
be such that all the segments are perpendicular to
\ell_{\theta_{0}}
. Clearly,
\pi_{\theta_{0}}(E)=0
, and if
0<\varepsilon<1
and
|\theta-\theta_{0}|\leq\varepsilon
then

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(J_{i}))\lesssim\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{i})=\varepsilon\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

In particular, our big projections assumption (
3.6
) implies that

G\cap(\theta_{0}-c\kappa,\theta_{0}+c\kappa)=\varnothing

(3.8)

for some small absolute
c>0
.

Fix
x\in E^{\prime}
. By the outer regularity of Lebesgue measure, for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists an open set
G^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x)\supset G^{\prime}(x)
with

\mathcal{H}^{1}(G^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x)\setminus G^{\prime}(x))\leq\varepsilon

(3.9)

We will choose some small
\varepsilon
below. Recall that any open subset of
\mathbb{R}
can be written as a union of a countable family of disjoint triadic intervals, and let
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)
be such family for
G^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x)
. Without loss of generality we assume that every
I\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x)
intersects
G^{\prime}(x)
. By (
3.8
) we can also assume that

G^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x)\cap(\theta_{0}-c\kappa,\theta_{0}+c\kappa)=\varnothing.

(3.10)

Moreover, since
G^{\prime}(x)\subset G\subset J_{0}\in\Delta(\mathbb{T})
, we can assume that all
I\in\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)
are contained in
J_{0}
.

Since

\mathcal{H}^{1}(G^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(x))\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(G^{\prime}(x))\geq\tfrac{\kappa}{4}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G),

we may choose a finite subset
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}(x)\subset\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)
such that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{\varepsilon}(x))\geq\tfrac{\kappa}{5}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G),
where
G_{\varepsilon}(x)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}(x)}I
.

Observe that for any
x\in E
and
\theta\in J_{0}\setminus(\theta_{0}-c\kappa,\theta_{0}+c\kappa)
we have

\pi_{\theta}\mu(\pi_{\theta}(x))\lesssim N\kappa^{-1}.

(3.11)

This follows from the fact that
E
is a union of
N
parallel segments, and the angle they make with the line
\pi_{\theta}^{-1}(\pi_{\theta}(x))
is
\gtrsim\kappa
.

Let
I\in\Delta(J_{0})
be such that
I\in\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}(x)
for some
x\in E^{\prime}
. By (
3.10
) we have
I\cap(\theta_{0}-c\kappa,\theta_{0}+c\kappa)=\varnothing
. Thus, by (
3.11
)

\int_{I}\|\pi_{\theta}\mu\|_{L^{2}}\,d\theta=\int_{I}\int_{E}\pi_{\theta}\mu(\pi_{\theta}(x))\,d\mu(x)d\theta\lesssim N\kappa^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\mu(E)<\infty.

Consequently, we may apply Corollary
3.4
to conclude that for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in E

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu(X(x,I^{\perp},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\int_{I}\pi_{\theta}\mu(\pi_{\theta}(x))\,d\theta.

(3.12)

Denote by
F_{I}\subset E
the set of points where the inequality above fails, so that
\mu(F_{I})=0
. Let

F=\bigcup\{F_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}I\in\Delta(J_{0})\text{ such that }I\in\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}(x)\text{ for some }x\in E^{\prime}\}.

Since the union above is at most countable, we get that
\mu(F)=0
. At the same time, for
x\in E^{\prime}\setminus F
the estimate (
3.12
) holds for all
I\in\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}(x)
. Thus,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{\varepsilon}(x)^{\perp},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}(x)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu(X(x,I^{\perp},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}(x)}\int_{I}\pi_{\theta}\mu(\pi_{\theta}(x))\,d\theta=\int_{G_{\varepsilon}(x)}\pi_{\theta}\mu(\pi_{\theta}(x))\,d\theta

\displaystyle=\int_{G_{\varepsilon}(x)\cap G^{\prime}(x)}\pi_{\theta}\mu(\pi_{\theta}(x))\,d\theta+\int_{G_{\varepsilon}(x)\setminus G^{\prime}(x)}\pi_{\theta}\mu(\pi_{\theta}(x))\,d\theta

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:Linfbd},\eqref{eq:proj-meas-bdd}}{\lesssim}\int_{G_{\varepsilon}(x)\cap G^{\prime}(x)}M\,d\theta+\int_{G_{\varepsilon}(x)\setminus G^{\prime}(x)}N\kappa^{-1}\,d\theta\overset{\eqref{eq:Gvesmallmeas}}{\leq}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)+\varepsilon N\kappa^{-1}.

We choose
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(N,\kappa,\mathcal{H}^{1}(G))
so small that the last term above is dominated by
M\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)
. For
x\in E^{\prime}\setminus F
we set
\mathcal{G}(x)\coloneqq\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}(x)
. Since
\mu(F)=0
, we may redefine
E^{\prime}
by removing
F
, and we don’t lose the measure bound
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E^{\prime})\geq\tfrac{\kappa}{4}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
.

The proof is almost finished. It is clear that
E^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}(x)
have the desired properties (1), (2), and (4). The remaining property (3) is easy to establish: for every
I\in\mathcal{G}(x)
we want to find
\theta_{I}\in I
such that

\mu_{\theta_{I}}(x)\leq M

(3.13)

Recall that
\mathcal{G}(x)\subset\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)
, and we assumed that every
I\in\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)
has non-empty intersection with
G^{\prime}(x)
. We pick any
\theta_{I}\in I\cap G^{\prime}(x)
and use (
3.7
) to get (
3.13
).
∎

3.4.
A family of metrics

Given an interval
I=(\theta-a,\theta+a)\subset\mathbb{T}
set

\pi_{I}\coloneqq\pi_{\theta}.

We define the metric

\displaystyle d_{I}(x,y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)^{-2}|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(x)-\pi_{I}^{\perp}(y)|^{2}+|\pi_{I}(x)-\pi_{I}(y)|^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\sim\max\left(\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)^{-1}|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(x)-\pi_{I}^{\perp}(y)|,|\pi_{I}(x)-\pi_{I}(y)|\right).

Remark 3.6
.

Observe that
(\mathbb{R}^{2},d_{I})
is isometric to
(\mathbb{R}^{2},d_{euc})
, where
d_{euc}
denotes the usual Euclidean distance. Indeed, let
R_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the rotation such that
\pi_{I}(R_{I}(x_{1},x_{2}))=x_{2}
and
\pi_{I}^{\perp}(R_{I}(x_{1},x_{2}))=x_{1}
, and let
S_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the linear map defined by

S_{I}(x_{1},x_{2})=(\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)x_{1},x_{2}).

Then,
d_{I}(S_{I}\circ R_{I}(x),S_{I}\circ R_{I}(y))=|x-y|
, so that
S_{I}\circ R_{I}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},d_{euc})\to(\mathbb{R}^{2},d_{I})
is an isometry.

Remark 3.7
.

It is easy to check that if
I,J\subset\mathbb{T}
are two intervals satisfying
I\subset CJ
and
J\subset CI
, then the metrics
d_{I}
and
d_{J}
are comparable, in the sense that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have

C^{-1}d_{J}(x,y)\lesssim d_{I}(x,y)\lesssim Cd_{J}(x,y).

In other words, nearby intervals of comparable length give rise to comparable metrics.

Throughout the article we will use the notation

B_{I}(x,r)\coloneqq B_{d_{I}}(x,r)=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}d_{I}(x,y)<r\}.

Observe that
B_{I}(x,r)
is essentially a tube of dimensions
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r\times r
, pointing in the direction of
I
. We will also write
\diam_{I}(A)
to denote diameter of
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with respect to
d_{I}
, and
\dist_{I}(x,A)
to denote the distance from
x
to
A
with respect to
d_{I}
.

The reason we define
d_{I}
as above is the relationship between
d_{I}
-balls and the cones associated to
I
, which is explored in the following three lemmas. We will use them repeatedly throughout the article, often without mentioning it explicitly.

Lemma 3.8
.

If
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
I\subset\mathbb{T}
is an interval, and
\alpha\geq 1
, then

X(x,\alpha I,r)\subset B_{I}(x,C\alpha r).

Proof.

Let
y\in X(x,\alpha I,r)
. Since
|x-y|<r
, to show
y\in B_{I}(x,C\alpha r)
we only need to prove
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)^{-1}|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(x)-\pi_{I}^{\perp}(y)|\lesssim\alpha r
. But this follows from (
3.1
):

|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(y)-\pi_{I}^{\perp}(x)|\leq\sin(\pi\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I))|x-y|\leq\pi\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r.

∎

Lemma 3.9
.

For every
\alpha>1
there exists
c=c(\alpha)\in(0,1)
such that if
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
I\subset\mathbb{T}
is an interval, and
y\in X(x,I,r,2r)
, then

B_{I}(y,cr)\subset X(x,\alpha I,r/2,4r).

Remark 3.10
.

In this paper we will only use the following corollary: for
x
and
y
as above we have

B(y,c\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r)\subset X(x,\alpha I,r/2,4r).

(3.14)

This follows from
B(y,c\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r)\subset B_{I}(y,cr)
.

Proof.

Assume that
y\in X(x,I,r,2r)
and
z\in B_{I}(y,cr)
for some small
0<c<1/2
to be chosen below. Note that

\frac{r}{2}\leq|x-y|-|y-z|\leq|x-z|\leq|x-y|+|y-z|\leq 4r.

It remains to prove that
z\in X(x,\alpha I)
. Using the triangle inequality and (
3.1
) we get

|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(z-x)|\leq|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(z-y)|+|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(y-x)|\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)d_{I}(z,y)+\sin(\pi\mathcal{H}^{1}(I))|y-x|\\
\leq c\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r+\sin(\pi\mathcal{H}^{1}(I))(|x-z|+cr)\\
\leq 2c\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)|x-z|+(1+2c)\sin(\pi\mathcal{H}^{1}(I))|x-z|\\
\leq\sin(\pi\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I))|x-z|

if we choose
c=c(\alpha)
small enough. By (
3.1
), this implies
z\in X(x,\alpha I)
and finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.11
.

For every
\alpha>1
there exists
C=C(\alpha)>4
such that if
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
I\subset\mathbb{T}
is an interval, and
R>r>Cd_{I}(x,y)
, then

X(x,I,r,R)\subset X(y,\alpha I,r/2,2R).

Proof.

Assume that
z\in X(x,I,r,R)
. Let’s show first that
\tfrac{1}{2}r\leq|z-y|\leq 2R
. Since
R>r>Cd_{I}(x,y)\geq C|x-y|
,

|z-y|\leq|z-x|+|y-x|\leq R+C^{-1}R

and

|z-y|\geq|z-x|-|y-x|\geq r-C^{-1}r,

and so the desired inequalities hold as soon as
C\geq 2
.

Now we show that
z\in X(y,\alpha I)
. Observe that

|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(z)-\pi^{\perp}_{I}(y)|\leq|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(z)-\pi^{\perp}_{I}(x)|+|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(x)-\pi^{\perp}_{I}(y)|\\
\leq\sin(\pi\mathcal{H}^{1}(I))|z-x|+C^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r,

where in the last estimate we used (
3.1
) and the fact that
|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(x)-\pi^{\perp}_{I}(y)|\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)d_{I}(x,y)\leq C^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r
.

Since
|z-x|\leq|z-y|+|y-x|\leq|z-y|+C^{-1}r
, we get

\sin(\pi\mathcal{H}^{1}(I))|z-x|+C^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r\leq\sin(\pi\mathcal{H}^{1}(I))|z-y|+5C^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r.

Recalling that
|z-y|\geq r/2
, we arrive at

\sin(\pi\mathcal{H}^{1}(I))|z-y|+5C^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r\leq\\
\sin(\pi\mathcal{H}^{1}(I))|z-y|+10C^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)|z-y|\leq\sin(\pi\mathcal{H}^{1}(\alpha I))|z-y|

assuming
C\gg(\alpha-1)^{-1}
is large enough. Together with our previous estimates this gives

|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(z)-\pi^{\perp}_{I}(y)|\leq\sin(\pi\mathcal{H}^{1}(\alpha I))|z-y|,

which is equivalent to
z\in X(y,\alpha I)
by (
3.1
).
∎

In the following lemma we show how the maximal function estimates obtained in Subsection
3.2
can be used to estimate the measure of
d_{I}
-balls. Recall that
\mu_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)=\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}\mu)(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(x))
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Suppose that
\alpha\geq 1
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
I\subset\mathbb{T}
is an interval, and
\mu_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)\leq M
for some
\theta\in\alpha I
. Then, for any
r>0

\mu(B_{I}(x,r))\lesssim\alpha M\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r.

Proof.

Let
\theta_{I}
be the midpoint of
I
. Observe that

|(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)-\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(y))-(\pi_{I}^{\perp}(x)-\pi_{I}^{\perp}(y))|=|(e_{\theta}^{\perp}-e_{\theta_{I}}^{\perp})\cdot(x-y)|\\
\lesssim|\theta-\theta_{I}||x-y|\lesssim\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)|x-y|.

It follows that for
y\in B_{I}(x,r)

|\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)-\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(y)|\leq|\pi_{I}^{\perp}(x)-\pi_{I}^{\perp}(y)|+C\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)|x-y|\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r+C\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r,

and so

B_{I}(x,r)\subset T\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}|\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)-\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(z)|\leq C^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r\}.

Setting
t=\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)
and using the maximal function bound we get

\mu(T)\leq(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}\mu)([t-C^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r,t+C^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r])\\
\leq\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}\mu)(t)\cdot 2C^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r=\mu_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)\cdot 2C^{\prime}\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r\lesssim M\alpha\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r.

∎

3.5.
Conical energy estimates

In this subsection we prove several estimates involving cones and conical energies.

Lemma 3.13
.

Suppose that
\mu
is a Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
G\subset\mathbb{T}
is measurable. Then, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
0<\rho\leq 1/2
we have

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\sim\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}.

(3.15)

Moreover, for any integers
j\geq l\geq 0

\sum_{k=l+1}^{j-1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}\lesssim_{\rho}\int_{\rho^{j}}^{\rho^{l}}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\\
\lesssim_{\rho}\sum_{k=l}^{j}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}.

(3.16)

Proof.

In (
3.15
) the “
\leq
” bound is immediate because
X(x,G,\rho r,r)\subset X(x,G,r)
. To see the converse, we estimate

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}=\sum_{l\geq 0}\int_{\rho^{l+1}}^{\rho^{l}}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho^{k+1}r,\rho^{k}r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\\
=\sum_{l\geq 0}\sum_{k\geq 0}\rho^{k}\int_{\rho^{l+1}}^{\rho^{l}}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho^{k+1}r,\rho^{k}r))}{\rho^{k}r}\frac{dr}{r}\\
=\sum_{l\geq 0}\sum_{k\geq 0}\rho^{k}\int_{\rho^{k+l+1}}^{\rho^{k+l}}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho s,s))}{s}\frac{ds}{s}\\
=\sum_{k\geq 0}\rho^{k}\int_{0}^{\rho^{k}}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho s,s))}{s}\frac{ds}{s}\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}\rho^{k}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho s,s))}{s}\frac{ds}{s}\\
\leq 2\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho s,s))}{s}\frac{ds}{s},

where in the last line we used the fact that
0<\rho\leq 1/2
.

Now we move on to (
3.16
). Regarding the second inequality,

\int_{\rho^{j}}^{\rho^{l}}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}=\sum_{k=l}^{j-1}\int_{\rho^{k+1}}^{\rho^{k}}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\\
\leq\sum_{k=l}^{j-1}\int_{\rho^{k+1}}^{\rho^{k}}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho^{k+2},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k+1}}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim_{\rho}\sum_{k=l}^{j-1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho^{k+2},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k+1}}\\
=\sum_{k=l}^{j-1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k+1}}+\sum_{k=l}^{j-1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho^{k+2},\rho^{k+1}))}{\rho^{k+1}}\\
\lesssim_{\rho}\sum_{k=l}^{j}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}.

Conversely, setting
\delta=\rho^{1/2}

\int_{\rho^{j}}^{\rho^{l}}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}=\sum_{k=2l}^{2j-1}\int_{\delta^{k+1}}^{\delta^{k}}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\delta^{2}r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\\
\geq\sum_{k=2l}^{2j-1}\int_{\delta^{k+1}}^{\delta^{k}}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\delta^{k+2},\delta^{k+1}))}{\delta^{k}}\frac{dr}{r}\sim_{\rho}\sum_{k=2l}^{2j-1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\delta^{k+2},\delta^{k+1}))}{\delta^{k}}\\
\gtrsim_{\rho}\sum_{k=l+1}^{j-1}\left(\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\delta^{2k+2},\delta^{2k+1}))}{\delta^{2k}}+\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\delta^{2k+1},\delta^{2k}))}{\delta^{2k-1}}\right)\\
\sim_{\rho}\sum_{k=l+1}^{j-1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}.

∎

In the following two lemmas we show how the maximal function estimates for projections can be used to estimate measure of cones. Recall that
\mu_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)=\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}\mu)(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(x))
.

Lemma 3.14
.

Suppose that
\alpha\geq 1
,
\mu
is a Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
\mathcal{I}
is a family of disjoint intervals in
\mathbb{T}
. Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
r>0
, and assume that for every
I\in\mathcal{I}
there exists
\theta_{I}\in\alpha I
and
y_{I}\in B_{I}(x,\alpha r)
with
\mu_{\theta_{I}}^{\perp}(y_{I})\leq M
. Then, setting
H=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I
we have

\mu(X(x,H,r))\lesssim\alpha^{2}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(H)r.

Proof.

For every
I\in\mathcal{I}
we have

X(x,I,r)\subset B_{I}(x,Cr)\subset B_{I}(y_{I},(C+\alpha)r),

and so by Lemma
3.12

\mu(X(x,I,r))\lesssim\alpha^{2}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)r.

Summing over
I\in\mathcal{I}
finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.15
.

There exists a small absolute constant
c_{0}>0
such that the following holds. Let
\alpha\geq 1
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\delta\in[0,1/2)
, and suppose that
J\subset\mathbb{T}
is an interval. Assume that

(a)

E
is Ahlfors regular with constant
A
, and
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{E}
,

(b)

\mathcal{I}
is a family of disjoint intervals contained in
J
such that for every
I\in\mathcal{I}
there exists
\theta_{I}\in\alpha I
and
y_{I}\in B_{I}(x,\alpha\delta)
‡
‡

‡

If
\delta=0
this means
y_{I}=x
.
with
\mu_{\theta_{I}}^{\perp}(y_{I})\leq M
,

(c)

H\coloneqq\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I
satisfies

\mathcal{H}^{1}(H)\leq\varepsilon\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)

(3.17)

with
\varepsilon\leq c_{0}\alpha^{-2}M^{-1}A^{-1}
.

Then, for any
r\geq\delta

\mu(X(x,0.9J,r,2r))\lesssim\mu(X(x,J\setminus H,r/2,4r)).

(3.18)

Proof.

Without loss of generality assume that
\delta>0
(the case
\delta=0
follows easily).

Observe that

\mu(X(x,0.9J,r,2r))=\mu(X(x,0.9J\cap H,r,2r))+\mu(X(x,0.9J\setminus H,r,2r))\\
\leq\mu(X(x,H,2r))+\mu(X(x,J\setminus H,r/2,4r)),

and so to get (
3.18
) it suffices to show
\mu(X(x,H,2r))\lesssim\mu(X(x,J\setminus H,r/2,4r))
.

If
X(x,0.9J,r,2r)\cap E=\varnothing
the estimate (
3.18
) is trivial, so suppose there exists
z\in X(x,0.9J,r,2r)\cap E
. Let
B_{z}\coloneqq B(z,c\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r)
for some small absolute constant
0<c<1
. If
c
is small enough, then

B_{z}\subset X(x,J,r/2,4r).

By Ahlfors regularity of
E
we get

\mu(X(x,J,r/2,4r))\geq\mu(B_{z})\geq cA^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r.

(3.19)

At the same time, since
r\geq\delta
we may use Lemma
3.14
to get

\mu(X(x,H,4r))\lesssim\alpha^{2}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(H)r\overset{\eqref{eq:smallH}}{\leq}c_{0}A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r.

(3.20)

Assuming
c_{0}
is small enough, we get from (
3.19
) and (
3.20
) that

\mu(X(x,H,4r))\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mu(X(x,J,r/2,4r))\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mu(X(x,H,4r))+\tfrac{1}{2}\mu(X(x,J\setminus H,r/2,4r)),

which implies the desired estimate

\mu(X(x,H,4r))\leq\mu(X(x,J\setminus H,r/2,4r)).

∎

In the lemma below we show that estimates for conical energies associated to
I
propagate to points that are close with respect to
d_{I}
, up to an appropriate truncation.

Lemma 3.16
.

Assume that
\rho\in(0,1)
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\mu
is a Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
I\subset\mathbb{T}
is an interval. Then

\int_{Cd_{I}(x,y)}^{1/2}\frac{\mu(X(y,1.5I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\int_{Cd_{I}(x,y)/2}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,2I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r},

(3.21)

where
C=C(\rho)>4
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.11
we have

X(y,1.5I,\rho r,r)\subset X(x,2I,\rho r/2,2r)

as soon as
\rho r\geq C^{\prime}d_{I}(x,y)
, where
C^{\prime}\geq 4
is absolute. Thus, taking
C=C^{\prime}/\rho

\int_{Cd_{I}(x,y)}^{1/2}\frac{\mu(X(y,1.5I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\int_{Cd_{I}(x,y)}^{1/2}\frac{\mu(X(x,2I,\rho r/2,2r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\\
\lesssim\int_{Cd_{I}(x,y)/2}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,2I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}.

∎

3.6.
Bad scales

Suppose that
\rho\in(0,1)
and
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
are fixed. Given an interval
J\subset\mathbb{T}
and
x\in E
we define the set of
bad scales
as

\mathsf{Bad}(x,J)=\{k\geq 0\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X(x,J,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k})\cap E\neq\varnothing\}.

Given a subset
F\subset E
we define the restricted set of bad scales as

\mathsf{Bad}_{F}(x,J)=\{k\geq 0\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}X(x,J,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k})\cap F\neq\varnothing\}.

Additionally, if
0\leq l\leq j
are integers we set

\mathsf{Bad}(x,J,l,j)=\{l\leq k\leq j\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}k\in\mathsf{Bad}(x,J)\}.

Observe that a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\diam(E)\leq 1
is a Lipschitz graph with respect to
J
if and only if

\mathsf{Bad}(x,J)=\varnothing\quad\text{for all $x\in E$,}

see Lemma 15.13 in
[
Mat95
]
.

The following theorem is a variant of the main proposition from
[
MO18
]
. We state only the planar case.

Theorem 3.17

(
[
MO18
, Proposition 1.12]
)
.

Assume that
\rho=1/2
,
E\subset B(0,1)
is an Ahlfors regular set with constant
A
,
\diam(E)=1
, and
F\subset E
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-measurable with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)\geq\tau
. Suppose that
J\subset\mathbb{T}
is an interval with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\leq c_{J}
for some absolute
0<c_{J}<1
, and that for every
x\in F
we have

\#\mathsf{Bad}_{F}(x,J)\leq M_{0}.

Then, there exists a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
with
\lip(\Gamma)\sim 2^{M_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)^{-1}
and

\mathcal{H}^{1}(F\cap\Gamma)\geq(c\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)A^{-2}\tau)^{2^{M_{0}}},

(3.22)

where
0<c<1
is absolute.

Remark 3.18
.

The statement above is slightly different than
[
MO18
, Proposition 1.12]
. Firstly, in
[
MO18
]
the authors do not explicitly keep track of parameter dependence, and so instead of (
3.22
) they only state
\mathcal{H}^{1}(F\cap\Gamma)\gtrsim_{\tau,M_{0},A,\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)}1
. We describe in Appendix
B
how to get the explicit estimate we claim above.

We also added the assumption
\diam(E)=1
, which after a tiny tweak to the proof in
[
MO18
]
yields a much better estimate in (
3.22
) than without it. Finally, in
[
MO18
]
one obtains a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
with
\lip(\Gamma)\sim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)^{-1}
, which is better than what we claim above. We could have done the same, but this would come at a price of an extra exponential loss in (
3.22
), and for our purposes the statement above is better. See also Remark
B.4
.

In the following lemma we relate the conical energies with the number of bad scales. This plays a similar role as Lemma 1.9 in
[
MO18
]
.

Recall that
\mu_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)=\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}\mu)(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(x))
.

Lemma 3.19
.

Fix
\alpha>1
and
\rho\in(0,1)
. Assume that
E
is Ahlfors regular with constant
A
, and
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{E}
. Suppose
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
J\subset\mathbb{T}
is an interval, and
0\leq l\leq j
are integers.
Then,

\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,J,l,j)\lesssim_{\alpha,\rho}A\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)^{-1}\int_{\rho^{j}}^{\rho^{l}}\frac{\mu(X(x,\alpha J,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}+1.

(3.23)

Assume additionally that there exist
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
d_{J}(x,y)\leq\alpha\rho^{j}
and
\theta\in\alpha J
such that
\mu_{\theta}^{\perp}(y)\leq M
. Then,

\int_{\rho^{j}}^{\rho^{l}}\frac{\mu(X(x,J,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim_{\alpha,\rho}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\cdot\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,J,l,j).

(3.24)

Proof.

We start by proving (
3.23
).
Fix
k\in\mathsf{Bad}(x,J,l,j)
, and let
x_{k}\in X(x,J,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k})\cap E
. By (
3.14
) if
0<c=c(\alpha,\rho)<1
is small enough, then the ball
B_{k}\coloneqq B(x_{k},c\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\rho^{k})
satisfies

B_{k}\subset X(x,\alpha J,\rho^{k+2},\rho^{k-1}).

By Ahlfors regularity of
\mu
this gives

\mu(X(x,\alpha J,\rho^{k+2},\rho^{k-1}))\geq\mu(B_{k})\gtrsim_{\alpha,\rho}A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\rho^{k}.

Let
S\coloneqq\mathsf{Bad}(x,J,l,j)\setminus\{l,l+1,j-1,j\}
. The inequality above gives

\#S\cdot A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\lesssim_{\alpha,\rho}\sum_{k\in S}\frac{\mu(X(x,\alpha J,\rho^{k+2},\rho^{k-1}))}{\rho^{k}}\\
=\sum_{k\in S}\frac{\mu(X(x,\alpha J,\rho^{k+2},\rho^{k+1}))}{\rho^{k}}+\frac{\mu(X(x,\alpha J,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}+\frac{\mu(X(x,\alpha J,\rho^{k},\rho^{k-1}))}{\rho^{k}}\\
\lesssim_{\rho}\sum_{k=l+1}^{j-1}\frac{\mu(X(x,\alpha J,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}.

Rearranging yields

\#S\lesssim_{\alpha,\rho}A\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)^{-1}\sum_{k=l+1}^{j-1}\frac{\mu(X(x,\alpha J,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}.

By (
3.16
) we have

\sum_{k=l+1}^{j-1}\frac{\mu(X(x,\alpha J,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}\lesssim_{\rho}\int_{\rho^{j}}^{\rho^{l}}\frac{\mu(X(x,\alpha J,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}

(3.25)

Together with the previous estimate and the fact that
\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,J,l,j)\leq\#S+4
, this gives (
3.23
).

We move on to (
3.24
). By (
3.16
) we have

\int_{\rho^{j}}^{\rho^{l}}\frac{\mu(X(x,J,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\sum_{k=l}^{j}\frac{\mu(X(x,J,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}=\sum_{k\in\mathsf{Bad}(x,J,l,j)}\frac{\mu(X(x,J,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}.

Observe that for any
l\leq k\leq j
we have

\mu(X(x,J,\rho^{k}))\leq\mu(B_{J}(x,C\rho^{k}))\leq\mu(B_{J}(y,C(\alpha)\rho^{k})\lesssim_{\alpha}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\rho^{k},

where in the second inequality we used the fact that
d_{J}(x,y)\leq\alpha\rho^{j}\lesssim_{\alpha}\rho^{k}
, and in the last inequality we used Lemma
3.12
. Together with the previous estimate this implies

\int_{\rho^{j}}^{\rho^{l}}\frac{\mu(X(x,J,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim_{\alpha}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\cdot\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,J,l,j).

This finishes the proof of (
3.24
).
∎

4.
Main proposition

In this section we state our main proposition, which asserts that “good directions propagate”. Then we use it to prove Theorem
1.1
.

Recall that
\mu_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)=\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}\mu)(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(x))
.

Proposition 4.1

(Main Proposition)
.

Let
0<\delta,\tau<1
and
1\leq A,M<\infty
. There exist absolute constants
0<c_{\varepsilon}<1
and
C>0
such that if
\varepsilon=c_{\varepsilon}A^{-1}M^{-1}
and
C_{\mathsf{Prop}}=C\tau^{-1}\delta^{-1}(AM)^{C}
, then the following holds. Assume that:

(a)

E\subset[0,1]^{2}
is an Ahlfors regular set with constant
A
, and
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{E}
,

(b)

J_{0}\in\Delta(\mathbb{T})
is a triadic interval with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\leq c_{J}A^{-1}M^{-1}
, where
c_{J}>0
is a small absolute constant,

(c)

E^{\prime}\subset E
satisfies
\mu(E^{\prime})\geq\delta\mu(E)
,

(d)

for every
x\in E^{\prime}
there exists a finite family
\mathcal{G}(x)\subset\Delta(J_{0})
of disjoint triadic sub-intervals of
J_{0}
, with
G(x)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}(x)}I
satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\geq\tau\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})
,

(e)

for every
I\in\mathcal{G}(x)
there exists
\theta_{I}\in I
such that
\mu_{\theta_{I}}^{\perp}(x)\leq M
.

Then, for every
x\in E^{\prime}
we may find a finite family
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)\subset\Delta(J_{0})
of disjoint triadic sub-intervals of
J_{0}
with the following properties:

(1)

every
I\in\mathcal{G}(x)
is contained in some
I_{*}\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
, and every
I_{*}\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
contains some
I\in\mathcal{G}(x)
,

(2)

denoting
G_{*}(x)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)}I
, either the set
E_{\mathsf{Fin}}\coloneqq\{x\in E^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}G_{*}(x)=J_{0}\}
is large:

\mu(E_{\mathsf{Fin}})\geq\frac{\mu(E^{\prime})}{4},

or the average length of
G_{*}(x)
is significantly larger than the length of
G(x)
:

\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{*}(x))\,d\mu(x)\geq\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\,d\mu(x)+c\tau\varepsilon\mu(E^{\prime})\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0}),

(4.1)

where
0<c<1
is absolute,

(3)

we control the conical energies related to
G_{*}(x)
:

\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\diam(E)}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{*}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\\
\leq C_{\mathsf{Prop}}\bigg(\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\diam(E)}\frac{\mu(X(x,G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\bigg).

(4.2)

We prove Proposition
4.1
in Sections
5
–
12
. Now we show how to use it to obtain Theorem
1.1
.

4.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

First, observe that Proposition
4.1
is made for iteration: by assertion (1) the families
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
we obtain satisfy assumptions (d) and (e), just like the original families
\mathcal{G}(x)
. We get the following corollary.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
0<\delta,\tau<1
and
1\leq A,M<\infty
. Assume that
E^{\prime}\subset E\subset[0,1]^{2}
,
J_{0}\in\Delta(\mathbb{T})
and families
\mathcal{G}(x)\subset\Delta(J_{0})
are as in Proposition
4.1
, so that they satisfy assumptions (a)–(e).

Then, there exists a set
F\subset E^{\prime}
with

\mu(F)\gtrsim\mu(E^{\prime})\geq\delta\mu(E)

(4.3)

and such that

\int_{F}\int_{0}^{\diam(E)}\frac{\mu(X(x,J_{0},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\\
\leq(2C_{\mathsf{Prop}})^{CAM\tau^{-1}}\bigg(\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\diam(E)}\frac{\mu(X(x,G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\bigg).

(4.4)

Here
C_{\mathsf{Prop}}=C\tau^{-1}\delta^{-1}(AM)^{C}
is as in Proposition
4.1
.

Proof.

We prove (
4.4
) by iterated application of Proposition
4.1
.

For every
x\in E^{\prime}
set
\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)\coloneqq\mathcal{G}(x)
. We apply Proposition
4.1
with families
\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
, and as a result for every
x\in E^{\prime}
we obtain new families of intervals, which we denote by
\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
. By assertion (1) of the proposition, each
I_{*}\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
contains an interval
I\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
.

By the assertion (3) of Proposition
4.1
, we have the following estimates for the conical energies associated to the sets
G_{1}(x)\coloneqq\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)}I
:

\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{1}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\\
\leq C_{\mathsf{Prop}}\bigg(\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{0}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\bigg).

(4.5)

Assertion (2) of Proposition
4.1
gives rise to two distinct cases.

If the set
E_{\mathsf{Fin},1}\coloneqq\{x\in E^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}G_{1}(x)=J_{0}\}
satisfies
\mu(E_{\mathsf{Fin},1})\geq\mu(E^{\prime})/4
, then we finish the iteration.

Otherwise, by (
4.1
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{1}(x))\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))\,d\mu(x)+c\tau\varepsilon\mu(E^{\prime})\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})

\displaystyle\geq c\varepsilon\tau\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E^{\prime})

for some small absolute constant
c>0
. Recall that
\varepsilon=c_{\varepsilon}A^{-1}M^{-1}
with
c_{\varepsilon}
absolute.

If the iteration has not finished, then we apply again Proposition
4.1
, with the same parameters
M,\delta
and
\tau
, the same set
E^{\prime}
, and with the family of intervals
\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
. The assumptions (a) – (d) are clearly satisfied. To see that (e) also holds, recall that each of the intervals
I_{*}\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
contains some
I\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
. At the same time, each
I\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
contained some
\theta_{I}\in I
with the desired property. Taking
\theta_{I_{*}}\coloneqq\theta_{I}
establishes (e).

Denote the resulting new families of intervals by
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
. As before, we get

\displaystyle\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{2}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{Prop}}\bigg(\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{1}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\bigg)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:new-ener}}{\leq}2C_{\mathsf{Prop}}^{2}\bigg(\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{0}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\bigg).

Furthermore, if the iteration has not finished (i.e.
\mu(E_{\mathsf{Fin},2})<\mu(E^{\prime})/4
, where
E_{\mathsf{Fin},2}=\{x\in E^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}G_{2}(x)=J_{0}\}
), then

\displaystyle\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{2}(x))\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{1}(x))\,d\mu(x)+c\tau\varepsilon\mu(E^{\prime})\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})

\displaystyle\geq 2c\varepsilon\tau\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E^{\prime}).

In general, after applying Proposition
4.1

k
-many times, we have

\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{k}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\\
\leq(2C_{\mathsf{Prop}})^{k}\bigg(\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{0}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\bigg),

(4.6)

and if the iteration has not finished (i.e.
\mu(E_{\mathsf{Fin},k})<\mu(E^{\prime})/4
), then

\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{k}(x))\,d\mu(x)\geq kc\varepsilon\tau\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E^{\prime}).

(4.7)

At the same time, recall that for every
x\in E^{\prime}
and any
k\geq 0
we have
G_{k}(x)\subset J_{0}
. Thus,

\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{k}(x))\,d\mu(x)\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E^{\prime}).

Comparing this to (
4.7
), we get that if the iteration has not finished after
k
steps, then

kc\varepsilon\tau\leq 1.

It follows that the iteration finishes after at most
(c\varepsilon\tau)^{-1}
-many steps. Recalling that
\varepsilon\sim A^{-1}M^{-1}
, we get that for some integer
1\leq k_{0}\leq CAM\tau^{-1}
the iteration has stopped after
k_{0}
steps.

In particular, the set
F\coloneqq E_{\mathsf{Fin},k_{0}}=\{x\in E^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}G_{k_{0}}(x)=J_{0}\}
satisfies

\mu(E_{\mathsf{Fin}})\geq\frac{\mu(E^{\prime})}{4}\gtrsim\delta\mu(E),

and the estimate (
4.4
) follows from

\displaystyle\int_{F}\int_{0}^{\diam(E)}

\displaystyle\frac{\mu(X(x,J_{0},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\diam(E)}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{k_{0}}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:ener-est-fin}}{\leq}(2C_{\mathsf{Prop}})^{k_{0}}\bigg(\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{0}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\bigg)

and the fact that
k_{0}\leq CAM\tau^{-1}
.

∎

We continue with the proof of Theorem
1.1
. In Appendix
A
we reduce matters to the case of finite unions of parallel segments. Hence, to prove Theorem
1.1
it is enough to establish the following.

Lemma 4.3
.

Suppose that
E\subset[0,1]^{2}
is a finite union of parallel segments, that it is Ahlfors regular with constant
A
, and that
\Fav(E)\geq\kappa\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
.

Then, there exists a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
with
\lip(\Gamma)\lesssim_{A,\kappa}1
such that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap\Gamma)\gtrsim_{\kappa,A}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

Proof.

Without loss of generality assume that
\diam(E)=1
. Our goal is to apply Corollary
4.2
. We need to find a subset
E^{\prime}\subset E
, an interval
J_{0}\in\Delta(\mathbb{T})
, and families
\mathcal{G}(x)
that satisfy the assumptions of that corollary. For that purpose we will use Lemma
3.5
.

Let

G\coloneqq\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))\geq\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)\}.

Since

\int_{\mathbb{T}\setminus G}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))\,d\theta\leq\frac{\kappa}{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E),

the assumption
\Fav(E)\geq\kappa\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
gives

\int_{G}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))\,d\theta\geq\frac{\kappa}{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

Taking into account the inequality
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E))\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
, we arrive at

\mathcal{H}^{1}(G)\geq\frac{\kappa}{2}.

Consider all triadic intervals
J\in\Delta(\mathbb{T})
of some fixed length
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\sim c_{J}A^{-1}\kappa
, where
c_{J}\in(0,1)
is the constant from Proposition
4.1
(b). Since such intervals partition
\mathbb{T}
, there exists at least one such triadic interval
J_{0}\in\Delta(\mathbb{T})
satisfying
