\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0}\cap G)\geq\frac{\kappa}{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0}).

We apply Lemma
3.5
to
E
and
G_{0}\coloneqq J_{0}\cap G
. It follows that there exists a set
E^{\prime}\subset E
such that

•

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E^{\prime})\gtrsim\kappa\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
,

•

for every
x\in E^{\prime}
we have a finite family of disjoint triadic intervals
\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\subset\Delta(J_{0})
with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{1}(x))\gtrsim\kappa\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0})\gtrsim\kappa^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})
,

•

for every
x\in E^{\prime}
and
I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
there exists
\theta_{I}\in I
such that
\mu_{\theta_{I}}(x)\leq M\coloneqq C\kappa^{-1}

•

for every
x\in E^{\prime}

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{1}^{\perp}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim M\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}).

(4.8)

Hence, we may use Corollary
4.2
with parameters
\delta\sim\kappa
,
\tau\sim\kappa^{2}
,
M\sim\kappa^{-1}
, and data
E^{\prime}\subset E
,
J_{0}
,
\mathcal{G}(x)=\{I^{\perp}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\}.

We obtain a subset
F\subset E^{\prime}
with
\mu(F)\gtrsim\kappa\mu(E)
and such that (
4.4
) holds. Since
C_{\mathsf{Prop}}\sim\tau^{-1}\delta^{-1}(AM)^{C}\sim(\kappa^{-1}A)^{C}
and
AM\tau^{-1}\sim A\kappa^{-3}
, this gives

\int_{F}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,J_{0},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\\
\leq(CA\kappa^{-1})^{CA\kappa^{-3}}\bigg(\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\diam(E)}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{1}^{\perp}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\bigg)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:bdd ener}}{\lesssim}(CA\kappa^{-1})^{CA\kappa^{-3}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E).

(4.9)

Let
F^{\prime}\subset F
consist of points
x\in F
such that

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,J_{0},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\leq 2\frac{1}{\mu(F)}\int_{F}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,J_{0},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x),

so that by Chebyshev’s inequality
\mu(F^{\prime})\geq\mu(F)/2\gtrsim\kappa\mu(E)
.

For every
x\in F^{\prime}
we apply the estimate (
3.23
) from Lemma
3.19
to bound

\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.5J_{0})\lesssim A\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})^{-1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,J_{0},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}+1\\
\leq 2A\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})^{-1}\frac{1}{\mu(F)}\int_{F}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,J_{0},r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+1\\
\lesssim A\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})^{-1}\frac{1}{\mu(F)}(CA\kappa^{-1})^{CA\kappa^{-3}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\lesssim(CA\kappa^{-1})^{CA\kappa^{-3}}.

Having a uniform bound for
\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.5J_{0})
for all
x\in F^{\prime}
, we may use Theorem
3.17
with
\tau=\mathcal{H}^{1}(F^{\prime})\sim\kappa\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)\gtrsim\kappa A^{-1}
and
M_{0}\sim(CA\kappa^{-1})^{CA\kappa^{-3}}
.

As a result, we obtain a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
with

\lip(\Gamma)\lesssim 2^{M_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})^{-1}\lesssim\exp((CA\kappa^{-1})^{CA\kappa^{-3}})\lesssim\exp(\exp(CA\kappa^{-3}\log(A\kappa^{-1})))

and such that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap\Gamma)\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(F^{\prime}\cap\Gamma)\gtrsim(c\kappa^{2}A^{-4})^{2^{M_{0}}}\gtrsim\exp(-\exp(\exp(CA\kappa^{-3}\log(A\kappa^{-1})))).

This finishes the proof of Lemma
4.3
.
∎

Lemma
4.3
together with Lemma
A.1
give Theorem
1.1
, with estimates

\lip(\Gamma)\lesssim\exp(\exp(CA^{5}\kappa^{-3}\log(A\kappa^{-1})))

(4.10)

and

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap\Gamma)\gtrsim\exp(-\exp(\exp(CA^{5}\kappa^{-3}\log(A\kappa^{-1}))))\cdot\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

(4.11)

The rest of the article is dedicated to the proof of Proposition
4.1
.

5.
Choosing new good directions
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)

We begin the proof of Proposition
4.1
. In this section for every
x\in E^{\prime}
we choose the family of intervals
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
, and we verify assertions (1) and (2) of Proposition
4.1
.

Without loss of generality, assume
\diam(E)=1
.

5.1.
Choosing very good directions
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)

Before we define
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
for all
x\in E^{\prime}
, we choose a particularly nice set
E_{0}\subset E^{\prime}
and for every
x\in E_{0}
a set of very good directions
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
. Roughly speaking, they are points and directions where we can bound corresponding conical energies pointwise. Throughout most of the article we will only work with
E_{0}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
.

Set

\mathcal{E}_{0}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\mu(E^{\prime})}\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+1.

The case
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\infty
is not interesting because that makes the energy estimate (
4.2
) trivial, and then we may simply choose
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)=\{J_{0}\}
for all
x\in E^{\prime}
. From now on we assume
\mathcal{E}_{0}<\infty
.

Consider

E_{0}\coloneqq\bigg\{x\in E^{\prime}\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ \int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\leq 2\mathcal{E}_{0}\bigg\},

so that by Chebyshev’s inequality

\mu(E_{0})\geq\frac{1}{2}\mu(E^{\prime}).

(5.1)

Recall that

\varepsilon=c_{\varepsilon}(AM)^{-1}

for some small absolute constant
c_{\varepsilon}>0
.

For every
x\in E_{0}
let
\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
be the family of maximal triadic intervals
I\in\Delta(J_{0})
such that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(I\cap G(x))\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(I).

(5.2)

By maximality, the intervals in
\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
are disjoint. Since
G(x)
is a finite union of triadic intervals, every
I\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
contains some
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
, and every
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
is contained in a unique
I\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
. In particular, the family
\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
is a covering of
G(x)
.

Set
G_{0}(x)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)}I
, and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigg\{I\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ \int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,I\cap G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\leq 4\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))}\mathcal{E}_{0}\bigg\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{J\in\Delta(J_{0})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}3J=I\ \text{for some }I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\}.

(5.3)

We also set
G_{1}(x)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)}I
and
G_{2}(x)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)}I
. In the lemma below we prove that
G_{1}(x)
covers a substantial portion of
G(x)
.

Lemma 5.1
.

For every
x\in E_{0}
we have

\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{1}(x)\cap G(x))\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x)).

(5.4)

Proof.

Observe that for
x\in E_{0}

\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)\setminus\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))}{4\mathcal{E}_{0}}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)\setminus\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,I\cap G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\\
\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))}{4\mathcal{E}_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))}{2},

where in the last inequality we used the definition of
E_{0}
.
Hence,

\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\geq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))}{2},

and in particular

\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{1}(x)\cap G(x))=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I\cap G(x))\\
=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)-\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I\setminus G(x))\geq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))}{2}-\sum_{J\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J\setminus G(x))\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:def-G0}}{\geq}\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))}{2}-\varepsilon\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon\right)\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))\geq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))}{3},

where in the last line we used that
G(x)\subset G_{0}(x)
.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that for every
x\in E_{0}
and
I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\geq 3^{-N}
for some huge
N>0
(over which we have no quantitative control). Indeed, let

\mathcal{G}_{1,N}(x)=\{I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\geq 3^{-N}\},

G_{1,N}(x)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{1,N}(x)}I
, and

E_{0,N}=\{x\in E_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{1,N}(x))\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{1}(x))/2\}.

By continuity of measures, if we take
N
large enough then
\mu(E_{0,N})\geq\mu(E_{0})/2
. Thus, (
5.1
) and (
5.4
) still hold with
E_{0,N}
and
G_{1,N}(x)
replacing
E_{0}
and
\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
, up to changing the absolute constants slightly.

5.2.
Properties of
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)

In the following lemmas we prove some properties of
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
that make these directions very nice, and which will be crucial later on.

Recall that
\mu_{\theta}^{\perp}(x)=\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}\mu)(\pi_{\theta}^{\perp}(x))
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For every
x\in E_{0}
and
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
there exists
\theta_{J}\in 3J
such that
\mu_{\theta_{J}}^{\perp}(x)\leq M
. Consequently, for all
r>0

\mu(B_{J}(x,r))\lesssim M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r.

(5.5)

Proof.

Let
I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
be such that
I=3J
, and recall that
\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\subset\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
, so that
I
is a maximal triadic interval satisfying (
5.2
). Since
G(x)
is a finite union of triadic intervals from
\mathcal{G}(x)
, it follows that
I
contains some interval
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
(it may happen that
I^{\prime}=I
). By assumption (e) of Proposition
4.1
, there exists
\theta_{I^{\prime}}\in I^{\prime}
such that
\mu_{\theta_{I^{\prime}}}^{\perp}(x)\leq M
. Since
\theta_{I^{\prime}}\in I^{\prime}\subset I=3J
, we may take
\theta_{J}=\theta_{I^{\prime}}
.

The estimate (
5.5
) now follows immediately from Lemma
3.12
.
∎

Recall that by the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
(
5.3
) for every
x\in E_{0}
and
I\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
we have

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,3I\cap G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\frac{\mathcal{E}_{0}}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I).

(5.6)

We set

\mathcal{E}_{1}\coloneqq\max\left(\frac{\mathcal{E}_{0}}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))},M\right).

In the lemma below we show that the estimate (
5.6
) implies a similar estimate with
3I\cap G(x)
replaced by
2I
.

Recall that
\varepsilon=c_{\varepsilon}M^{-1}A^{-1}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

If
c_{\varepsilon}>0
is chosen small enough, then for all
x\in E_{0}
and
I\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
we have

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,2I,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I).

(5.7)

Proof.

We are going to use Lemma
3.15
with
J=3I
,
\delta=0,\alpha=5
. We define
\mathcal{I}
as the the collection of maximal triadic intervals contained in
3I\setminus G(x)
. Let us check that the assumptions of Lemma
3.15
are met.

First, we show that for every
J\in\mathcal{I}
there exists
\theta_{J}\in 5J
such that
\mu_{\theta_{J}}^{\perp}(x)\leq M
.
By maximality, for any
J\in\mathcal{I}
its parent
J^{1}
satisfies

J^{1}\cap G(x)\neq\varnothing.

Since
G(x)
is a union of triadic intervals from
\mathcal{G}(x)
, we have that either
J^{1}\subset G(x)
or
J^{1}
contains some interval from
\mathcal{G}(x)
. The first alternative is impossible because
J\cap G(x)=\varnothing
. Thus, there exists
J^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
such that
J^{\prime}\subset J^{1}
, and by the assumption (e) of Proposition
4.1
there exists
\theta_{J^{\prime}}\in J^{\prime}
such that
\mu_{\theta_{J^{\prime}}}^{\perp}(x)\leq M
.
Setting
\theta_{J}\coloneqq\theta_{J^{\prime}}
, we see that

\theta_{J}\in J^{1}\subset 5J.

The second assumption of Lemma
3.15
we should check is the measure bound (
3.17
). But this follows immediately from the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
: since
I\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
we have
3I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\subset\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
, and so
\mathcal{H}^{1}(3I\setminus G(x))\lesssim\varepsilon\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)
by the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
(
5.2
). This gives (
3.17
) assuming
c_{\varepsilon}\leq c_{0}/25
.

Thus, we may apply Lemma
3.15
to conclude that for any
r>0

\mu(X(x,2I,r,2r))\lesssim\mu(X(x,3I\cap G(x),r/2,4r)).

Integrating yields

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,2I,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\overset{\eqref{eq:standard2}}{\sim}\int_{0}^{1/2}\frac{\mu(X(x,2I,r,2r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\\
\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,3I\cap G(x),r/2,4r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\int_{0}^{4}\frac{\mu(X(x,3I\cap G(x),r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:ener-est}}{\lesssim}\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)+\int_{1}^{4}\frac{\mu(X(x,3I\cap G(x),r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}.

Since

\int_{1}^{4}\frac{\mu(X(x,3I\cap G(x),r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\leq\int_{1}^{4}\frac{\mu(B_{I}(x,Cr))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\mu(B_{I}(x,4C))\overset{\eqref{eq:ball-est}}{\lesssim}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(I),

and
\mathcal{E}_{1}\geq M
, we finally get (
5.7
).

∎

In the lemma below we use Lemma
3.16
to transfer the conical energy estimate (
5.7
) to nearby points.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\rho\in(0,1)
. If
x\in E_{0}
,
I\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
, and
y\in B_{I}(x,10\rho^{k})
, then

\int_{\rho^{k}}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(y,1.5I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim_{\rho}\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I).

(5.8)

Proof.

By Lemma
3.16
we have

\int_{C\rho^{k}}^{1/2}\frac{\mu(X(y,1.5I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,2I,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\overset{\eqref{eq:des-est}}{\lesssim}\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I).

for some
C=C(\rho)>1
. At the same time,

\int_{\rho^{k}}^{C\rho^{k}}\frac{\mu(X(y,1.5I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\int_{\rho^{k}}^{C\rho^{k}}\frac{\mu(B_{I}(y,C^{\prime}\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim_{\rho}\frac{\mu(B_{I}(y,C^{\prime}\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}\\
\lesssim\frac{\mu(B_{I}(x,C^{\prime\prime}\rho^{k}))}{\rho^{k}}\overset{\eqref{eq:ball-est}}{\lesssim}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)

and similarly

\int_{1/2}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(y,1.5I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\mu(B_{I}(x,2))\overset{\eqref{eq:ball-est}}{\lesssim}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(I).

Since
\mathcal{E}_{1}\geq M
, the three estimates above give (
5.8
).
∎

5.3.
Definition of
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)

In this subsection we finally define the families
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
and establish assertions (1) and (2) of Proposition
4.1
.

First, for every
x\in E_{0}
we define
\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x)
to be the maximal intervals from
\{I^{1}\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\}
. By maximality, the intervals in
\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x)
are pairwise disjoint. We set
G_{1}^{1}(x)=\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x)
.

For the sake of future reference we make the following observation: for every
I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
we have
I^{1}\subset 5I
, and since there exists
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
with
I=3J
, it follows that
I^{1}\subset 15J
. Hence,

G_{1}^{1}(x)\subset\widetilde{G_{2}}(x)\coloneqq\bigcup_{J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)}15J.

(5.9)

There are three cases to consider when defining
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
. We partition
E_{0}
into

\displaystyle E_{00}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{x\in E_{0}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)=\{J_{0}\}\},

\displaystyle E_{01}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{x\in E_{0}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)\neq\{J_{0}\}\}.

Points
x\in E^{\prime}\setminus E_{0}
.
For such
x
we set
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)\coloneqq\mathcal{G}(x)
.

Points
x\in E_{00}
.
For such
x
we set
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)=\{J_{0}\}
. Recalling that
E_{\mathsf{Fin}}=\{x\in E^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}G_{*}(x)=J_{0}\}
, we get that
E_{00}\subset E_{\mathsf{Fin}}
. Remark that
x\in E_{00}
if and only if
\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)=\{J_{0}\}
, simply because
\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\subset\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
and
\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
is non-empty.

Points
x\in E_{01}
.

Observe that if
x\in E_{0}
satisfies
\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)\neq\{J_{0}\}
, then every
I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\subset\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
is strictly contained in
J_{0}
, and in particular
\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x)\subset\Delta(J_{0})
. For all such
x
we define
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
to be the family of maximal intervals from

\mathcal{G}(x)\cup\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x).

Taking into account that every
I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
contains some
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
, it is easy to see that
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
consists of all the intervals from
\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x)
and those intervals from
\mathcal{G}(x)
which are not contained in any interval from
\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x)
.

We set
G_{*}(x)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)}I
.

Assertion (1)

We check now that assertion (1) of Proposition
4.1
holds for the choice of
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
we made above.

Lemma 5.6
.

Every
I\in\mathcal{G}(x)
is contained in some
I_{*}\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
, and every
I_{*}\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
contains some
I\in\mathcal{G}(x)
.

Proof.

If
x\in E^{\prime}\setminus E_{0}
we have
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)=\mathcal{G}(x)
, so this is trivial. Similarly, for
x\in E_{00}
we have
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)=\{J_{0}\}
so there is nothing to check. Suppose that
x\in E_{01}
.

Since
\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
is the family of maximal intervals from
\mathcal{G}(x)\cup\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x)
, it is clear that every
I\in\mathcal{G}(x)
is contained in some
I_{*}\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
. Now suppose that
I_{*}\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
, and we wish to find
I\in\mathcal{G}(x)
contained in
I_{*}
. If
I_{*}\in\mathcal{G}(x)
then there is nothing to do, so assume
I_{*}\in\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x)
. Let
J\subset I_{*}
be an interval from
\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
such that
J^{1}=I_{*}
. Since
\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)\subset\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
we get that
J
is a maximal triadic subinterval of
J_{0}
satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J\cap G(x))\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)
. But this implies that there is some
I\in\mathcal{G}(x)
with
I\subset J\subset J^{1}=I_{*}
. This concludes the proof of assertion (1).
∎

Assertion (2)

We will derive assertion (2) of Proposition
4.1
from the following lemma.

Lemma 5.7
.

There exists an absolute constant
0<c<1
such that if
x\in E_{0}\setminus E_{\mathsf{Fin}}
, then
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{*}(x))\geq(1+c\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))
.

Proof.

By the definition of
\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
(
5.2
), for every
I\in\mathcal{G}_{0}(x)
strictly contained in
J_{0}
we have

\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{1}\cap G(x))\leq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{1}).

In particular, since
x\notin E_{\mathsf{Fin}}\supset E_{00}
we get that
x\in E_{01}\setminus E_{\mathsf{Fin}}
. Consequently, all the
I\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)
are strictly contained in
J_{0}
, and for every
I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x)
we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I\cap G(x))\leq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)
. Moreover, taking into account that
G_{1}(x)\subset G_{1}^{1}(x)
, (
5.4
) implies

\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x)\cap G^{1}_{1}(x))\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{*}(x))

\displaystyle=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)\cap\mathcal{G}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)\setminus\mathcal{G}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)\cap\mathcal{G}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)+\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{*}(x)\cap\mathcal{G}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)+\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}^{1}(x)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I\cap G(x))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x)\setminus G^{1}_{1}(x))+\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x)\cap G^{1}_{1}(x))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))-\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x)\cap G^{1}_{1}(x))+\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x)\cap G^{1}_{1}(x))

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))+\varepsilon\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x)\cap G^{1}_{1}(x))\overset{\eqref{eq:G1large}}{\geq}(1+c\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x)).

∎

Now we can easily establish assertion (2) of Proposition
4.1
. If

\mu(E_{\mathsf{Fin}})\geq\frac{\mu(E_{0})}{2}\overset{\eqref{eq:E0large}}{\geq}\frac{\mu(E^{\prime})}{4},

then we get the first alternative of assertion (2).

Suppose the opposite inequality holds:
\mu(E_{\mathsf{Fin}})\leq\mu(E_{0})/2
. Then,

\mu(E_{0}\setminus E_{\mathsf{Fin}})\geq\frac{\mu(E_{0})}{2}\overset{\eqref{eq:E0large}}{\geq}\frac{\mu(E^{\prime})}{4}.

(5.10)

By the already established assertion (1) we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{*}(x))\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))
for every
x\in E^{\prime}
. Using also Lemma
5.7
and that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\geq\tau\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})
by the assumption (d) of Proposition
4.1
, we get

\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{*}(x))\,d\mu(x)=\int_{E^{\prime}\setminus(E_{0}\setminus E_{\mathsf{Fin}})}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{*}(x))\,d\mu(x)+\int_{E_{0}\setminus E_{\mathsf{Fin}}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{*}(x))\,d\mu(x)\\
\geq\int_{E^{\prime}\setminus(E_{0}\setminus E_{\mathsf{Fin}})}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\,d\mu(x)+\left(1+c\varepsilon\right)\int_{E_{0}\setminus E_{\mathsf{Fin}}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\,d\mu(x)\\
=\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\,d\mu(x)+c\varepsilon\int_{E_{0}\setminus E_{\mathsf{Fin}}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\,d\mu(x)\\
\geq\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\,d\mu(x)+c\varepsilon\tau\mu(E_{0}\setminus E_{\mathsf{Fin}})\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:dcefe}}{\geq}\int_{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G(x))\,d\mu(x)+\tfrac{c}{4}\varepsilon\tau\mu(E^{\prime})\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0}).

This establishes the second alternative of assertion (2), the estimate (
4.1
).

Towards assertion (3)

To complete the proof of Proposition
4.1
it remains to prove assertion (3), which states that

\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{*}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\\
\leq C_{\mathsf{Prop}}\bigg(\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\bigg),

(5.11)

where
C_{\mathsf{Prop}}=C\tau^{-1}\delta^{-1}(AM)^{C}
.
Recalling that for
x\in E^{\prime}\setminus E_{0}
we have
G_{*}(x)=G(x)
, the estimate above follows from

\int_{E_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G_{*}(x)\setminus G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\\
\leq(C_{\mathsf{Prop}}-1)\bigg(\int_{E^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,G(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\bigg)\\
=(C_{\mathsf{Prop}}-1)\bigg(\mathcal{E}_{0}\mu(E^{\prime})+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\bigg).

(5.12)

Lemma 5.8
.

For all
x\in E_{0}
we have

G_{*}(x)\setminus G(x)\subset\widetilde{G_{2}}(x),

where
\widetilde{G_{2}}(x)=\bigcup_{J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)}15J
.

Proof.

By (
5.9
), it suffices to show that
G_{*}(x)\setminus G(x)\subset G_{1}^{1}(x)
.

If
x\in E_{00}
, then we already remarked that
J_{0}\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
. In particular,
G_{*}(x)=J_{0}\subset J_{0}^{1}=G_{1}^{1}(x)
.

Now suppose that
x\in E_{01}
. Then
G_{*}(x)=G(x)\cup G_{1}^{1}(x)
and so
G_{*}(x)\setminus G(x)\subset G^{1}_{1}(x)
.
∎

In the light of lemma above and (
5.12
), and using the fact that

\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)=\max\left(\mathcal{E}_{0}\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{0}(x))}\frac{\mu(E)}{\mu(E^{\prime})}\mu(E^{\prime}),M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\right)\\
\lesssim\max\left(\tau^{-1}\delta^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{0}\mu(E^{\prime}),M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\right),

to finish the proof of Proposition
4.1
, it suffices to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 5.9
.

We have

\int_{E_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,\widetilde{G_{2}}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\lesssim(AM)^{11}\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E).

(5.13)

The remainder of the article is dedicated to the proof of this estimate.

6.
Dyadic lattices

6.1.
David-Mattila dyadic lattice

In the proof of Proposition
5.9
we will extensively use generalized dyadic lattices constructed by David and Mattila in
[
DM00
, Theorem 3.2]
. Although
[
DM00
]
deals with the usual Euclidean space, their result easily implies a similar statement valid for the metric spaces
(\mathbb{R}^{2},d_{J})
, where
d_{J}
are the metrics defined in Subsection
3.4
. We explain this further in Remark
6.2
.

Lemma 6.1
.

There exist parameters
C_{0}>1
and
0<\rho=\rho(C_{0})<(5000C_{0})^{-1}
such that the following holds. Let
\mu
be a Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
E=\supp\mu
. Let
J\subset\mathbb{T}
be an interval. There exists a sequence of partitions of
E
into Borel subsets
\mathcal{D}(E,J)=\{\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}k\geq 0\}
with the following properties:

a)

For each integer
k\geq 0

E=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J)}Q

and the sum above is disjoint.

b)

If
k<l
,
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{l}(E,J)
, and
R\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J)
, then either
Q\cap R=\varnothing
or else
Q\subset R
.

c)

For each
k\geq 0
and each cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J)
there is a ball
B(Q)=B_{J}(x_{Q},r(Q))
such that

\displaystyle x_{Q}\in Q,\quad\rho^{k}\leq r(Q)\leq C_{0}\rho^{k},

(6.1)

\displaystyle E\cap B(Q)\subset Q\subset E\cap 28B(Q)=E\cap B_{J}(x_{Q},28r(Q)),

(6.2)

and the balls
5B(Q),Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J),
are disjoint.

d)

The cubes
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J)
have small boundaries in the following sense. For each
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J)
and each integer
l\geq 0
set

\displaystyle N_{l}^{ext}(Q)=\{x\in E\setminus Q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\dist_{J}(x,Q)<\rho^{k+l}\},

\displaystyle N_{l}^{int}(Q)=\{x\in Q\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\dist_{J}(x,E\setminus Q)<\rho^{k+l}\},

\displaystyle N_{l}(Q)=N_{l}^{ext}(Q)\cup N_{l}^{int}(Q).

Then

\mu(N_{l}(Q))\leq\rho^{l/2}\mu(90B(Q)).

(6.3)

Remark 6.2
.

As mentioned above, the construction from
[
DM00
, Theorem 3.2]
only deals with the standard Euclidean metric, which corresponds to the case of
J=\mathbb{T}
above. We explain briefly how this implies Lemma
6.1
for general
J
.

Recall that there exists a linear map
A_{J}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
A_{J}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}(\mathbb{R}^{2},d_{euc})\to(\mathbb{R}^{2},d_{J})
is an isometry, see Remark
3.6
.
Given a measure
\mu
on
(\mathbb{R}^{2},d_{J})
with
\supp\mu=E
we define the push-forward measure
\mu_{J}=A_{J}^{-1}\mu
, we apply
[
DM00
, Theorem 3.2]
to
\mu_{J}
to get a sequence of partitions
\mathcal{D}_{k}(E_{J})
, where
E_{J}=A_{J}^{-1}(E)
, and finally we obtain
\mathcal{D}(E,J)
as

\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J)\coloneqq\{A_{J}(Q)\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E_{J})\}.

Remark 6.3
.

It may seem that using the construction from
[
DM00
]
is an overkill given that the classical construction of Christ
[
Chr90
]
is already valid in metric measure spaces endowed with a doubling measure, and it has the thin boundaries property. However, we cannot ensure that
(E,d_{J},\mu)
is doubling, and so we cannot apply the result from
[
Chr90
]
. Nevertheless, most of the balls appearing in our proof indeed will be doubling, which will allow us to use (
6.3
).

We introduce additional notation. For
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J)
we set
\ell(Q)=\rho^{k}
and

B_{Q}\coloneqq 50B(Q)=B_{J}(x_{Q},50r(Q)),

so that
r(B_{Q})\sim_{C_{0}}\ell(Q)
,
Q\subset E\cap B_{Q}
, and
B_{Q}\subset B_{P}
whenever
Q\subset P
. From now on we fix
C_{0}
and
\rho
as above, and we consider them absolute constants.

6.2.
Shattering cubes

In Section
7
we are going to define a tree-like structure
\mathcal{T}
consisting of subsets of
E
. Typically, similar tree constructions go as follows:

(1)

Fix a dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}
on
E
and choose a root cube
R\in\mathcal{D}
. Add
R
to
\mathcal{T}
.

(2)

Define a set of conditions you want the cubes in
\mathcal{T}
to satisfy.

(3)

Inductively add cubes to
\mathcal{T}
: if
Q\in\mathcal{T}
, then for any dyadic child
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
test whether
Q^{\prime}
satisfies the desired conditions. If yes, add
Q^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{T}
, otherwise stop the construction on
Q^{\prime}
.

Our construction will differ from the above algorithm in the following way: instead of using a single dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}
, we need to use a countable family of lattices corresponding to different metrics
\{d_{J}\}_{J\in\Delta(\mathbb{T})}
. We will explain this in detail in Section
7
. One of the operations we need to perform is dividing a cube
Q\in\mathcal{T}
with a corresponding metric
d_{Q}
into subcubes
Q^{\prime}\subset Q
of the same generation, i.e.
\ell(Q^{\prime})=\ell(Q)
, but corresponding to a different metric
d_{Q^{\prime}}
. We call this operation “shattering a cube”, and we describe it in this subsection.

We begin with some notation. Set

\mathbb{D}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}(E,\mathbb{T}),

so that
\mathbb{D}
is the “usual” lattice of David-Mattila cubes, corresponding to the Euclidean distance. We also set
\mathbb{D}_{k}\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,\mathbb{T})
.
Note that by (
6.1
) for every
Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}

E\cap B(x_{Q},\rho^{k})\subset Q\subset B(x_{Q},\rho^{k-1}).

(6.4)

We are going to use
\mathbb{D}
as a building block for a family of partitions of
E
that will be used in Section
7
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Assume that
J_{P}\subset\mathbb{T}
is an interval and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
m(P)\in\mathbb{N}
be the unique integer such that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})\rho^{k+2}<5\rho^{m(P)}\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})\rho^{k+1}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathbb{D}_{m(P)}
is a non empty family of cubes, and set
P=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{P}}Q
.

Let
J\subset J_{P}
also be an interval, and let
l\geq 0
be an integer. We can define a family of “dyadic descendants” of
P
corresponding to metric
d_{J}
, denoted by
\mathbb{D}_{k+l}(P,J)
, such that

P=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k+l}(P,J)}Q,

(6.5)

the sets in
\mathbb{D}_{k+l}(P,J)
are pairwise disjoint, and
for every
Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k+l}(P,J)
:

(i)

there exists
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathbb{D}_{m}
such that
Q=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{Q}}S
, where
m\geq m(P)
is the unique integer satisfying

\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\rho^{k+l+2}<5\rho^{m}\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\rho^{k+l+1}.

(6.6)

(ii)

there exists
S\in\mathcal{Q}
such that for
x_{Q}\coloneqq x_{S}
we have

B_{J}(x_{Q},0.5\rho^{k+l})\cap P\subset Q\subset B_{J}(x_{Q},4\rho^{k+l})\cap P.

(6.7)

Remark 6.5
.

The lemma is formulated in this slightly awkward way to underline the fact that the resulting cubes
Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k+l}(P,J)
have similar structure to the cube
P
we started with, and so the lemma may be applied to each
Q
again (with
k+l
replacing
k
and
J
replacing
J_{P}
).

Remark 6.6
.

In our application we will mostly use this construction for
P\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(S,J_{P})
for some
S\subset E
,
J_{P}\in\Delta(J_{0})
, and:

(i)

for
l=0
,
J\in\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(J_{P})
. To simplify notation, we set

\mathsf{Sh}(P,J)\coloneqq\mathbb{D}_{k}(P,J).

\mathsf{Sh}(P,J)
should be seen as a “shattering” of
P
into cubes of the same generation as
P
corresponding to directions from
J
.

(ii)

for
l=1,

J=J_{P}
. We set

\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})\coloneqq\mathbb{D}_{k+1}(P,J_{P}).

If
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})
, we will say that
P
is the parent of
Q
, and we will write
Q^{1}\coloneqq P
.

Proof of Lemma
6.4
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be the unique integer satisfying (
6.6
).
Since
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})
and
l\geq 0
, we have
m\geq m(P)
. Define

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\coloneqq\{S\in\mathbb{D}_{m}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\subset P\},

so that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is a partition of
P
, and for every
S\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
there is a unique
R\in\mathcal{P}
such that
S\subset R
. We are going to partition
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
into a finite number of subfamilies
\{\mathcal{Q}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that

\mathbb{D}_{k+l}(P,J)\coloneqq\{Q_{i}\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ Q_{i}\coloneqq\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}}S,\ i\in I\}

will have all the desired properties.

First, let
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a maximal subset of
\{x_{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\}
such that for any
i\neq i^{\prime}\in I
we have
d_{J}(x_{i},x_{i^{\prime}})>3\rho^{k+l}
. In other words, it is a maximal
3\rho^{k+l}
-separated subset of
\{x_{S}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\}
with respect to metric
d_{J}
.

Now to each
S\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
we will associate some
i\in I
, denoted by
i(S)
. We do this as follows: if there exists
i\in I
such that
d_{J}(x_{i},x_{S})\leq\rho^{k+l}
, then we say that
S
is very close to
x_{i}
, and we set
i(S)\coloneqq i
. Note that there is at most one such
i\in I
, because if we also had
d_{J}(x_{i^{\prime}},x_{S})\leq\rho^{k+l}
for another
i^{\prime}\in I
, then by the triangle inequality
d_{J}(x_{i},x_{i^{\prime}})\leq 2\rho^{k+l}
, which is a contradiction with the
3\rho^{k+l}
-separation of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}
.

If
S
is not very close to any
x_{i}
, then we pick any
i\in I
satisfying
d_{J}(x_{i},x_{S})\leq 3\rho^{k+l}
and set
i(S)=i
. Note that at least one such
i\in I
exists by the maximality of
\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}
. In this case we say that
S
is pretty close to
x_{i}
.

Having chosen
i(S)\in I
for every
S\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, we are ready to define the families
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
. For each
i\in I
we set

\mathcal{Q}_{i}\coloneqq\{S\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}i(S)=i\}

and
Q_{i}=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}}S
. We also set
x_{Q_{i}}\coloneqq x_{i}
and
\mathbb{D}_{k+l}(P,J)\coloneqq\{Q_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}i\in I\}
. Note that

P=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}S=\bigcup_{i\in I}\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}}S=\bigcup_{i\in I}Q_{i},

so that (
6.5
) holds. Since each
S\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
belongs to exactly one family
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
, the sets
Q_{i}
are pairwise disjoint.

It remains to show (
6.7
). Fix
Q_{i}
. First, observe that for all
S\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{D}_{m}
we have

\diam_{J}(S)\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)^{-1}\diam(S)\overset{\eqref{eq:cube-size2}}{\leq}2\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)^{-1}\rho^{m-1}\overset{\eqref{eq:side}}{\leq}0.4\rho^{k+l}.

Let
z\in B_{J}(x_{i},0.5\rho^{k+l})\cap P
, and let
S\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be the unique cube from
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
containing
z
. Then,

d_{J}(x_{S},x_{i})\leq d_{J}(x_{S},z)+d_{J}(z,x_{i})\leq 0.4\rho^{k+l}+0.5\rho^{k+l}\leq\rho^{k+l},

and so
S
is very close to
x_{i}
. This means that
S\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
, which implies
z\in S\subset Q_{i}
. Hence,

B_{J}(x_{i},0.5\rho^{k+l})\cap P\subset Q_{i}.

Now suppose that
z\in Q_{i}
, and let
S\in\mathcal{Q}_{i}
be the unique cube containing
z
. Note that
S
is either very close or pretty close to
x_{i}
, so that
d_{J}(x_{S},x_{i})\leq 3\rho^{k+l}
. It follows that

d_{J}(z,x_{i})\leq d_{J}(z,x_{S})+d_{J}(x_{S},x_{i})\leq\diam_{J}(S)+d_{J}(x_{S},x_{i})\leq 0.4\rho^{k+l}+3\rho^{k+l}.

This gives
Q_{i}\subset B_{J}(x_{i},4\rho^{k+l})\cap P
.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

Recall that the main reason behind the construction above is to be able to shatter a cube, i.e. divide it into a family
\mathsf{Sh}(P,J)=\mathbb{D}_{k}(P,J)
. It may seem more natural to simply apply the David-Mattila lattice (Lemma
6.1
) to
\mu|_{P}
and
J
to get the desired partition
\widetilde{\mathsf{Sh}}(P,J)\coloneqq\mathcal{D}_{k}(P,J)
. However, it is not clear whether the cubes
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(P,J)
satisfy

\mu(Q)\gtrsim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\rho^{k}

and we desperately need such lower bounds in subsequent arguments.

On the other hand, for the partitions we constructed above such lower bounds are easy to obtain: any
Q\in\mathsf{Sh}(P,J)=\mathbb{D}_{k}(P,J)
contains some
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}_{m}
with
\rho^{m}\sim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\rho^{k}
, and then by lower Ahlfors regularity of
\mu
we get

\mu(Q)\geq\mu(Q^{\prime})\geq\mu(B(x_{Q},\rho^{m}))\gtrsim A^{-1}\rho^{m}\gtrsim A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\rho^{k}.

7.
Construction of a tree

In this section we construct a tree-like structure
\mathcal{T}
consisting of subsets of
E
, which is adapted to our set of “good directions”

\{(x,\theta)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}x\in E_{0},\theta\in G_{2}(x)\},

and which we will use later on to prove the energy estimate (
5.13
).

Recall that for every
x\in E_{0}
there exists a family of “very good” intervals
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
defined in Subsection
5.1
. Since the conical energy estimates are passed on to nearby points (see Lemma
5.5
), it is natural to consider families of good intervals corresponding to different scales.

Definition 7.1
.

For every
x\in E
and
k\geq 0
we define
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(x,k)\subset\Delta(J_{0})
as the family of triadic intervals satisfying the following:
I\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(x,k)
if and only if there exists
y\in E_{0}\cap B_{I}(x,10\rho^{k})
such that
I\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(y)
.

We also define
\mathcal{G}(x,k)
as the collection of maximal intervals from
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(x,k)
, and write

G(x,k)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}(x,k)}I.

We are going to use the collections
\mathcal{G}(x,k)
to define a tree-like structure
\mathcal{T}
consisting of subsets of
E
.

Remark 7.2
.

To be more precise, one should see
\mathcal{T}
as a collection of triples
(Q,J_{Q},k_{Q})
, where
Q\subset E
is a subset of
E
,
J_{Q}\in\Delta(J_{0})
is the interval of directions corresponding to
Q
, and
k_{Q}\in\mathbb{N}
is the generation of
Q
. The shape of each cube
Q
will be roughly
B_{J_{Q}}(x_{Q},\rho^{k_{Q}})
. For simplicity, we will usually keep
J_{Q}
and
k_{Q}
implicit, so that statements like “
Q,P\in\mathcal{T},\,Q\neq P
” should be understood as “
(Q,J_{Q},k_{Q})\neq(P,J_{P},k_{P})
”, and the underlying sets
Q
and
P
might coincide.

Recall that

\varepsilon\coloneqq c_{\varepsilon}M^{-1}A^{-1},

where
0<c_{\varepsilon}<1
is a small absolute constant. We set

\mathcal{E}_{2}\coloneqq A\mathcal{E}_{1}.

(7.1)

Proposition 7.3
.

Assume that the absolute constant
0<c_{\varepsilon}<1
is small enough. Then, there exists a collection
\mathcal{T}
of subsets of
E
satisfying the following properties:

(T1)

\mathcal{T}=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{T}_{k}
, and for each
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
there is
x_{Q}\in Q
and
J_{Q}\in\Delta(J_{0})
such that

B(x_{Q},\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\rho^{k+3})\cap E\subset Q\subset B_{J_{Q}}(x_{Q},4\rho^{k})\cap E,

(T2)

for every
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k},\ k\geq 1
, there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap Q
with
J_{Q}\cap G_{2}(x)\neq\varnothing
, and

\int_{Q}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}\cap G(x,k))\,d\mu(x)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q),

(T3)

for every
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
,
k\geq l\geq 0
, there is a unique
P\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
such that
Q\subset P
and
J_{Q}\subset J_{P}
; this introduces a partial order on
\mathcal{T}
, and we denote it by
Q\prec P
,

(T4)

if
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k},

P\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
,
k\geq l\geq 0
, then either
Q\prec P
, or
Q\cap P=\varnothing
, or
J_{Q}\cap J_{P}=\varnothing
,

(T5)

there is a family
\mathsf{Roots}\subset\mathcal{T}
satisfying

\sum_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\mu(R)\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E),

(T6)

there is a partition

\mathcal{T}=\bigcup_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}}\mathcal{T}(R)

such that each
\mathcal{T}(R)
is a subtree of
\mathcal{T}
with root
R
, and

\mathcal{T}(R)=\{Q\in\mathcal{T}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,Q\prec R,\,J_{Q}=J_{R}\}.

(T7)

for every
x\in E_{0}
,
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
, and
k\geq 0
, there is a unique
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
such that
x\in Q
and
J\subset J_{Q}
. In particular, for every
k\geq 0
we have

\{(x,\theta)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}x\in E_{0},\,\theta\in G_{2}(x)\}\subset\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}}Q\times J_{Q}.

(T8)

for every
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
and
x\in Q
we have

\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.8J_{Q},0,k)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{2},

where

\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.8J_{Q},0,k)=\{0\leq j\leq k\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,X(x,0.8J_{Q},\rho^{j+1},\rho^{j})\cap E\neq\varnothing\}

The construction of
\mathcal{T}
above occupies the rest of this section. The properties
(T1)
–
(T8)
are proven in Section
8
.

Before we begin the construction in earnest we describe the general idea. At the beginning of Subsection
6.2
we discussed how the usual stopping time arguments are used to construct trees of cubes satisfying desired properties. Our construction is rather different because there is no fixed dyadic lattice on
E
that works well for our purposes, but rather we have to use cubes corresponding to different directions at different scales. This could be seen as a stopping time argument moving in two directions (generation
k\in\mathbb{N}
and directions
J\in\Delta(J_{0})
), while normally there is only one direction (generation
k\in\mathbb{N}
).

Our algorithm is roughly the following.

Step 1.
We define the
0
-th generation
\mathcal{T}_{0}\subset\mathbb{D}_{0}(E,J_{0})
consisting of cubes of sidelength
1
corresponding to directions in
J_{0}
. This choice is somewhat arbitrary.

Step 2.
The key properties of
\mathcal{T}
we need are
(T5)
–
(T8)
. This leads us to the following desired properties that the cubes from
\mathcal{T}
should satisfy:

(S1)

for every
Q\in\mathcal{T}
there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap Q
with
G_{2}(x)\cap J_{Q}\neq\varnothing
,

(S2)

if
Q=(Q,J_{Q},k)\in\mathcal{T}
then

\int_{Q}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}\cap G(y,k))\,d\mu(y)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q).

The condition
(S1)
comes from
(T7)
: if a cube fails
(S1)
then it is useless to us. The condition
(S2)
will be used to derive
(T5)
–
(T8)
. Note that if
(S2)
holds, then for most of
y\in Q
the interval
J_{Q}
is mostly covered by
G(y,k)
, the intervals good for
y
at scale
k
.

Step 3.
We add cubes to
\mathcal{T}
inductively. Recall that in Remark
6.6
we introduced the notation
\mathsf{Ch}(Q,J_{Q})
and
\mathsf{Sh}(Q,J)
corresponding to different partitions of
Q
.

If
Q=(Q,J_{Q},k)\in\mathcal{T}
, then for every “dyadic child”
Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Ch}(Q,J_{Q})=\mathbb{D}_{k+1}(Q,J_{Q})
we check whether
(Q^{\prime},J_{Q},k+1)
satisfies the desired conditions
(S1)
and
(S2)
. If yes, add
(Q^{\prime},J_{Q},k+1)
to
\mathcal{T}
. If not, there are two cases.

If
(S1)
fails, then the construction stops at
Q^{\prime}
.

If
(S1)
holds, but
(S2)
fails, then we cannot simply stop the construction; if we did, then our tree would not satisfy
(T7)
. What we do instead is perform a “shattering procedure”: for every triadic child
J\in\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(J_{Q})
and every “shattered subcube”
P\in\mathsf{Sh}(Q^{\prime},J)=\mathbb{D}_{k+1}(Q^{\prime},J)
we check whether
(P,J,k+1)
satisfies
(S1)
and
(S2’)
, a modified version of
(S2)
which we discuss below. If yes, we set
J_{P}=J
and add
(P,J_{P},k+1)
to
\mathcal{T}
. If
(S1)
fails, we stop the construction at
P
. Finally, if
(S1)
holds but
(S2’)
fails, then we shatter
P
again, and continue in a similar way with the shattered subcubes of
P
.

This concludes the informal description of our algorithm.

Remark 7.4
.

The family
\mathsf{Roots}
consists of
\mathcal{T}_{0}
and the cubes
Q\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0}
that arise from the shattering procedure. The packing condition
(T5)
means that the shattering does not occur too often in
\mathcal{T}
.

In the construction above instead of
(S2)
it is tempting to consider a simpler condition:

(S2’)

if
Q\in\mathcal{T}
then there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap Q
with
J_{Q}\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
.

It is easy to see that any cube satisfying
(S2’)
also satisfies
(S2)
, see Lemma
8.2
. Moreover, the tree obtained using
(S1)
and
(S2’)
would satisfy all the properties
(T1)
–
(T8)
except for
(T5)
. Unfortunately, the packing condition for
\mathsf{Roots}
is crucial to our arguments later on, and the condition
(S2’)
is too restrictive for this to hold – shattering procedure induced by the failure of
(S2’)
may occur arbitrarily many times. This forces us to work with the more cumbersome condition
(S2)
, although
(S2’)
is still used within the shattering procedure itself.

7.1.
Construction of
\mathcal{T}

We define families
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathsf{Roots}\subset\mathcal{T}
one generation at a time.

First, let
m_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
be the unique integer satisfying

\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\rho^{2}<5\rho^{m_{0}}\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\rho.

We plug
k=0,

J=J_{P}=J_{0}
,
\mathcal{P}=\mathbb{D}_{m_{0}}
and
l=0
into Lemma
6.4
to get a family
\mathbb{D}_{0}(E,J_{0})
forming a partition of
E
. This can be seen as the cubes on
E
of
0
-th generation relative to the metric
d_{J_{0}}
. We set

\mathcal{T}_{0}\coloneqq\{Q\in\mathbb{D}_{0}(E,J_{0})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}E_{0}\cap Q\neq\varnothing\},

and
\mathcal{T}_{0}\cap\mathsf{Roots}\coloneqq\mathcal{T}_{0}
. For all
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
set
J_{Q}=J_{0}
.

Now assume that

\mathcal{T}^{k}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i=0}^{k}\mathcal{T}_{i}

and
\mathsf{Roots}\cap\mathcal{T}^{k}
have already been defined, and that every
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
is a union of cubes from
\mathbb{D}_{m(Q)}
, where
m(Q)
satisfies
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\rho^{k+2}<5\rho^{m(Q)}\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\rho^{k+1}
. For every
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
we will define its
\mathcal{T}
-children, denoted by
\mathsf{Ch}(Q,\mathcal{T}),
and then we will set
\mathcal{T}_{k+1}=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}}\mathsf{Ch}(Q,\mathcal{T})
. Every
P\in\mathsf{Ch}(Q,\mathcal{T})
will satisfy
P\subset Q
and
J_{P}\subset J_{Q}
.

Fix
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
, and let
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathbb{D}_{m(Q)}
be such that
Q=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{Q}}S
. Consider the family
\mathsf{Ch}(Q,J_{Q})=\mathbb{D}_{k+1}(Q,J_{Q})
obtained from Lemma
6.4
by plugging in
k=k,

J_{P}=J=J_{Q},

\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{Q},
and
l=1
. For every
P\in\mathsf{Ch}(Q,J_{Q})
we set
J_{P}=J_{Q}
. We divide
\mathsf{Ch}(Q,J_{Q})
into three subfamilies.

•

If
P\in\mathsf{Ch}(Q,J_{Q})
fails
(S1)
, that is,

P\cap E_{0}=\varnothing,\quad\text{or}\quad G_{2}(x)\cap J_{P}=\varnothing\quad\text{for all $x\in P\cap E_{0}$,}

then we write
P\in\mathsf{End}_{0}(Q)
.

•

If
P\in\mathsf{Ch}(Q,J_{Q})\setminus\mathsf{End}_{0}(Q)
fails
(S2)
, that is,

\int_{P}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P}\cap G(x,k+1))\,d\mu(x)<(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})\mu(P),

(7.2)

then we write
P\in\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(Q)
.

•

If
P\in\mathsf{Ch}(Q,J_{Q})\setminus\mathsf{End}_{0}(Q)
satisfies
(S2)
, so that

\int_{P}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P}\cap G(x,k+1))\,d\mu(x)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})\mu(P),

(7.3)

then we write
P\in\mathsf{Good}_{0}(Q)
.

The cubes from
\mathsf{Good}_{0}(Q)
will be added to
\mathcal{T}_{k+1}
and
\mathsf{Ch}(Q,\mathcal{T})
. The cubes from
\mathsf{End}_{0}(Q)
are not added to
\mathcal{T}
, this is where the tree ends. Finally, the cubes from
\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(Q)
require further processing – they need to be shattered.

7.2.
Shattering procedure

The shattering procedure is defined inductively. Assume that
\mathsf{Good}_{j}(Q)
,
\mathsf{End}_{j}(Q)
and
\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q)
have already been defined for some
j\geq 0
, and if
j\geq 1
then every
S\in\mathsf{Good}_{j}(Q)\cup\mathsf{End}_{j}(Q)\cup\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q)
satisfies
S\in\mathsf{Sh}(S^{\prime},J_{S})
for some
S^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{j-1}(Q)
and
J_{S}\in\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(J_{S^{\prime}})
; for
j=0
we have
S\in\mathsf{Ch}(Q,J_{Q})
and
J_{S}=J_{Q}
.

Assume further that they satisfy the following:

•

If
S\in\mathsf{End}_{j}(Q)
, then it fails
(S1)
: either
S\cap E_{0}=\varnothing
, or for all
x\in S\cap E_{0}
we have
G_{2}(x)\cap J_{S}=\varnothing
.

•

If
S\in\mathsf{Good}_{j}(Q)
and
j\geq 1
, then it satisfies
(S2’)
, that is, there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap S
with
J_{S}\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
.

•

If
S\in\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q)
, then it satisfies
(S1)
but fails
(S2’)
, that is,
S\notin\mathsf{Good}_{j}(Q)
and there exists
x\in S\cap E_{0}
with
G_{2}(x)\cap J_{S}\neq\varnothing
.

If
\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q)=\varnothing
, there is nothing to do and the shattering procedure ends. Suppose that
\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q)\neq\varnothing
, and let
S\in\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q)
.

Let
J\in\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(J_{S})
. We are going to partition the family
\mathsf{Sh}(S,J)=\mathbb{D}_{k+1}(S,J)
obtained using Lemma
6.4
. Fix
P\in\mathsf{Sh}(S,J)
and set
J_{P}\coloneqq J
. There are 3 possible outcomes:

•

If
P
fails
(S1)
, i.e.
P\cap E_{0}=\varnothing
or for all
x\in P\cap E_{0}
we have
G_{2}(x)\cap J_{P}=\varnothing
, then we write
P\in\mathsf{End}_{*}(S,J)
.

•

If
P
satisfies
(S2’)
, i.e. there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap P
with
J_{P}\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
, then we write
P\in\mathsf{Good}_{*}(S,J)
.

•

If
P
satisfies
(S1)
but fails
(S2’)
, i.e.
P\notin\mathsf{Good}_{*}(S,J)
and there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap P
with
J_{P}\cap G_{2}(x)\neq\varnothing
, then we write
P\in\mathsf{Sh}_{*}(S,J)
.

Finally, we define

\displaystyle\mathsf{Good}_{j+1}(Q)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{S\in\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q)}\bigcup_{J\in\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(J_{S})}\mathsf{Good}_{*}(S,J),

\displaystyle\mathsf{End}_{j+1}(Q)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{S\in\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q)}\bigcup_{J\in\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(J_{S})}\mathsf{End}_{*}(S,J),

\displaystyle\mathsf{Sh}_{j+1}(Q)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{S\in\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q)}\bigcup_{J\in\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(J_{S})}\mathsf{Sh}_{*}(S,J).

This completes the description of the shattering procedure.

Remark 7.5
.

It is not difficult to see that
\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q)=\varnothing
for
j
large enough. In other words, the shattering procedure ends after a finite number of “shatterings”, and our algorithm never gets stuck in an infinite loop.

Indeed, in Remark
5.2
we assumed that for every
x\in E_{0}
and
I\in\mathcal{G}_{1}(x)
we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\geq 3^{-N}
for some huge
N\geq 1
. This implies that for every
I\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\geq 3^{-N-1}
. At the same time, for every
P\in\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q)
we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})=3^{-j}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})
because
J_{P}\subset J_{Q}
is
j
generations of triadic intervals below
J_{Q}
. It follows that
\mathsf{Sh}_{N+1}(Q)=\varnothing
.

We define

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ch}(Q,\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{j\geq 0}\mathsf{Good}_{j}(Q),

\displaystyle\mathsf{Roots}(Q)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{j\geq 1}\mathsf{Good}_{j}(Q).

Note that
\mathsf{Roots}(Q)
are preciesly the
\mathcal{T}
-children of
Q
that arise from the shattering procedure. Finally, we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{k+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}}\mathsf{Ch}(Q,\mathcal{T}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{k+1}\cap\mathsf{Roots}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}}\mathsf{Roots}(Q),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{T}_{k},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Roots}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{T}_{k}\cap\mathsf{Roots}.

This completes the construction of
\mathcal{T}
.

We define an auxiliary collection
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
consisting of all the sets appearing in the construction above:
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}=\mathcal{T}_{0}
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{k+1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}}\bigcup_{j\geq 0}\mathsf{Good}_{j}(Q)\cup\mathsf{End}_{j}(Q)\cup\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{k}

Remark 7.6
.

It follows directly from the construction above that for every
k\geq 1
and
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
there exists
P\in\mathcal{T}_{k-1}
and
j\geq 0
such that
Q\in\mathsf{Good}_{j}(P)
. Moreover, if
j=0
then
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})
and
J_{Q}=J_{P}
, and if
j\geq 1
, then there exists a sequence of cubes
S_{0},\dots,S_{j-1}
with
S_{i}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{i}(P)
and triadic intervals
J_{0},\dots,J_{j-1}
with
J_{i}=J_{S_{i}}
such that

•

J_{0}=J_{P}
and
S_{0}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P)\subset\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})
,

•

J_{i+1}\in\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(J_{i})
and
S_{i+1}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{*}(S_{i},J_{i+1})\subset\mathsf{Sh}(S_{i},J_{i+1})

•

J_{Q}\in\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(J_{j-1})
and
Q\in\mathsf{Good}_{*}(S_{j-1},J_{Q})\subset\mathsf{Sh}(S_{j-1},J_{Q})
.

8.
Properties of the tree

In this section we establish properties
(T1)
–
(T8)
stated in Proposition
7.3
.

8.1.
Basic properties of
\mathcal{T}

For each
k\geq 0
and
Q\in{\mathcal{T}}_{k}
we set

\ell(Q)=\rho^{k}

and

B_{Q}\coloneqq B_{J_{Q}}(x_{Q},4\ell(Q)).

Lemma 8.1
.

Property
(T1)
holds: for each
Q\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
there is
x_{Q}\in Q
and
J_{Q}\in\Delta(J_{0})
such that

B(x_{Q},\rho^{3}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\ell(Q))\cap E\subset Q\subset B_{Q}\cap E,

Proof.

Let
Q\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{k}
. By the construction, we have
Q\in\mathbb{D}_{k}(P,J_{Q})
for some subset
P\subset E
, and
Q
is a union of cubes from a family
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathbb{D}_{m(Q)}
, where
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\rho^{k+2}\leq 5\rho^{m(Q)}\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\rho^{k+1}
. By Lemma
6.4
, there exists
S\in\mathcal{Q}
such that for
x_{Q}\coloneqq x_{S}
we have

B_{J_{Q}}(x_{Q},0.5\rho^{k})\cap P\subset Q\subset B_{J_{Q}}(x_{Q},4\rho^{k})\cap P.

Since
B_{J_{Q}}(x_{Q},4\rho^{k})\cap P\subset B_{Q}\cap E
, we get
Q\subset B_{Q}\cap E
.

On the other hand, by Lemma
6.1
c) we have
E\cap B(x_{S},\rho^{m(Q)})\subset E\cap B(S)\subset S
. Since
x_{S}=x_{Q}
and

\rho^{3}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\ell(Q)=\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\rho^{k+3}\leq 5\rho^{m(Q)+1}\leq\rho^{m(Q)},

we get
B(x_{Q},\rho^{3}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\ell(Q))\cap E\subset S\subset Q
. ∎

Lemma 8.2
.

For every
Q\in\mathcal{T}
we have
(S1)
: there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap Q
with
J_{Q}\cap G_{2}(x)\neq\varnothing
.

Moreover, if
Q\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{k}
satisfies
(S2’)
, i.e. there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap Q
with
J_{Q}\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
, then
J_{Q}\subset G(y,k)
for all
y\in Q
. In particular, it satisfies
(S2)
.

Proof.

The fact that there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap Q
with
J_{Q}\cap G_{2}(x)\neq\varnothing
follows immediately from the construction because no cube failing
(S1)
was added to
\mathcal{T}
.

Now suppose
Q\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{k}
and there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap Q
with
J_{Q}\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
. By the definition of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(y,k)
, for all
y\in E\cap B_{J_{Q}}(x,10\rho^{k})
we have
J_{Q}\in\mathcal{G}^{\prime}(y,k)
, and in particular
J_{Q}\subset G(y,k)
. Using Lemma
8.1
and the triangle inequality, we have

Q\subset B_{Q}\cap E=B_{J_{Q}}(x_{Q},4\rho^{k})\cap E\subset B_{J_{Q}}(x,10\rho^{k})\cap E,

and so
J_{Q}\subset G(y,k)
for all
y\in Q
.
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

Property
(T2)
holds: for every
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k},\ k\geq 1
, and

\int_{Q}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}\cap G(x,k))\,d\mu(x)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q).

(8.1)

Proof.

By the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{k}
we have
Q\in\mathsf{Good}_{j}(P)
for some
j\geq 0
and
P\in\mathcal{T}_{k-1}
. If
j=0
, then (
8.1
) follows immediately from the property
(S2)

defining
\mathsf{Good}_{0}(P)
.

If
j\geq 1
, then
Q
satisfies
(S2’)
and this is even stronger than (
8.1
) by Lemma
8.2
.
∎

The following lemma prepares ground for establishing
(T3)
and
(T4)
.

Lemma 8.4
.

If
P\in\mathcal{T}
and
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
, then
Q\subset P
and
J_{Q}\subset J_{P}
. If
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})\cap\mathsf{Roots}
, then
J_{Q}\subsetneq J_{P}
.

Moreover, if
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
satisfy
Q\neq Q^{\prime}
, then either
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\varnothing
or
J_{Q}\cap J_{Q^{\prime}}=\varnothing
.

Proof.

Let
j\geq 0
be such that
Q\in\mathsf{Good}_{j}(P)
. If
j=0
, then
J_{Q}=J_{P}
and
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})
so that
Q\subset P
.

If
j\geq 1
(which happens if and only if
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})\cap\mathsf{Roots}
), let
S_{i}
and
J_{i}
be as in Remark
7.6
. Clearly, we have
J_{Q}\subsetneq J_{j-1}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq J_{0}=J_{P}
. At the same time,
Q\in\mathsf{Sh}(S_{j-1},J_{Q})
,
S_{i+1}\in\mathsf{Sh}(S_{i},J_{i+1})
, and
S_{0}\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})
. This implies

Q\subset S_{j-1}\subset\dots\subset S_{0}\subset P,

and so we get the first assertion of the lemma.

Now suppose that
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
satisfy
Q\neq Q^{\prime}
. If
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Good}_{0}(P)
, then we have
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\varnothing
because they are distinct cubes from
\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})
. If
Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Good}_{0}(P)
and
Q\in\mathsf{Good}_{j}(P)
for
j\geq 1
, then
Q\subset S_{0}
for some
S_{0}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P)
. Since
Q^{\prime}
and
S_{0}
are distinct cubes from
\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})
, we also get
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\varnothing
.

Finally, suppose that
Q\in\mathsf{Good}_{j}(P)
and
Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Good}_{j^{\prime}}(P)
for some
j,j^{\prime}\geq 1
, and without loss of generality assume
j\geq j^{\prime}
. If
J_{Q}\cap J_{Q^{\prime}}=\varnothing
, we are done, so assume the contrary. Since both
J_{Q}
and
J_{Q^{\prime}}
are triadic intervals, and
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})=3^{-j}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P}),

\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q^{\prime}})=3^{-j^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P}),
this implies
J_{Q}\subset J_{Q^{\prime}}
. Let
S_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{i}(P)
be the sequence of cubes associated to
Q^{\prime}
given by Remark
7.6
. Since
J_{Q}\subset J_{Q^{\prime}}
, and the intervals
\{J_{S_{i}}\}
and
\{J_{S_{i}^{\prime}}\}
are uniquely defined by
J_{Q}
and
J_{Q^{\prime}}
, respectively, we get that
J_{S_{i}}=J_{S_{i}^{\prime}}
for all
0\leq i\leq j^{\prime}-1
.

If
S_{i}=S_{i}^{\prime}
for all
0\leq i\leq j^{\prime}-1
, then we get that either

•

j=j^{\prime}
and
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Good}_{*}(S_{j^{\prime}-1},J_{Q^{\prime}})
, in which case
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\varnothing
because they are distinct cubes from
\mathsf{Sh}(S_{j^{\prime}-1},J_{Q^{\prime}})
,

•

j>j^{\prime}
,
Q^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Good}_{*}(S_{j^{\prime}-1},J_{Q^{\prime}})
, and
S_{j^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{*}(S_{j^{\prime}-1},J_{Q^{\prime}})
, in which case
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\varnothing
because
Q\subset S_{j^{\prime}}
and
Q^{\prime},S_{j^{\prime}}
are distinct cubes from
\mathsf{Sh}(S_{j^{\prime}-1},J_{Q^{\prime}})
.

Finally, suppose that there exists
0\leq i\leq j^{\prime}-1
with
S_{i}\neq S_{i}^{\prime}
, and let
i
be the smallest such integer. We have that either

•

i=0
, in which case
S_{0},S_{0}^{\prime}
are distinct cubes from
\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})
. Hence,
S_{0}\cap S_{0}^{\prime}=\varnothing
, and since
Q\subset S_{0}
and
Q^{\prime}\subset S_{0}^{\prime}
, we get
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\varnothing
.

•

i>0
, in which case
S_{i},S_{i}^{\prime}
are distinct cubes from
\mathsf{Sh}(S_{i-1},J_{S_{i}})
. But this again implies
S_{i}\cap S_{i}^{\prime}=\varnothing
, so that
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\varnothing
.

Since we have covered all the cases, this shows that
J_{Q}\cap J_{Q^{\prime}}\neq\varnothing
implies
Q\cap Q^{\prime}=\varnothing
.
∎

Lemma 8.5
.

Properties
(T3)
and
(T4)
hold: for every
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
,
k\geq l\geq 0
, there is a unique
P\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
such that
Q\subset P
and
J_{Q}\subset J_{P}
. For every other
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
we have either
Q\cap P^{\prime}=\varnothing
or
J_{Q}\cap J_{P^{\prime}}=\varnothing
.

Proof.

First, we cover the case
k=l
. We only need to show that if
P,Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
,
Q\cap P\neq\varnothing
and
J_{Q}\cap J_{P}\neq\varnothing
, then
Q=P
. If
k=0
, then this is clear because
\mathcal{T}_{0}\subset\mathbb{D}_{0}(E,J_{0})
. Now suppose that
k\geq 1
, and the claim is already known for
k-1
. Suppose that distinct cubes
P,Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
satisfy
Q\cap P\neq\varnothing
and
J_{Q}\cap J_{P}\neq\varnothing
. Let
Q^{\prime},P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{k-1}
be such that
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(Q^{\prime},\mathcal{T})
and
P\in\mathsf{Ch}(P^{\prime},\mathcal{T})
. By Lemma
8.4
, we get that
Q^{\prime}\neq P^{\prime}
. But now, by the inductive assumption, we have either
Q^{\prime}\cap P^{\prime}=\varnothing
or
J_{Q^{\prime}}\cap J_{P^{\prime}}=\varnothing
. By Lemma
8.4
we have
Q\subset Q^{\prime},

J_{Q}\subset J_{Q^{\prime}}
and
P\subset P^{\prime},

J_{P}\subset J_{P^{\prime}}
, and so either
Q\cap P=\varnothing
or
J_{Q}\cap J_{P}=\varnothing
. This finishes the proof in the case
k=l
.

Now suppose that
k\geq l\geq 0
. It suffices to prove the lemma for
k=l+1
, and the general claim follows by induction.

If
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{l+1}
, then there exists
P\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
such that
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
. By Lemma
8.4
we have
Q\subset P
and
J_{Q}\subset J_{P}
. Now suppose that
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
is different from
P
. By the first part of the proof (case
k=l
), we have either
P\cap P^{\prime}=\varnothing
, or
J_{P}\cap J_{P^{\prime}}=\varnothing
. Hence, we get
Q\cap P^{\prime}=\varnothing
or
J_{Q}\cap J_{P^{\prime}}=\varnothing
. This finishes the proof.
∎

We introduce the following notation: for every
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k},k\geq 1
we denote by
Q^{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{k-1}
its
\mathcal{T}
-parent: the unique cube from
\mathcal{T}_{k-1}
such that
Q\prec Q^{1}
. Note that
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(Q^{1},\mathcal{T})
. This is well-defined by
(T3)
.

It will be convenient to have the following strengthening of the property
(S1)
.

Lemma 8.6
.

For every
Q\in\mathcal{T}
there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap Q
and
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
with
J\subset J_{Q}
.

Proof.

If
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
we have
J_{Q}=J_{0}
and the result is trivial. Suppose
Q\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{T}_{0}
, and let
P\in\mathcal{T}
be such that
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
. If
Q\in\mathsf{Good}_{j}(P)
with
j\geq 1
, then by the construction
Q
satisfies
(S2’)
so that there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap Q
with
J_{Q}\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
, and we are done. Assume that
Q\in\mathsf{Good}_{0}(P)
.

We know by property
(S1)
that there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap Q
with
J_{Q}\cap G_{2}(x)\neq\varnothing
. If there exists
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
with
J\subset J_{Q}
, then we are done. Otherwise, we get that there exists
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
with
J_{Q}\subsetneq J
(because
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)\subset\Delta(J_{0})
and
J_{Q}\in\Delta(J_{0})
). We will show that this leads to a contradiction.

Let
k\geq 1
be such that
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
, and denote by
Q_{i}\in\mathcal{T}_{i}
the unique cube such that
Q\prec Q_{i}
. This is well defined by
(T3)
. Since
J\subset J_{0}=J_{Q_{0}}
and
J_{Q}=J_{Q_{k}}\subsetneq J
, we can find a cube
Q_{i}
such that

J_{Q_{i}}\subsetneq J\subset J_{Q_{i-1}}.

It follows that
Q_{i}
was defined using the shattering procedure. Since
J_{Q_{i}}\subsetneq J\subset J_{Q_{i-1}}
, there exists some
j\geq 0
and
S\in\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q_{i-1})
such that
J_{S}=J
and
Q_{i}\prec S
. Recall that

x\in Q\cap E_{0}\subset Q_{i}\cap E_{0}\subset S\cap E_{0}

and
J_{S}=J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
. But this implies
S\in\mathsf{Good}_{j}(Q_{i-1})
(for
j\geq 1
directly by the definition of
\mathsf{Good}_{j}(Q_{i-1})
, for
j=0
by the second half of Lemma
8.2
). This contradicts the fact that
S\in\mathsf{Sh}_{j}(Q_{i-1})
.
∎

We will need the following measure estimates for
Q\in\mathcal{T}
.

Lemma 8.7
.

For every
Q\in\mathcal{T}
and
C\geq 1
we have

\mu(CB_{Q})\lesssim CM\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\ell(Q)

(8.2)

and

\mu(Q)\gtrsim A^{-1}\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\ell(Q).

(8.3)

In particular,

\mu(CB_{Q})\lesssim CAM\,\mu(Q).

(8.4)

Proof.

Suppose that
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
. By Lemma
8.6
there exists
x\in Q\cap E_{0}
and
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
such that
J\subset J_{Q}
.

By Lemma
5.3
, there exists
\theta\in 3J
such that
\mu_{\theta}^{\perp}(y)\leq M
. Since
J\subset J_{Q}
, we also have
\theta\in 3J_{Q}
. Since
x\in B_{Q}
,
we have
CB_{Q}\subset B_{J_{Q}}(x,5C\ell(Q))
.
Lemma
3.12
gives

\mu(CB_{Q})\leq\mu(B_{J_{Q}}(x,5C\ell(Q)))\lesssim CM\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\ell(Q)

This concludes the proof of (
8.2
).

Concerning (
8.3
), by
(T1)
and lower Ahlfors regularity of
E
we have

\mu(Q)\geq\mu(B(x_{Q},\rho^{3}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\ell(Q))\cap E)\gtrsim A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\ell(Q).

∎

8.2.
Coronization of
\mathcal{T}

The properties
(T5)
and
(T6)
can be seen as a “coronization” of
\mathcal{T}
, in the spirit of
[
DS91
]
. We prove them in this subsection.

Given
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
, let
\mathsf{Next}(R)
denote the family of cubes
P\in\mathsf{Roots}\setminus\{R\}
such that
P\prec R
and there is no other
S\in\mathsf{Roots}\setminus\{P,R\}
satisfying
P\prec S\prec R
. We can decompose
\mathsf{Roots}
into “layers”: set
\mathsf{Roots}_{0}=\mathcal{T}_{0}
, and then inductively

\mathsf{Roots}_{k+1}=\bigcup_{P\in\mathsf{Roots}_{k}}\mathsf{Next}(P),

(8.5)

so that

\mathsf{Roots}=\bigcup_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathsf{Roots}_{k},

(8.6)

and the union above is disjoint.

Given
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
denote by
\mathcal{T}(R)
the cubes
Q\in\mathcal{T}
such that
Q\prec R
and there is no
P\in\mathsf{Next}(R)
such that
Q\prec P\prec R
. In particular,
\mathsf{Next}(R)
is not part of
\mathcal{T}(R)
. We get a decomposition

\mathcal{T}=\bigcup_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}}\mathcal{T}(R),

(8.7)

where the union above is disjoint.

Lemma 8.8
.

The property
(T6)
holds: if
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
then

\mathcal{T}(R)=\{Q\in\mathcal{T}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,Q\prec R,\,J_{Q}=J_{R}\}.

(8.8)

Proof.

We begin by proving “
\subset
” in (
8.8
). Suppose that
Q\in\mathcal{T}(R)
. We need to show that
J_{Q}=J_{R}
. If
Q=R
then there is nothing to prove, so suppose that
Q\neq R
. Assume that
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
, and let
Q=Q^{0}\prec Q^{1}\prec\dots\prec Q^{l}=R
be the sequence of cubes such that
Q^{i}\in\mathcal{T}_{k-i}
(it exists and it is unique by
(T3)
). Since none of the
Q^{0},\dots,Q^{l-1}
belong to
\mathsf{Roots}
, it follows directly from the definition of the family
\mathsf{Roots}
that
Q^{i}\in\mathsf{Good}_{0}(Q^{i+1})
for all
i\in\{0,\dots,l-1\}
. But going back to the definition of
\mathsf{Good}_{0}(Q^{i+1})
, we see that
J_{Q^{i}}=J_{R}
. This proves
\mathcal{T}(R)\subset\{Q\in\mathcal{T}\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,Q\prec R,\,J_{Q}=J_{R}\}
.

Now assume that
Q\prec R
and
J_{Q}=J_{R}
. If
Q\notin\mathcal{T}(R)
, then there exists
R^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Roots}\setminus\{R\}
with
Q\prec R^{\prime}\prec R
, and in particular
J_{Q}\subset J_{R^{\prime}}\subset J_{R}
. Since
J_{Q}=J_{R}
, we get

J_{R^{\prime}}=J_{R}.

(8.9)

Let
P\in\mathcal{T}
be such that
R^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
, so that
R^{\prime}\prec P\prec R
(it may happen that
P=R
). Since
R\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})\cap\mathsf{Roots}
, by Lemma
8.4
we have
J_{R}\subsetneq J_{P}\subset J_{R}
, but this contradicts (
8.9
). Hence,
Q\in\mathcal{T}(R)
.
∎

Observe that since all
Q\in\mathcal{T}(R)
satisfy
J_{Q}=J_{R}
, the family
\mathcal{T}(R)
is a tree of generalized dyadic cubes in the “usual sense”, with partial order given by the inclusion relation.

For
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
we define

\mathcal{S}(R)\coloneqq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(R)}\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(Q)\cup\mathsf{End}_{0}(Q).

(8.10)

Observe that for every
P\in\mathcal{S}(R)
there exists
P^{1}\in\mathcal{T}(R)
such that
P\in\mathsf{Ch}(P^{1},J_{R})
, but
P\notin\mathcal{T}(R)
. Note also that
\mathcal{S}(R)\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}\setminus\mathcal{T}
.

We may further divide

\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{Sh}}(R)\coloneqq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(R)}\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(Q)

(8.11)

and

\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{End}}(R)\coloneqq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(R)}\mathsf{End}_{0}(Q),

so that
\mathcal{S}(R)=\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{Sh}}(R)\cup\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{End}}(R)
. It follows from the definitions of
\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(Q)
and
\mathsf{End}_{0}(Q)
that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{Sh}}(R)\cap\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{End}}(R)=\varnothing
.

Lemma 8.9
.

The cubes in
\mathcal{S}(R)
are pairwise disjoint, and we have

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{S}(R)}\mu(Q)\leq\mu(R).

(8.12)

Proof.

Suppose that
Q,P\in\mathcal{S}(R)
,
Q\neq P
, and
Q\cap P\neq\varnothing
. Let
Q^{1},P^{1}\in\mathcal{T}(R)
be such that
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(Q^{1},J_{R})
and
P\in\mathsf{Ch}(P^{1},J_{R})
. Then,
Q^{1}\cap P^{1}\neq\varnothing
and by
(T4)
we have either
Q^{1}\subset P^{1}
or
P^{1}\subset Q^{1}
; without loss of generality assume the former. Then, there exists
\widetilde{Q}\in\mathsf{Good}_{0}(P^{1})
such that
Q^{1}\subset\widetilde{Q}
. At the same time, since
P\in\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P^{1})\cup\mathsf{End}_{0}(P^{1})
and
\mathsf{Good}_{0}(P^{1}),

\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P^{1}),

\mathsf{End}_{0}(P^{1})
are disjoint subfamilies of
\mathsf{Ch}(P^{1},J_{R})
, we get
P\cap\widetilde{Q}=\varnothing
, which is a contradiction with
P\cap Q\neq\varnothing
. This shows that the cubes in
\mathcal{S}(R)
are pairwise disjoint.

The estimate (
8.12
) follows from the disjointedness and the fact that
Q\subset R
for all
Q\in\mathcal{S}(R)
.
∎

We move on to the proof of the packing estimate for
\mathsf{Roots}
, that is, property
(T5)
. We start with some preliminary lemmas.

Lemma 8.10
.

If
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
, then

\mathsf{Next}(R)=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{Sh}}(R)}\{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}(S^{1})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Q\subset S\},

and the union above is disjoint.

Proof.

First, let us show that if
P\in\mathcal{T}
, then

\mathsf{Roots}(P)=\bigcup_{S_{0}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P)}\{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Q\subset S_{0}\},

(8.13)

and the unions above are disjoint.

Recall that
\mathsf{Roots}(P)=\bigcup_{j\geq 1}\mathsf{Good}_{j}(P)=\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})\setminus\mathsf{Good}_{0}(P)
. Recalling Remark
7.6
, for every
Q\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)
there is a unique sequence of cubes
Q\prec S_{k}\prec\dots\prec S_{0}
such that
S_{i}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{i}(P)
and
Q\in\mathsf{Good}_{*}(S_{k},J_{Q})
. Since
\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P)
consists of pairwise disjoint cubes, we get (
8.13
).

Observe that
\mathsf{Next}(R)=\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{T}(R)}\mathsf{Roots}(P),
and that
\mathsf{Roots}(P)
is non-empty if and only if
\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P)
is non-empty. Note also that if
S\in\mathsf{End}_{0}(P)
, then
\{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Q\subset S\}=\varnothing.
Recalling (
8.11
) we get

\mathsf{Next}(R)=\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{T}(R)}\bigcup_{S_{0}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P)}\{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Q\subset S_{0}\}\\
=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{Sh}}(R)}\{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}(S^{1})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Q\subset S\}.

∎

The following estimate is key to obtaining
(T5)
.

Lemma 8.11
.

If
P\in\mathcal{T}
and
S_{0}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P)
, then

\sum_{S\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\subset S_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{S})\mu(S)\leq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})\mu(S_{0}).

(8.14)

Proof.

Fix
S_{0}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P)
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
P\in\mathcal{T}_{k-1}
, so that for all
S\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)
we have
S\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
.
By the construction, for every
S\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)
we have
(S2’)
: there exists
x\in E_{0}\cap S
with
J_{S}\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
. Then, by Lemma
8.2
for all
y\in S
we have
J_{S}\subset G(y,k)
. It follows that

\sum_{S\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\subset S_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{S})\mu(S)=\sum_{S\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\subset S_{0}}\int_{S}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{S}\cap G(y,k))\,d\mu(y)\\
=\sum_{S\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\subset S_{0}}\int_{S}\int_{J_{S}}\mathds{1}_{G(y,k)}(t)\,dt\,d\mu(y).

Observe that Lemma
8.4
can be rephrased by saying that
J_{Q}\times Q\subset J_{P}\times P
for all
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
, and
\{J_{Q}\times Q\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})\}
is a family of pairwise disjoint sets. Since
\mathsf{Roots}(P)\subset\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
, the same is true for
\{J_{S}\times S\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ S\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)\}
. Recalling also that
J_{S_{0}}=J_{P}
, and that cubes from
\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P)
fail
(S2)
, we get

\sum_{S\in\mathsf{Roots}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S\subset S_{0}}\int_{S}\int_{J_{S}}\mathds{1}_{G(y,k)}(t)\,dt\,d\mu(y)\leq\int_{S_{0}}\int_{J_{P}}\mathds{1}_{G(y,k)}(t)\,dt\,d\mu(y)\\
=\int_{S_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P}\cap G(y,k))\,d\mu(y)\overset{\eqref{eq:shatter}}{<}(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})\mu(S_{0}).

∎

Lemma 8.12
.

For every
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
we have

\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Next}(R)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})\mu(P)\leq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\mu(R).

(8.15)

Proof.

By Lemma
8.10
,

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Next}(R)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})\mu(P)=

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{Sh}}(R)}\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Roots}(S^{1})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}P\subset S}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})\mu(P)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(R)}\sum_{S\in\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(Q)}\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Roots}(Q)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}P\subset S}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})\mu(P)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:sh-decrease}}{\leq}

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(R)}\sum_{S\in\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(Q)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(S)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{Sh}}(R)}\mu(S)\leq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\mu(R),

where in the last line we used the fact that
J_{Q}=J_{R}
for
Q\in\mathcal{T}(R)
and that cubes from
\mathcal{S}(R)
are pairwise disjoint.
∎

Lemma 8.13
.

Property
(T5)
holds: the family
\mathsf{Roots}
satisfies a packing estimate

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E).

(8.16)

Proof.

Recall that we had a decomposition of
\mathsf{Roots}
into layers
\mathsf{Roots}_{k}
, see (
8.5
) and (
8.6
). It follows that

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}_{k}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)\\
=\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)+\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}_{k}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q).

If
k\geq 1
then

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}_{k}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)=\sum_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}_{k-1}}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Next}(R)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)\overset{\eqref{eq:sh-decr}}{\leq}(1-\varepsilon)\sum_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}_{k-1}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\mu(R).

Iterating this estimate, we get

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}_{k}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)\leq(1-\varepsilon)^{k}\sum_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\mu(R).

Recalling that
\mathsf{Roots}_{0}=\mathcal{T}_{0}\subset\mathbb{D}_{0}(E,J_{0})
, and that the cubes
R\in\mathbb{D}_{0}(E,J_{0})
are pairwise disjoint with
J_{R}=J_{0}
, we finally arrive at

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Roots}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}(1-\varepsilon)^{k}\sum_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\mu(R)\\
\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)\sum_{k\geq 0}(1-\varepsilon)^{k}=\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E).

∎

8.3.
Relating
\mathcal{T}
with good directions

In this subsection we prove
(T7)
and
(T8)
, which show how the tree
\mathcal{T}
relates to the good directions.

Lemma 8.14
.

Property
(T7)
holds: for every
x\in E_{0}
,
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
, and
k\geq 0
, there is a unique
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
such that
x\in Q
and
J\subset J_{Q}
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in E_{0}
and
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
. Observe that by
(T4)
there exists at most one
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
such that
x\in Q
and
J\subset J_{Q}
. Thus, we only need to prove the existence of such
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
for every
k\geq 0
.

We prove the lemma by induction on
k
. First, observe that for
k=0
the claim is certainly true: since
\mathcal{T}_{0}\subset\mathbb{D}_{0}(E,J_{0})
consists of all the cubes in
\mathbb{D}_{0}(E,J_{0})
intersecting
E_{0}
, there exists a unique cube
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
that contains
x
, and it satisfies
J_{Q}=J_{0}\supset J
(recall that
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)\subset\Delta(J_{0})
).

Now suppose that the lemma is true for
k-1
, so that there exists
P\in\mathcal{T}_{k-1}
with
x\in P
and
J\subset J_{P}
. We will find
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
such that
J\subset J_{Q}
. This will close the induction because
\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})\subset\mathcal{T}_{k}
.

We need to trace the algorithm defining
\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
. First, we consider the cubes in
\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})
. Since
\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})
is a partition of
P
, there exists a unique
S_{0}\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,J_{P})
such that
x\in S_{0}
. There are three possibilities.

If
S_{0}\in\mathsf{Good}_{0}(P)
, then
S_{0}\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})\subset\mathcal{T}_{k}
and
J_{S_{0}}=J_{P}\supset J
, so we can take
Q=S_{0}
and we are done.

If
S_{0}\in\mathsf{End}_{0}(P)
, then it fails
(S1)
: either
S_{0}\cap E_{0}=\varnothing
, or
G_{2}(x)\cap J_{P}=\varnothing
. The former is false because
x\in S_{0}\cap E_{0}
. The latter is false because
J\subset G_{2}(x)\cap J_{P}
. We conclude that
S_{0}\notin\mathsf{End}_{0}(P)
.

The remaining alternative is that
S_{0}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{0}(P)
. We claim that this implies
J\subsetneq J_{P}
. Indeed, if
J=J_{P}
, then by Lemma
8.2
we have
J_{P}=J_{S_{0}}\subset G(y,k)
for all
y\in S_{0}
(recall that
x\in S_{0}\cap E_{0}
and
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
). But this implies
(S2)
:

\int_{S_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{S_{0}}\cap G(y,k))\,d\mu(y)=\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{S_{0}})\mu(S_{0})\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{S_{0}})\mu(S_{0}),

and so
S_{0}\in\mathsf{Good}_{0}(P)
, which is a contradiction. Thus,
J\subsetneq J_{P}
.

Since
J
and
J_{P}
are triadic intervals and
J\subsetneq J_{P}
, we get that there is a unique
J_{1}\in\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(J_{P})
such that
J\subset J_{1}
. We partition
S_{0}
into
\mathsf{Sh}(S_{0},J_{1})
, so that there is a unique
S_{1}\in\mathsf{Sh}(S_{0},J_{1})
such that
x\in S_{1}
. As before, there are three cases to consider.

If
S_{1}\in\mathsf{Good}_{*}(S_{0},J_{1})
, then
S_{1}\in\mathsf{Good}_{1}(P)\subset\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
, and we may take
Q=S_{1}
because
x\in S_{1}
and
J\subset J_{1}=J_{S_{1}}
.

If
S_{1}\in\mathsf{End}_{*}(S_{0},J_{1})
, then either
S_{1}\cap E_{0}=\varnothing
, or
G_{2}(x)\cap J_{1}=\varnothing
; both are false, so this case does not occur.

Finally, it may happen that
S_{1}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{*}(S_{0},J_{1})
. In this case we have
J\subsetneq J_{1}
because
J=J_{1}
would imply
S_{1}\in\mathsf{Good}_{*}(S_{0},J_{1})
, as before. Hence, there is a unique
J_{2}\in\mathsf{Ch}_{\Delta}(J_{1})
and
S_{2}\in\mathsf{Sh}(S_{1},J_{2})
such that
x\in S_{2}
and
J\subset J_{2}
.

Arguing this way we obtain a sequence
\{S_{i}\}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{k}
such that
S_{i}\supset S_{i+1}
,
J_{S_{i}}\supsetneq J_{S_{i+1}}
,
x\in S_{i}
, and
J\subset J_{S_{i}}
. If there is
j\geq 0
such that
S_{j}\in\mathsf{Good}_{j}(P)
, then we can take
Q=S_{j}
and we are done because
Q\in\mathsf{Ch}(P,\mathcal{T})
.

If there is no such
j\geq 0
, then the sequence
\{S_{i}\}
is infinite and we have
S_{i}\in\mathsf{Sh}_{i}(P)
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. But this is impossible by Remark
7.5
(or simply by recalling that
J\subset J_{S_{i}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{S_{i}})=3^{-i}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{P})
).

In conclusion, we have found
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
such that
x\in Q
and
J\subset J_{Q}
. This closes the induction.
∎

We will need the following corollary. It can be seen as a converse to Lemma
8.6
.

Lemma 8.15
.

If
x\in E_{0}
,
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
, and
x\in Q\in\mathcal{T}
satisfies
J\cap J_{Q}\neq\varnothing
, then
J\subset J_{Q}
.

Proof.

Recall that both
J
and
J_{Q}
are triadic intervals. Since
J\cap J_{Q}\neq\varnothing
we have either
J\subset J_{Q}
or
J_{Q}\subsetneq J
. Suppose it is the latter.

Let
k\geq 0
be such that
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
. By Lemma
8.14
, there exists a unique
P\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
such that
x\in P
and
J\subset J_{P}
. Since
J_{Q}\subsetneq J
, we get that
J_{Q}\subsetneq J_{P}
. Taking into account that
x\in Q\cap P
,
(T4)
implies
Q\prec P
. Then, by
(T3)
(applied with
k=l
, and using the uniqueness assertion) we get that
Q=P
. This contradicts
J_{Q}\subsetneq J_{P}
.
∎

We move on to the proof of
(T8)
, which we recall below.

Lemma 8.16
.

Property
(T8)
holds: for every
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
and
x\in Q
we have

\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.8J_{Q},0,k)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{2},

(8.17)

where

\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.8J_{Q},0,k)=\{0\leq j\leq k\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,X(x,0.8J_{Q},\rho^{j+1},\rho^{j})\cap E\neq\varnothing\}

and

\mathcal{E}_{2}=A\mathcal{E}_{1}
.

We split the proof into several steps.

Lemma 8.17
.

For every
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
there exists
x_{0}\in Q
such that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}\cap G(x_{0},k))\geq(1-2\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}).

(8.18)

Proof.

By the property
(T2)
of
\mathcal{T}
we know that

\int_{Q}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}\cap G(x,k))\,d\mu(x)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q).

(8.19)

At the same time, if
Q_{B}\coloneqq\{x\in Q\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}\cap G(x,k))\leq(1-2\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\}
then

\int_{Q}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}\cap G(x,k))\,d\mu(x)\leq\int_{Q_{B}}(1-2\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\,d\mu(x)+\int_{Q\setminus Q_{B}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\,d\mu(x)\\
=\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})((1-2\varepsilon)\mu(Q_{B})+\mu(Q\setminus Q_{B}))=\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)-2\varepsilon\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q_{B}).

Comparing this to (
8.19
) we get

(1-\varepsilon)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)-2\varepsilon\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q_{B}),

which gives
\mu(Q_{B})\leq\mu(Q)/2
. In particular, there exists
x_{0}\in Q\setminus Q_{B}
, so that (
8.18
) holds.
∎

Recall that by (
5.8
) for every
x\in E_{0}
,
I\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
and
y\in B_{I}(x,10\rho^{k})
we have the pointwise energy estimate

\int_{\rho^{k}}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(y,1.5I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I).

(8.20)

In particular, by the definition of
\mathcal{G}(y,k)
(Definition
7.1
) the estimate above holds for every
y\in E
and
I\in\mathcal{G}(y,k)
.

Fix
x_{0}\in Q
satisfying (
8.18
). We claim that

\#\mathsf{Bad}(x_{0},0.85J_{Q},0,k)\lesssim A\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{E}_{2}.

(8.21)
