Recall that
G(x_{0},k)=\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k)}I
, where
\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k)
is a family of disjoint triadic intervals, and
J_{Q}
is also a triadic interval. There are two cases to consider: either for every
I\in\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k)
with
I\cap J_{Q}=\varnothing
we have
I\subset J_{Q}
, or there exists (a unique)
I\in\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k)
with
J_{Q}\subset I
. We consider each case in a separate lemma.

Lemma 8.18
.

Assume that for every
I\in\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k)
with
I\cap J_{Q}=\varnothing
we have
I\subset J_{Q}
. If
0<c_{\varepsilon}<1
is chosen small enough, then we have

\int_{\rho^{k}}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x_{0},0.9J_{Q},\rho r,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}),

(8.22)

and consequently (
8.21
) holds.

Proof.

We begin the proof of (
8.22
). First, we apply (
8.20
) to each
I\in\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k)
satisfying
I\cap J_{Q}=\varnothing
to get

\int_{\rho^{k}}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x_{0},J_{Q}\cap G(x_{0},k),\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k),I\subset J_{Q}}\int_{\rho^{k}}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x_{0},I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\\
\lesssim\sum_{I\in\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k),I\subset J_{Q}}\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\leq\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}),

(8.23)

where in the last inequality we used the fact that the intervals in
\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k)
are disjoint.

We are in a similar situation as in the proof of Lemma
5.4
: we are going to apply Lemma
3.15
to transfer this estimate to the conical energy associated with
0.9J_{Q}
.

We take
J=J_{Q}
,
\delta=\ell(Q)=\rho^{k}
, and we define
\mathcal{I}
as the family of maximal triadic intervals contained in
J_{Q}\setminus G(x_{0},k)
. We need to check assumptions (b) and (c) of Lemma
3.15
.

The assumption (c) is straightforward to check. For
H\coloneqq\bigcup_{I\in\mathcal{I}}I=J_{Q}\setminus G(x_{0},k)
we have

\mathcal{H}^{1}(H)=\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})-\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}\cap G(x_{0},k))\overset{\eqref{eq:xnotbad}}{\leq}2\varepsilon\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}).

Recalling that
\varepsilon=c_{\varepsilon}M^{-1}A^{-1}
, we get (
3.17
) assuming
c_{\varepsilon}
is chosen small enough.

Now we are going to check assumption (b): for every
I\in\mathcal{I}
there exists
\theta_{I}\in 15I
and
y_{I}\in B_{I}(x_{0},C\delta)
with
\mu^{\perp}_{\theta_{I}}(y_{I})\leq M
. Fix
I\in\mathcal{I}
. By the maximality of
I
, we get that
I^{1}
intersects
G(x_{0},k)
, which implies that it contains some
J\in\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k)
. By the definition of
\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k)
, there exists
y\in E_{0}
such that
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(y)
and
y\in B_{J}(x_{0},10\rho^{k})
. Furthermore, by Lemma
5.3
, there exists
\theta_{J}\in 3J
such that
\mu_{\theta_{J}}^{\perp}(y)\leq M
. Note that
\theta_{J}\in 15I
and
y\in B_{I}(x_{0},C\rho^{k})
. Thus, setting
\theta_{I}\coloneqq\theta_{J}
and
y_{I}\coloneqq y
we get the desired property of
\mathcal{I}
for some
\alpha\sim 1
.

We have checked all the assumptions of Lemma
3.15
, and so we get that for all
r\geq\rho^{k}

\mu(X(x_{0},0.9J_{Q},r,2r))\lesssim\mu(X(x_{0},J_{Q}\cap G(x_{0},k),r/2,4r)).

Fix
r^{\prime}\in[\rho^{k-1},1]
. Applying the estimate above with
r_{j}=\{2^{j}\rho r^{\prime}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
, and summing over
j\in\{0,1,\dots,\lceil-\log_{2}\rho\rceil\}
yields

\mu(X(x_{0},0.9J_{Q},\rho r^{\prime},r^{\prime}))\lesssim\mu(X(x_{0},J_{Q}\cap G(x_{0},k),\rho r^{\prime}/2,4r^{\prime})).

Integrating over
r^{\prime}\in[2\rho^{k-1},1/4]
yields

\int_{2\rho^{k-1}}^{1/4}\frac{\mu(X(x_{0},0.9J_{Q},\rho r,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\int_{2\rho^{k-1}}^{1/4}\frac{\mu(X(x_{0},J_{Q}\cap G(x_{0},k),\rho r/2,4r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\\
\lesssim\int_{\rho^{k-1}}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x_{0},J_{Q}\cap G(x_{0},k),\rho r,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\overset{\eqref{eq:suppl-est}}{\lesssim}\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}).

(8.24)

We also have

\int_{\rho^{k}}^{2\rho^{k-1}}\frac{\mu(X(x_{0},0.9J_{Q},\rho r,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}+\int_{1/4}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x_{0},0.9J_{Q},\rho r,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\\
\lesssim\frac{\mu(B_{J_{Q}}(x_{0},C\rho^{k-1}))}{\rho^{k}}+\mu(B_{J_{Q}}(x_{0},C))\leq\frac{\mu(C^{\prime}B_{Q})}{\rho^{k}}+\mu(C^{\prime}\rho^{-k}B_{Q})\overset{\eqref{eq:meas-upper-bd}}{\lesssim}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}).

(8.25)

Since
M\leq\mathcal{E}_{1}
, the estimate above together with (
8.24
) gives (
8.22
).

It remains to derive (
8.21
) from (
8.22
). This follows immediately from estimate (
3.23
) in Lemma
3.19
:

\#\mathsf{Bad}(x_{0},0.85J_{Q},0,k)\lesssim A\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})^{-1}\int_{\rho^{k}}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,0.9J_{Q},\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}+1\lesssim A\mathcal{E}_{1}.

∎

Now we settle the second case.

Lemma 8.19
.

Assume that there exists
I\in\mathcal{G}(x_{0},k)
such that
J_{Q}\subset I
. Then, (
8.21
) holds.

Proof.

By (
8.20
) we have

\int_{\rho^{k}}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x_{0},1.5I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I).

(8.26)

By the estimate (
3.23
) in Lemma
3.19
this implies

\#\mathsf{Bad}(x_{0},I,0,k)\lesssim A\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)^{-1}\int_{\rho^{k}}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,1.5I,\rho r,r))}{r}\frac{dr}{r}+1\lesssim A\mathcal{E}_{1}.

Since
J_{Q}\subset I
we have
\mathsf{Bad}(x_{0},J_{Q},0,k)\subset\mathsf{Bad}(x_{0},I,0,k)
, and so the estimate above implies (
8.21
).
∎

Now that we have established (
8.21
) we are ready to finish the proof of Lemma
8.16
.

Proof of Lemma
8.16
.

Let
x\in Q
, and let
x_{0}\in Q
be such that (
8.21
) holds. By Lemma
3.11

X(x,0.8J_{Q},\rho r,r)\subset X(x_{0},0.85J_{Q},\rho^{2}r,\rho^{-1}r)\quad\text{if $r>Cd_{J_{Q}}(x,x_{0})$,}

(8.27)

where
C\geq 1
is an absolute constant.

Recall that
d_{J_{Q}}(x,x_{0})\leq\diam_{{J_{Q}}}(Q)\leq 8\rho^{k}
by
(T1)
. We claim that there is an absolute constant
C^{\prime}\geq 1
such that for every
j\in\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.8J_{Q},0,k)
with
C^{\prime}\leq j\leq k-C^{\prime}
we have

\{j-1,j,j+1\}\cap\mathsf{Bad}(x_{0},0.85J_{Q},0,k)\neq\varnothing.

(8.28)

Indeed, for any such
j
we have

\rho^{j}\geq\rho^{k-C^{\prime}}\geq\frac{\rho^{-C^{\prime}}}{8}d_{J_{Q}}(x,x_{0})\geq Cd_{J_{Q}}(x,x_{0})

assuming
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(\rho,C)
is large enough. Thus, applying (
8.27
) with
r=\rho^{j}
yields

\varnothing\neq E\cap X(x,0.8J_{Q},\rho^{j+1},\rho^{j})\subset E\cap X(x_{0},0.85J_{Q},\rho^{j+2},\rho^{j-1}).

This implies (
8.28
). Hence,

\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.8J_{Q},0,k)\lesssim\#\mathsf{Bad}(x_{0},0.85J_{Q},0,k)+1\overset{\eqref{eq:des1}}{\lesssim}A\mathcal{E}_{1}.

∎

This concludes the proof of Proposition
7.3
.

9.
From conical energies to
\mathsf{Bad}
cubes

Recall that our goal is proving the energy estimate from Proposition
5.9
, that is,

\int_{E_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,\widetilde{G_{2}}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}d\mu(x)\lesssim(AM)^{11}\mathcal{E}_{1}\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E),

(9.1)

where

\widetilde{G_{2}}(x)=\bigcup_{J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)}15J.

Observe that by Lemma
8.14
for any
x\in E_{0}
and
k\geq 0
we have

\widetilde{G_{2}}(x)\subset\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,x\in Q}15J_{Q}.

This leads us to the following definition of family
\mathsf{Bad}\subset\mathcal{T}
:
Q\in\mathsf{Bad}
if there exists
x\in Q
such that

X(x,15J_{Q},\rho\,\ell(Q),\ell(Q))\cap E\neq\varnothing.

Later on we will show the following packing estimate for
\mathsf{Bad}
.

Proposition 9.1
.

We have

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)\lesssim(AM)^{10}\mathcal{E}_{2}\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E).

Now we show how Proposition
9.1
implies the energy estimate (
9.1
).

Proof of Proposition
5.9
using Proposition
9.1
.

Let
x\in E_{0}
. By the definition of
\widetilde{G_{2}}(x)
,

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,\widetilde{G_{2}}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,\bigcup_{J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)}15J,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\\
\leq\sum_{J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,15J,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}.

(9.2)

Now fix
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
. By (
3.15
)

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,15J,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,15J,\rho r,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}.

(9.3)

Recall that

\mathsf{Bad}(x,15J)=\{k\geq 0\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}E\cap X(x,15J,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k})\neq\varnothing\}.

We are going to use estimate (
3.24
) from Lemma
3.19
. By Lemma
5.3
the assumption involving the maximal function is satisfied with
y=x
, and so we may apply (
3.24
) with arbitrarily large
j
. Letting
j\to\infty
yields

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,15J,\rho r,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\cdot\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,15J).

Let
k\in\mathsf{Bad}(x,15J)
. By
(T7)
, there exists a unique
Q\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
such that
x\in Q
and
J\subset J_{Q}
. Since
15J\subset 15J_{Q}
and
\ell(Q)=\rho^{k}
, it follows that

E\cap X(x,15J,\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k})\subset E\cap X(x,15J_{Q},\rho\ell(Q),\ell(Q)),

which gives
Q\in\mathsf{Bad}
. Thus, for every
k\in\mathsf{Bad}(x,J)
there is a unique
Q\in\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}_{k}
such that
x\in Q
and
J\subset J_{Q}
. Hence,

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,15J,\rho r,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\lesssim M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\cdot\#\{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}x\in Q,\,J\subset J_{Q}\}.

Observe further that, by Lemma
8.15
, for any
Q\in\mathsf{Bad}
such that
x\in Q
we have either
J\cap J_{Q}=\varnothing
, or
J\subset J_{Q}
. Therefore,

\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\cdot\#\{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}x\in Q,\,J\subset J_{Q}\}=\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,x\in Q}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J\cap J_{Q}).

Putting this together with the previous estimate, summing over all
J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
, and recalling that the intervals in
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)
are pairwise disjoint yields

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,\widetilde{G_{2}}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\overset{\eqref{eq:G11toG2},\eqref{eq:sum1}}{\leq}\sum_{J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,15J,\rho r,r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\\
\lesssim M\sum_{J\in\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,x\in Q}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J\cap J_{Q})=M\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,x\in Q}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{2}(x)\cap J_{Q})\\
\leq M\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}}\mathds{1}_{Q}(x)\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q}).

Integrating over
x\in E_{0}
and using Proposition
9.1
we get

\int_{E_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mu(X(x,\widetilde{G_{2}}(x),r))}{r}\,\frac{dr}{r}\,d\mu(x)\lesssim M\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q\cap E_{0})\\
\lesssim A^{10}M^{11}\mathcal{E}_{2}\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)=(AM)^{11}\mathcal{E}_{1}\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E)

and so (
9.1
) holds.
∎

It remains to prove Proposition
9.1
.
Recalling that the family
\mathsf{Roots}
satisfies a packing condition
(T5)
, to get Proposition
9.1
it suffices to prove the following.

Lemma 9.2
.

For every
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
we have

\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{T}(R)\cap\mathsf{Bad}}\mu(Q)\lesssim(AM)^{9}\mathcal{E}_{2}\mu(R).

Proof of Proposition
9.1
using Lemma
9.2
.

Using the decomposition of
\mathcal{T}
given by
(T6)
, as well as the packing estimates from Lemma
9.2
and
(T5)
we get

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)=\sum_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}(R)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{Q})\mu(Q)\\
=\sum_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}(R)}\mu(Q)\lesssim(AM)^{9}\mathcal{E}_{2}\sum_{R\in\mathsf{Roots}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\mu(R)\\
\leq\varepsilon^{-1}(AM)^{9}\mathcal{E}_{2}\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\mu(E).

(9.4)

Recalling that
\varepsilon=c_{\varepsilon}A^{-1}M^{-1}
finishes the proof.
∎

The rest of the article is dedicated to the proof of Lemma
9.2
.

9.1.
Trees of David-Mattila cubes

In the proof of Lemma
9.2
we will need to use the small boundaries property from Lemma
6.1
, which we cannot guarantee for our
ad hoc
cubes comprising
\mathcal{T}
. For this reason to every
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
we associate an auxiliary family of cubes
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
which consists of David-Mattila cubes contained in a fixed dyadic lattice
\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
.

Definition 9.3
.

Let
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
. We define
\mathsf{Tree}(R)\subset\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
to be the family of cubes
Q\in\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
for which there exists
P_{Q}\in\mathcal{T}(R)
with
\ell(P_{Q})=\ell(Q)
and
P_{Q}\cap Q\neq\varnothing
.

For the sake of brevity, for every
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
we set

d_{R}\coloneqq d_{J_{R}}.

Remark 9.4
.

In the proof of Lemma
9.2
we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{T}(R)
is a finite family of cubes, simply by truncating it at an arbitrarily small generation, and then proving the packing estimate which does not depend on the truncation. From now on we assume that is the case. This implies that
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
is also a finite family of cubes.

In the following lemma we show that
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
consists of a bounded number of trees.

Lemma 9.5
.

The family
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
has the following properties:

a)

For every
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
we have
Q\subset B_{Q}\subset CB_{R}
for some absolute constant
C\geq 1
.

b)

There exist pairwise disjoint cubes
R_{1},\dots,R_{k}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
with
\ell(R_{i})=\ell(R)
,
R_{i}\cap R\neq\varnothing
, and such that for every
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
we have
Q\subset R_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq k
. Moreover,
k\lesssim 1
.

c)

If
Q,S\in\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
satisfy
Q\subset S
,
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, and
\ell(S)\leq\ell(R)
, then we have
S\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
.

Proof.

We start with a). Suppose that
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, and let
P_{Q}\in\mathcal{T}(R)
be such that
\ell(P_{Q})=\ell(Q)
and
P_{Q}\cap Q\neq\varnothing
. Recall that there exists
x_{Q}\in Q
such that
Q\subset B_{Q}=B_{J_{R}}(x_{Q},50r(Q))
with
r(Q)\sim\ell(Q)
, and also that there exists
x_{R}\in R
such that
R\subset B_{R}=B_{J_{R}}(x_{R},4\ell(R))
. Since
P_{Q}\subset R
we have
Q\cap R\neq\varnothing
and so

d_{R}(x_{Q},x_{R})\leq\diam_{d_{R}}(B_{Q})+\diam_{d_{R}}(B_{R})\lesssim\ell(Q)+\ell(R)\lesssim\ell(R).

Hence,
Q\subset B_{Q}\subset CB_{R}
.

We move on to b). Let
\{R_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\in\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
be the family of all cubes satisfying
\ell(R_{i})=\ell(R)
and
R_{i}\cap R\neq\varnothing
. Clearly, all
R_{i}
belong to
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, so that
B_{R_{i}}\subset CB_{R}
by a). The
R_{i}
’s are disjoint because they belong to the same generation of
\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
.

Using Lemma
6.1
c) we get that the
d_{R}
-balls
5B(R_{i})
are disjoint, which together with the fact that
5B(R_{i})\subset B_{R_{i}}\subset CB_{R}
and
\ell(R_{i})=\ell(R)
gives that the number of
R_{i}
’s is bounded, i.e.
\#\mathcal{I}\lesssim 1
.

Let
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, so that there exists
P_{Q}\in\mathcal{T}(R)
with
\ell(P_{Q})=\ell(Q)
and
Q\cap P_{Q}\neq\varnothing
. Let
S\in\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
be the unique cube such that
Q\subset S
and
\ell(S)=\ell(R)
. Then,
S\cap P_{Q}\neq\varnothing
, and in particular
S\cap R\neq\varnothing
. This means that
S=R_{i}
for some
i\in\mathcal{I}
. This concludes the proof of b).

Regarding c), let
Q,S\in\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
satisfy
Q\subset S
,
\ell(S)\leq\ell(R)
and
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
. Let
P_{Q}\in\mathcal{T}(R)
be such that
Q\cap P_{Q}\neq\varnothing
and
\ell(Q)=\ell(P_{Q})
. Let
P_{S}\in\mathcal{T}
be the unique
\mathcal{T}
-ancestor of
P_{Q}
satisfying
\ell(P_{S})=\ell(S)
. In particular,
J_{R}=J_{P_{Q}}\subset J_{P_{S}}
and
P_{Q}\subset P_{S}
.

We claim that
P_{S}\in\mathcal{T}(R)
. Since
\ell(P_{S})\leq\ell(R)
,
P_{S}\cap P_{R}\neq\varnothing
, and
J_{R}\cap J_{P_{S}}\neq\varnothing
, it follows from
(T4)
that
P_{S}\prec R
. Moreover, since
J_{R}\subset J_{P_{S}}
we also have
J_{P_{S}}=J_{R}
. By
(T6)
this gives
P_{S}\in\mathcal{T}(R)
. Together with the fact that

\varnothing\neq Q\cap P_{Q}\subset S\cap P_{S}

and
\ell(S)=\ell(P_{S})
, this gives
S\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
.
∎

For any
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J_{R})
with
k\geq 1
denote by
Q^{1}
the dyadic parent of
Q
. We define
\mathsf{Stop}(R)\subset\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
as the family of cubes satisfying
Q^{1}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
but
Q\notin\mathsf{Tree}(R)
. It is easy to see that the cubes in
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
are pairwise disjoint, and that they are all contained in
CB_{R}
. Moreover, since
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
is a finite family we have

\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)}Q=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)}Q=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}R_{i},

(9.5)

where
R_{i}
are as in Lemma
9.5
b). We set

\widetilde{R}\coloneqq\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}R_{i}.

(9.6)

Note that

\mu(\widetilde{R})=\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)}\mu(Q)\leq\mu(CB_{R})\overset{\eqref{eq:doubling}}{\lesssim}AM\mu(R).

(9.7)

We have the following counterpart of Lemma
8.7
.

Lemma 9.6
.

For every
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)
and
C\geq 1
we have

\mu(CB_{Q})\lesssim CM\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q)

(9.8)

and

\mu(Q)\gtrsim A^{-1}\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q).

(9.9)

In particular,

\mu(CB_{Q})\lesssim CAM\,\mu(Q).

(9.10)

Proof.

Suppose that
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
and let
P_{Q}\in\mathcal{T}(R)
be the cube such that
\ell(Q)=\ell(P_{Q})
and
Q\cap P_{Q}\neq\varnothing
. Since
CB_{Q}\subset C^{\prime}CB_{P_{Q}}
, the estimate (
9.8
) follows from (
8.2
). If
Q\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)
, then
Q^{1}\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
and then
CB_{Q}\subset CB_{Q^{1}}
so that

\mu(CB_{Q})\lesssim\mu(CB_{Q^{1}})\lesssim CM\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q^{1})\sim CM\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q).

To get (
9.9
), recall that

B_{J_{R}}(x_{Q},\ell(Q))\cap E\subset Q

by Lemma
6.1
c). Since
B(x_{Q},c\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q))\subset B_{J_{R}}(x_{Q},\ell(Q))
, the estimate (
9.9
) follows from Ahlfors regularity of
E
.
∎

Let
\mathsf{Bad}(R)\subset\mathsf{Tree}(R)
be the family of cubes
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
for which there exists
x\in Q
with

X(x,15J_{R},\rho\ell(Q),\ell(Q))\cap E\neq\varnothing.

Recall that our goal is Lemma
9.2
. We reduce the packing estimate for
\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}(R)
to a packing estimate for
\mathsf{Bad}(R)
.

Lemma 9.7
.

For every
P\in\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}(R)
there exists
Q\in\mathsf{Bad}(R)
with
\ell(Q)=\ell(P)
and
Q\cap P\neq\varnothing
.

Proof.

If
P\in\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}(R)
, then there exists
x\in P
with
X(x,15J_{R},\rho\ell(P),\ell(P))\cap E\neq\varnothing
. Observe that the unique cube
Q\in\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
with
\ell(Q)=\ell(P)
and
x\in Q
satisfies
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, and so
Q\in\mathsf{Bad}(R)
.
∎

Given
Q\in\mathsf{Bad}(R)
set

\mathsf{Bad}_{Q}\coloneqq\{P\in\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}(R)\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,\ell(P)=\ell(Q),\,Q\cap P\neq\varnothing\}.

Our previous lemma states that for every
P\in\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}(R)
there exists
Q\in\mathsf{Bad}(R)
with
P\in\mathsf{Bad}_{Q}
.

Lemma 9.8
.

We have

\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}(R)}\mu(P)\lesssim AM\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}(R)}\mu(Q).

Proof.

By Lemma
9.7
for every
P\in\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}(R)
there exists
Q\in\mathsf{Bad}(R)
with
P\in\mathsf{Bad}_{Q}
. Since every
P\in\mathsf{Bad}_{Q}
satisfies
P\subset CB_{Q}
, and
\mathsf{Bad}_{Q}
is a family of disjoint cubes, we have

\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Bad}_{Q}}\mu(P)\leq\mu(CB_{Q})\overset{\eqref{eq:doubling2}}{\lesssim}AM\mu(Q).

Thus,

\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}(R)}\mu(P)\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}(R)}\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Bad}_{Q}}\mu(P)\lesssim AM\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}(R)}\mu(Q)

∎

In the light of Lemma
9.8
, to prove Lemma
9.2
it suffices to prove the packing estimate for
\mathsf{Bad}(R)
.

Lemma 9.9
.

For every
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
we have

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}(R)}\mu(Q)\lesssim(AM)^{8}\mathcal{E}_{2}\mu(R).

(9.11)

We divide
\mathsf{Bad}(R)
into two subfamilies: “interior bad cubes”
\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
and “exterior bad cubes”
\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)
. We write

Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
if there exists
x\in Q
such that

X(x,0.5J_{R},\rho\ell(Q),\ell(Q))\cap E\neq\varnothing,

and
Q\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)
if there exists
x\in Q
such that

X(x,15J_{R}\setminus 0.5J_{R},\rho\ell(Q),\ell(Q))\cap E\neq\varnothing.

We prove the packing estimate for
\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
in Lemma
10.1
, and for
\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)
in Lemma
11.1
. Together they will imply (
9.11
).

10.
Interior bad cubes pack

In this section we prove the packing estimate for
\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
.

Lemma 10.1
.

For every
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
we have

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)}\mu(Q)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{3}\mu(R),

(10.1)

where
\mathcal{E}_{3}=A^{2}M^{2}\mathcal{E}_{2}
.

The estimate (
10.1
) is a consequence of property
(T8)
of
\mathcal{T}
. However, in order to use
(T8)
it would be convenient if the following were true: if
Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
, then
for all

x\in Q

X(x,0.5J_{R},\rho\ell(Q),\ell(Q))\cap E\neq\varnothing.

Unfortunately, the definition of
\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
only asserts that there
exists
some
x\in Q
for which this holds. To overcome this minor issue, we define an auxiliary family of bad cubes.

Definition 10.2
.

Let
\gamma=\rho^{-k_{\gamma}}>\rho^{-2}
be a large absolute constant to be chosen in Lemma
10.4
. Here
k_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{N}
.

We define
\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)\subset\mathsf{Tree}(R)
as the family of

Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
such that there exists
x\in B_{Q}\cap E
with

X(x,0.5J_{R},\gamma\ell(Q),\gamma^{2}\ell(Q))\cap E\neq\varnothing.

(10.2)

First, we show that every
Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
contains a relatively large sub-cube from
\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Suppose that
Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
. Then, there exists
P\in\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)
with
P\subset Q
and
\ell(P)\geq\gamma^{-2}\ell(Q)
.

Proof.

Let
\ell(Q)=\rho^{k}
. If
Q
contains
P\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)
with
\ell(P)\geq\gamma^{-2}\ell(Q)
then we are done. Assume it does not.

It follows that for all
m\geq k
with
\rho^{m}\geq\gamma^{-2}\rho^{k}
the cube
Q
can be partitioned into cubes from
\mathsf{Tree}(R)\cap\mathcal{D}_{m}(E,J_{R})
.
Let
m\geq k
be the smallest integer satisfying
\gamma\rho^{m}\leq\rho^{k+1}
. Then,
\gamma\rho^{m-1}>\rho^{k+1}
and so
\gamma^{2}\rho^{m}\geq\rho^{k}
because
\gamma\geq\rho^{-2}
.

Since
Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
, there exists
x\in Q
such that
X(x,0.5J_{R},\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k})\cap E\neq\varnothing
. Let
P\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\cap\mathcal{D}_{m}(E,J_{R})
be the unique cube containing
x
. Since
\gamma\rho^{m}\leq\rho^{k+1}
and
\gamma^{2}\rho^{m}\geq\rho^{k}
we have

X(x,0.5J_{R},\gamma\rho^{m},\gamma^{2}\rho^{m})\cap E\subset X(x,0.5J_{R},\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k})\cap E\neq\varnothing,

and so
P\in\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)
.
∎

Our strategy is the following: we are going to show that the cubes in
\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)
pack. Together with the packing of
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
, the lemma above, and the doubling property of
\mu
on
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
(
9.10
), this will imply the packing estimate for
\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
.

First, we show that the existence of a single point
x\in B_{Q}\cap E
satisfying (
10.2
) implies a similar property for all
y\in B_{Q}
(with a slightly larger cone).

Lemma 10.4
.

If
\gamma
is chosen large enough, then for every
Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)
and
y\in B_{Q}
we have

X(y,0.6J_{R},\ell(Q),\gamma^{3}\ell(Q))\cap E\neq\varnothing.

(10.3)

Proof.

Let
x\in B_{Q}\cap E
be such that (
10.2
) holds. We claim that for every
y\in B_{Q}

X(x,0.5J_{R},\gamma\ell(Q),\gamma^{2}\ell(Q))\subset X(y,0.6J_{R},\ell(Q),\gamma^{3}\ell(Q)),

(10.4)

assuming
\gamma
is chosen large enough. Indeed, by Lemma
3.11
we get that (
10.4
) holds as soon as
\gamma\ell(Q)\geq Cd_{R}(x,y)=Cd_{Q}(x,y)
, where
C\geq 4
is absolute. Since
x,y\in B_{Q}
, we have

Cd_{Q}(x,y)\leq CC^{\prime}\ell(Q)\leq\gamma\ell(Q)

assuming
\gamma
is large enough. This shows (
10.4
), and then (
10.3
) follows from (
10.2
). ∎

Lemma 10.5
.

The family
\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)
has bounded intersection:

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)}\mathds{1}_{Q}\lesssim_{\gamma}\mathcal{E}_{2}.

(10.5)

In consequence,

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)}\mu(Q)\lesssim_{\gamma}\mathcal{E}_{2}\mu(CB_{R}).

(10.6)

Proof.

Suppose (
10.5
) fails, so that there is a decreasing sequence of cubes

Q_{1}\supset Q_{2}\supset\dots\supset Q_{N}\quad\text{with}\quad\{Q_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}\subset\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)

with
N\geq C_{0}\mathcal{E}_{2}
and
C_{0}=C_{0}(\gamma)\geq 100
very large. For every
1\leq i\leq N
let
\ell(Q_{i})=\rho^{k_{i}}
.

By Lemma
10.4
for all
x\in Q_{i}
we have

X(x,0.6J_{R},\rho^{k_{i}},\gamma^{3}\rho^{k_{i}})\cap E\neq\varnothing.

(10.7)

Since
\{Q_{i}\}
is a nested family of cubes, we get that for every
x\in Q_{N}
(
10.7
) holds .

Let
P_{N}\in\mathcal{T}(R)
be a cube such that
\ell(P_{N})=\ell(Q_{N})=\rho^{k_{N}}
and
Q_{N}\cap P_{N}\neq\varnothing
(such cube exists by the definition of
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, Definition
9.3
). By
(T8)
applied to
P_{N}
we have

\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.8J_{R},0,k_{N})\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{2}\quad\text{for all $x\in P_{N}$.}

(10.8)

Fix
x_{0}\in Q_{N}\cap P_{N}
. Observe that both (
10.8
) and (
10.7
) (for all
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
) hold for
x_{0}
.

We claim that (
10.7
) implies that for every
3k_{\gamma}\leq i\leq N
we have

[k_{i}-3k_{\gamma},k_{i})\cap\mathsf{Bad}(x_{0},0.8J_{R},0,k_{N})\neq\varnothing,

(10.9)

where
\gamma=\rho^{-k_{\gamma}}
. Indeed, by (
10.7
) we have

X(x,0.6J_{R},\rho^{j+1},\rho^{j})\cap E\neq\varnothing

for some
j
with
\rho^{k_{i}}\leq\rho^{j+1}\leq\rho^{j}\leq\gamma^{3}\rho^{k_{i}}=\rho^{k_{i}-3k_{\gamma}}
. In particular,
k_{i}-3k_{\gamma}\leq j<k_{i}
. Assuming
3k_{\gamma}\leq i\leq N
we also get that
0\leq j\leq k_{N}
. This gives (
10.9
).

Observe that the intervals
[k_{i}-3k_{\gamma},k_{i})
can overlap at most
\leq 3k_{\gamma}
many times. Thus, (
10.9
) implies

\#\mathsf{Bad}(x,0.8J_{R},0,k_{N})\geq\frac{N-3k_{\gamma}}{3k_{\gamma}}\gtrsim N\geq C_{0}\mathcal{E}_{2}.

Comparing this to (
10.8
) and assuming that
C_{0}
is large enough leads to contradiction. This concludes the proof of (
10.5
).

Recalling that all the cubes in
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
are contained in
CB_{R}
, and integrating (
10.5
) over
CB_{R}
gives (
10.6
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
10.1
.

For every
Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
let
S_{Q}\in\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)
be the cube from Lemma
10.3
, so that
S_{Q}\subset Q
and
\ell(S_{Q})\geq\gamma^{-2}\ell(Q)
.

It follows from the doubling property (
9.10
) that

\mu(Q)\lesssim\gamma^{2}AM\mu(S_{Q}).

(10.10)

For a fixed
S\in\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)
we define

\mathcal{Q}(S)=\{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}S_{Q}=S\}.

We claim that
\#\mathcal{Q}(S)\lesssim_{\gamma}1
.

First, note that every
Q\in\mathcal{Q}(S)
satisfies
S\subset Q
and
\ell(S)\leq\ell(Q)\leq\gamma^{2}\ell(S)
. By Lemma
6.1
c) we know that for a fixed generation
k
the balls

\{5B(Q)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Q\in\mathcal{Q}(S)\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J_{R})\}

are disjoint. Since they are all contained in
C(\gamma)B_{S}
and satisfy
r(Q)\sim_{\gamma}\ell(S)
, we get that there are at most
\lesssim_{\gamma}1
many cubes in
\mathcal{Q}(S)\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J_{R})
. At the same time, there are
\lesssim_{\gamma}1
integers
k
such that
\mathcal{Q}(S)\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J_{R})
is non-empty. Thus,

\#\mathcal{Q}(S)\lesssim_{\gamma}1.

(10.11)

Using the packing estimate from Lemma
10.5
and the fact that the cubes in
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
are disjoint allows us to estimate as follows:

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)}\mu(Q)=\sum_{S\in\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}(S)}\mu(Q)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:doubl}}{\lesssim_{\gamma}}AM\sum_{S\in\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}(S)}\mu(S)\overset{\eqref{eq:overlaps}}{\lesssim_{\gamma}}AM\sum_{S\in\mathsf{B}_{int}^{*}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)}\mu(S)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:Bipack},\eqref{eq:stopR}}{\lesssim_{\gamma}}AM\big(\mathcal{E}_{2}\mu(CB_{R})+\mu(\widetilde{R})\big)\overset{\eqref{eq:doubling},\eqref{eq:wtRest}}{\lesssim}(AM)^{2}\mathcal{E}_{2}\mu(R).

This finishes the proof of Lemma
10.1
.
∎

11.
Exterior bad cubes pack

Recall that
\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)
consists of cubes
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
such that

X(x,15J_{R}\setminus 0.5J_{R},\rho\ell(Q),\ell(Q))\cap E\neq\varnothing.

In this section we prove a packing estimate for
\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)
.

Lemma 11.1
.

For every
R\in\mathsf{Roots}
we have

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)}\mu(Q)\lesssim(AM)^{6}\mathcal{E}_{3}\,\mu(R).

11.1.
Strategy of the proof

We are going to use the key geometric lemma stated below, which is a refinement of
[
Dąb22b
, Lemma 8.4]
. First we introduce some notation.

Definition 11.2
.

Given an open set
U\subsetneq\mathbb{R}
, the
Whitney decomposition
of
U
is a partition of
U
into dyadic intervals with the property
3I\subset U
and
3I^{1}\not\subset U
, where
I^{1}
is the dyadic parent of
I
.

It is easy to see that for any open
U\subsetneq\mathbb{R}
a Whitney decomposition exists: simply take the maximal dyadic intervals with the property
3I\subset U
.

Whitney decompositions have many nice properties (e.g. bounded overlaps between enlarged intervals) that have been used in analysis for decades, see e.g. Appendix J in
[
Gra14
]
or Chapter VI.1 in
[
Ste70
]
. In our application we won’t need these more sophisticated properties. All we will use is that Whitney decomposition of
U
forms a partition of
U
, and that if
I\subset U
is an interval with
CI\not\subset U
, then there exists a Whitney interval
J
such that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\sim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)
and
I\subset C_{0}CJ
where
C_{0}\sim 1
is absolute

Recall that
\pi_{J_{R}}=\pi_{\theta}
where
\theta
is the mid-point of
J_{R}
. For brevity, we set

\pi_{R}\coloneqq\pi_{J_{R}}.

Given
P\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, denote by
\mathcal{W}(P)
the Whitney decomposition of

U(P)\coloneqq\mathbb{R}\setminus\pi_{R}^{\perp}(\overline{P}).

Note that
U(P)
is an open set, so the Whitney decomposition is well-defined.

For
r>0
we set

\mathcal{W}(P,r)=\{I\in\mathcal{W}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}C^{-1}\lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})r\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\leq C\lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})r\}

where
C>2
is an absolute constant to be chosen later on, and
\lambda=c_{\lambda}M^{-1}A^{-1}
is the constant from Lemma
11.3
below.

For any
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
set

I_{Q}\coloneqq\pi_{R}^{\perp}(B_{Q})\subset\mathbb{R}.

Note that
I_{Q}
is an interval of length

\mathcal{H}^{1}(I_{Q})\sim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q).

The following is our key geometric lemma.

Lemma 11.3
.

There exist small absolute constants
0<c_{J}<1
,
0<c_{\lambda}<1
and a large absolute constant
\rho^{-1}<\Lambda\ll c_{\lambda}^{-1}
such that the following holds.

Let
\lambda=c_{\lambda}M^{-1}A^{-1}
.
Assume that

(i)

R\in\mathsf{Roots}
and
P,Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
satisfy
Q\subset P
and

X(z,0.5J_{R},\lambda\ell(Q),\Lambda\ell(P))\cap P=\varnothing\quad\text{for all $z\in\Lambda B_{Q}\cap E$.}

(11.1)

(ii)

there exists
x\in Q
such that

X(x,15J_{R}\setminus 0.5J_{R},\rho\ell(Q),\ell(Q))\cap E\neq\varnothing.

Then, there exists a Whitney interval
I\in\mathcal{W}(P,\ell(Q))
such that
I_{Q}\subset\lambda^{-1}\Lambda I
.

The proof of Lemma
11.3
is deferred to Section
12
.

We outline the proof of Lemma
11.1
. We would like to apply Lemma
11.3
. Recall that for every
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\setminus\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
we have

X(z,0.5J_{R},\rho\ell(Q),\ell(Q))\cap E=\varnothing\quad\text{for all $z\in Q$.}

This is similar to the assumption (
11.1
) except the truncation parameters don’t match, and we would like this to hold for all
z\in\Lambda B_{Q}\cap E
and not just for
z\in Q
.

To overcome these issues we will use the packing estimate for
\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
established in Lemma
10.1
to get a partition

\mathsf{Tree}(R)=\mathsf{Evil}(R)\cup\bigcup_{P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)}\mathsf{Branch}(P)

such that each
\mathsf{Branch}(P)
is a sub-tree with root
P
, for each
Q\in\mathsf{Branch}(P)
we will have (
11.1
), and the families
\mathsf{Top}(R)
and
\mathsf{Evil}(R)
pack:

\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)\cup\mathsf{Evil}(R)}\mu(P)\lesssim(AM)^{4}\mathcal{E}_{3}\,\mu(R).

Then, we will apply Lemma
11.3
inside each
\mathsf{Branch}(P)
, and with a little more work this will give

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)\cap\mathsf{Branch}(P)}\mu(Q)\lesssim(AM)^{2}\mu(P).

Together with the packing estimates for
\mathsf{Top}(R)
and
\mathsf{Evil}(R)
, this will establish Lemma
11.1
.

11.2.
Evil cubes

We begin by defining a new family of bad cubes called
\mathsf{Evil}(R)
.

For every
S\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)
let

\displaystyle\mathsf{Peer}(S)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,\Lambda^{2}B_{S}\cap\Lambda^{2}B_{Q}\neq\varnothing,\,\lambda\ell(Q)\leq\ell(S)\leq\lambda^{-1}\ell(Q)\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Near}(S)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,\Lambda^{2}B_{Q}\cap S\neq\varnothing,\,\ell(Q)\leq\ell(S),\,S\cap Q=\varnothing\}.

The constants
\Lambda>1
and
\lambda=c_{\lambda}M^{-1}A^{-1}
are the same as in Lemma
11.3
.

Remark 11.4
.

Without loss of generality we may assume that
\lambda=\rho^{k_{\lambda}}
and
\Lambda=\rho^{-k_{\Lambda}}
for some
k_{\lambda},k_{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{N}
. To do that, we slightly decrease
\lambda
and increase
\Lambda
. It is clear that Lemma
11.3
still holds with these modified parameters.

We define

{\mathsf{Evil}}(R)\coloneqq\{Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Lambda\ell(Q)>\ell(R)\}\cup\bigcup_{P\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)}\mathsf{Peer}(P)\cup\mathsf{Near}(P).

Our next objective is a packing estimate for
\mathsf{Evil}(R)
.

Lemma 11.5
.

For any
P\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)
we have

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Peer}(P)}\mu(Q)\lesssim(AM)^{4}\,\mu(P).

(11.2)

Proof.

Let
k\geq 0
be such that
\ell(P)=\rho^{k}.
Let
j\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
\lambda\rho^{j}\leq\rho^{k}\leq\lambda^{-1}\rho^{j}
. Recalling that
\lambda^{-1}\geq c_{\lambda}^{-1}\gg\Lambda
, we get that all
Q\in\mathsf{Peer}(P)\cap\mathcal{D}_{j}(E,J_{R})
satisfy
Q\subset\lambda^{-2}B_{P}
. Hence,

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Peer}(P)\cap\mathcal{D}_{j}(E,J_{R})}\mu(Q)\leq\mu(\lambda^{-2}B_{P})\overset{\eqref{eq:doubling2}}{\lesssim}AM\lambda^{-2}\mu(P).

Taking into account that the sum above is non-empty for
\lesssim|\log_{\rho}\lambda|\lesssim AM
many integers
j
, and that
\lambda\sim A^{-1}M^{-1}
, we get the desired estimate.
∎

The reason we needed the small boundary property of David-Mattila cubes (
6.3
) is to get the following estimate.

Lemma 11.6
.

For any
P\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\cup\mathsf{Stop}(R)
we have

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Near}(P)}\mu(Q)\lesssim AM\mu(P).

(11.3)

Proof.

Let
k\geq 0
be such that
P\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J_{R})
. Let
Q\in\mathsf{Near}(P)
. Since
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(P)=\rho^{k}
,
\Lambda^{2}B_{Q}\cap P\neq\varnothing
and
Q\cap P=\varnothing
, it follows that for every
x\in Q

\dist_{J_{R}}(x,P)\lesssim\Lambda^{2}\ell(Q).

Hence, if
l\in\mathbb{N}
and
\ell(Q)\leq c\Lambda^{-2}\rho^{k+l}
, then
Q\subset N_{l}^{ext}(P)
, where
0<c<1
is an absolute constant and
N_{l}^{ext}(P)
is defined above (
6.3
).

For
l\geq 0
let

\mathsf{Near}_{l}(P)=\{Q\in\mathsf{Near}(P)\,\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\,c\Lambda^{-2}\rho^{k+l+1}<\ell(Q)\leq c\Lambda^{-2}\rho^{k+l}\}.

Note that
\{\mathsf{Near}_{l}(P)\}_{l\geq 0}
are disjoint families of cubes, each
\mathsf{Near}_{l}(P)
consists of pairwise disjoint cubes, and for any
Q\in\mathsf{Near}_{l}(P)
we have
Q\subset N_{l}^{ext}(P)
. Moreover, if
Q\in\mathsf{Near}_{big}(P)\coloneqq\mathsf{Near}(P)\setminus\bigcup_{l\geq 0}\mathsf{Near}_{l}(P)
, then
c\Lambda^{-2}\ell(P)\leq\ell(Q)\leq\ell(P)
. Thus,

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Near}_{big}(P)}\mu(Q)\lesssim_{\Lambda}\mu(\Lambda^{3}B_{P})\overset{\eqref{eq:doubling2}}{\lesssim_{\Lambda}}AM\mu(P).

We estimate

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Near}(P)}\mu(Q)=\sum_{l\geq 0}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Near}_{l}(P)}\mu(Q)+\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Near}_{big}(P)}\mu(Q)\\
\lesssim_{\Lambda}\sum_{l\geq 0}\mu(N_{l}^{ext}(P))+AM\mu(P)\overset{\eqref{eq:doubl-DM}}{\lesssim}\sum_{l\geq 0}\rho^{l/2}\mu(3B_{P})+AM\mu(P)\lesssim AM\mu(P).

∎

We are ready to prove the packing estimate for
\mathsf{Evil}(R)
.

Lemma 11.7
.

We have

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Evil}(R)}\mu(Q)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{4}\,\mu(R),

(11.4)

where
\mathcal{E}_{4}\coloneqq(AM)^{4}\mathcal{E}_{3}
.

Proof.

We estimate

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Evil}(R)}\mu(Q)\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\Lambda\ell(Q)>\ell(R)}\mu(Q)+\sum_{P\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Peer}(P)\cup\mathsf{Near}(P)}\mu(Q)\\
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Peer}(P)\cup\mathsf{Near}(P)}\mu(Q)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:peer-est},\eqref{eq:near-est}}{\lesssim}|\log_{\rho}\Lambda|\cdot\mu(\widetilde{R})+(AM)^{4}\sum_{P\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)}\mu(P)+(AM)^{4}\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)}\mu(P)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:bad-est},\eqref{eq:wtRest}}{\lesssim}(AM)^{4}\mathcal{E}_{3}\,\mu(R)+(AM)^{5}\mu(R)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{4}\,\mu(R).

∎

Recall that by (
9.5
)

\widetilde{R}=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)}Q=\bigcup_{Q\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)}Q.

The following lemma will allow us to get (
11.1
) inside
\mathsf{Branch}(P)
.

Lemma 11.8
.

If
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\setminus\mathsf{Evil}(R)
then

X(z,0.5J_{R},\lambda\ell(Q),\Lambda\ell(Q))\cap E=\varnothing\quad\text{for all $z\in\Lambda^{2}B_{Q}\cap\widetilde{R}$.}

Proof.

Proof by contradiction. Suppose that there exists
z\in\Lambda^{2}B_{Q}\cap\widetilde{R}
such that

X(z,0.5J_{R},\lambda\ell(Q),\Lambda\ell(Q))\cap E\neq\varnothing.

Since
\lambda=\rho^{k_{\lambda}}
and
\Lambda=\rho^{-k_{\Lambda}}
for some integers
k_{\lambda},k_{\Lambda}\geq 1
, there exists
k\geq 0
such that

\lambda\rho^{-1}\ell(Q)\leq\rho^{k}\leq\Lambda\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)

(11.5)

and

X(z,0.5J_{R},\rho^{k+1},\rho^{k})\cap E\neq\varnothing,

(11.6)

where the last inequality in (
11.5
) is derived from
Q\notin\mathsf{Evil}(R)
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J_{R})
be the unique cube with
z\in P
. We claim that
P\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
.
Indeed, if
P\notin\mathsf{Tree}(R)
, then there exists a cube
S\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)
with
z\in S
and
P\subset S
(here we use that
z\in\widetilde{R}
,
\widetilde{R}
is covered by
\mathsf{Stop}(R)
, and
\ell(P)\leq\ell(R)
, recall Lemma
9.5
). It follows that
z\in S\cap\Lambda^{2}B_{Q}
, and
\ell(S)\geq\ell(P)=\rho^{k}\geq\lambda\rho^{-1}\ell(Q)
. There are two cases to consider:

•

If
\ell(S)\leq\ell(Q)
, then we have
Q\in\mathsf{Peer}(S)
, but this is a contradiction with
Q\notin\mathsf{Evil}(R)
.

•

If
\ell(S)>\ell(Q)
, then clearly
S\not\subset Q
. At the same time
Q\not\subset S
because
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
and
S\in\mathsf{Stop}(R)
. Thus,
Q\cap S=\varnothing
. But this implies
Q\in\mathsf{Near}(S)
, which again contradicts
Q\notin\mathsf{Evil}(R)
.

Since we reached a contradiction in both cases, we get that
P\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
.

Note that (
11.6
) and
P\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
imply
P\in\mathsf{B}_{int}(R)
. Moreover, (
11.5
) and
P\cap\Lambda^{2}B_{Q}\neq\varnothing
imply
Q\in\mathsf{Peer}(P)
. But this again means that
Q\in\mathsf{Evil}(R)
, which is a contradiction.
∎

11.3.
Dividing
\mathsf{Tree}(R)
into sub-trees

Recall that for every
Q\in\mathcal{D}_{k}(E,J_{R}),k\geq 1
we write
Q^{1}
to denote its dyadic parent: the unique cube
Q^{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{k-1}(E,J_{R})
with
Q\subset Q^{1}
.

Set

\mathsf{Top}(R)\coloneqq\{P\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)\setminus\mathsf{Evil}(R)\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ P^{1}\in\mathsf{Evil}(R)\}.

We decompose
\mathsf{Tree}(R)\setminus\mathsf{Evil}(R)
into sub-trees (or “branches”) associated to cubes from
\mathsf{Top}(R)
.

Definition 11.9
.

For every
P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)
let
\mathsf{Branch}(P)
denote the cubes
Q\in\mathsf{Tree}(R)
such that
Q\subset P
and there is no
S\in\mathsf{Evil}(R)
with
Q\subset S\subset P
.

We have

\mathsf{Tree}(R)\setminus\mathsf{Evil}(R)=\bigcup_{P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)}\mathsf{Branch}(P),

(11.7)

and the union above is disjoint. A packing estimate for
\mathsf{Top}(R)
follows easily from the packing estimate for
\mathsf{Evil}(R)
.

Lemma 11.10
.

We have

\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)}\mu(P)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{4}\,\mu(R).

(11.8)

Proof.

Since any cube
P\in\mathcal{D}(E,J_{R})
has only a bounded number of children (this follows from Lemma
6.1
c)), it follows that for every
Q\in\mathsf{Evil}(R)
there is at most a bounded number of cubes
P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)
with
P^{1}=Q
. Thus,

\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)}\mu(P)\leq\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)}\mu(P^{1})\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Evil}(R)}\mu(Q)\overset{\eqref{eq:evil-est}}{\lesssim}\mathcal{E}_{4}\,\mu(R)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{4}\,\mu(R).

∎

We are going to apply Lemma
11.3
with
P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)
and
Q\in\mathsf{Branch}(P)
. To do that, we need to verify that (
11.1
) holds for
Q\in\mathsf{Branch}(P)
.

Lemma 11.11
.

If
P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)
and
Q\in\mathsf{Branch}(P)
, then

X(z,0.5J_{R},\lambda\ell(Q),\Lambda\ell(P))\cap P=\varnothing\quad\text{for all $z\in\Lambda B_{Q}\cap E$.}

(11.9)

Proof.

Suppose that (
11.9
) fails, so that there exist
z\in\Lambda B_{Q}\cap E
and
x\in X(z,0.5J_{R},\lambda\ell(Q),\Lambda\ell(P))\cap P
. Let
S\in\mathsf{Branch}(P)
be such that
Q\subset S\subset P
and

x\in X(z,0.5J_{R},\lambda\ell(S),\Lambda\ell(S))\cap P.

Then, by the symmetricity of cones, we have

z\in X(x,0.5J_{R},\lambda\ell(S),\Lambda\ell(S))\cap E.

(11.10)

At the same time, since
d_{J_{R}}(x,z)\lesssim\Lambda\ell(S)
(this follows from (
11.10
) and Lemma
3.8
) and
z\in\Lambda B_{Q}\subset\Lambda B_{S}
, we have

d_{J_{R}}(x,x_{S})\leq d_{J_{R}}(x,z)+d_{J_{R}}(z,x_{S})\lesssim\Lambda\ell(S),

so that
x\in\Lambda^{2}B_{S}\cap P
. Thus, we have found a point
x\in\Lambda^{2}B_{S}\cap\widetilde{R}
with
X(x,0.5J_{R},\lambda\ell(S),\Lambda\ell(S))\cap E\neq\varnothing
. But this is a contradiction with Lemma
11.8
since
S\in\mathsf{Branch}(P)\subset\mathsf{Tree}(R)\setminus\mathsf{Evil}(R)
.
∎

We need two corollaries of (
11.9
). Recall that
I_{Q}=\pi_{R}^{\perp}(B_{Q})
.

Lemma 11.12
.

If
P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)
and
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathsf{Branch}(P)
satisfy
\ell(Q_{1})\leq\ell(Q_{2})
and
I_{Q_{1}}\cap I_{Q_{2}}\neq\varnothing
, then
B_{Q_{1}}\subset CB_{Q_{2}}
, where
C>1
is an absolute constant.

Proof.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
let
x_{i}\coloneqq x_{Q_{i}}\in Q_{i}
and
y_{i}\in B_{Q_{i}}
be such that
\pi_{R}^{\perp}(y_{1})=\pi_{R}^{\perp}(y_{2})
. Note that to show
B_{Q_{1}}\subset CB_{Q_{2}}
it suffices to prove that
d_{J_{R}}(x_{1},x_{2})\lesssim\ell(Q_{2})
.

Recall that
B_{Q_{1}}
and
B_{Q_{2}}
are
d_{J_{R}}
-balls. By the definition of metric
d_{J_{R}}
(see Subsection
3.4
)

|\pi_{R}^{\perp}(x_{1}-x_{2})|\leq|\pi_{R}^{\perp}(x_{1}-y_{1})|+|\pi_{R}^{\perp}(y_{1}-y_{2})|+|\pi_{R}^{\perp}(y_{2}-x_{2})|\\
\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q_{1})+0+\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q_{2})\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q_{2}).

Thus, to get
d_{J_{R}}(x_{1},x_{2})\lesssim\ell(Q_{2})
it remains to show
|\pi_{R}(x_{1}-x_{2})|\leq C\ell(Q_{2}).

Assume the opposite, so that
|\pi_{R}(x_{1}-x_{2})|\geq C\ell(Q_{2})
for some large
C>1
. Then, we may use the previous computation to estimate

|\pi_{R}^{\perp}(x_{1}-x_{2})|\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q_{2})\leq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})}{C}|x_{1}-x_{2}|.

If
C
is chosen large enough, we get
x_{1}\in X(x_{2},0.5J_{R})
. At the same time, since
x_{1},x_{2}\in P
we have
|x_{1}-x_{2}|\leq\Lambda\ell(P)
. Thus,

x_{1}\in X(x_{2},0.5J_{R},\lambda\ell(Q_{2}),\Lambda\ell(P))\cap P.

But this is a contradiction with (
11.9
) because
x_{2}\in Q_{2}\subset\Lambda B_{Q_{2}}\cap E
. ∎

For
k\geq 0
let

\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(P)=\{Q\in\mathsf{Branch}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ell(Q)=\rho^{k}\}.

Lemma 11.13
.

For
P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)
and any
k\geq 0
the family of intervals
\{I_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(P)}
has bounded overlaps:

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(P)}\mathds{1}_{I_{Q}}\lesssim 1.

(11.11)

In particular, for any interval
K\subset\mathbb{R}
we have

\#\big\{Q\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(P)\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ I_{Q}\subset K\big\}\lesssim\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(K)}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\rho^{k}}.

(11.12)

Proof.

Suppose that
Q,S\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(P)
satisfy
I_{Q}\cap I_{S}\neq\varnothing
. Then, by Lemma
11.12
we have
B_{Q}\subset CB_{S}
. At the same time, by Lemma
6.1
c) the balls
5B(Q)
with
Q\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(P)
are pairwise disjoint. Since
r(B(Q))\sim r(B_{Q})=r(B_{S})
, it follows that

\#\{Q\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}B_{Q}\subset CB_{S}\}\lesssim_{C}1.

This gives (
11.11
).

To see (
11.12
), recall that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I_{Q})\sim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\rho^{k}
for every
Q\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(P)
. Thus,

\#\big\{Q\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(P)\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ I_{Q}\subset K\big\}\lesssim\frac{1}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\rho^{k}}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(P)}\int_{K}\mathds{1}_{I_{Q}}(t)\,dt\\
=\frac{1}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\rho^{k}}\int_{K}\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(P)}\mathds{1}_{I_{Q}}(t)\,dt\overset{\eqref{eq:int-bdd-ove}}{\lesssim}\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(K)}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\rho^{k}}.

∎

We are ready to apply our key geometric lemma, Lemma
11.3
, to estimate the measure of exterior bad cubes in a fixed
\mathsf{Branch}(P)
.

Lemma 11.14
.

For every
P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)
we have

\sum_{S\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)\cap\mathsf{Branch}(P)}\mu(S)\lesssim A^{2}M^{2}\mu(P).

(11.13)

Proof.

Suppose that
S\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)\cap\mathsf{Branch}(P)
.
It follows from Lemma
11.3
that there exists
I\in\mathcal{W}(P,\ell(S))
such that
I_{S}\subset\lambda^{-1}\Lambda I
. Observe that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\sim\lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(S)\leq\lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(P)\sim\lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(I_{P}).

Since
I_{P}\cap\lambda^{-1}\Lambda I\neq\varnothing
, we get that
I\subset CI_{P}
for
C=C(\Lambda)
.

It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)\cap\mathsf{Branch}(P)}\mu(S)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{W}(P,\rho^{k})}\sum_{S\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(R)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}I_{S}\subset\lambda^{-1}\Lambda I}\mu(S)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:meas-upper-bd2}}{\lesssim}M\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{W}(P,\rho^{k})}\sum_{S\in\mathsf{Branch}_{k}(R)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}I_{S}\subset\lambda^{-1}\Lambda I}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\rho^{k}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:bounded number of intervals}}{\lesssim}M\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{I\in\mathcal{W}(P,\rho^{k})}\frac{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\lambda^{-1}\Lambda I)}{\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\rho^{k}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\rho^{k}

\displaystyle\lesssim M\lambda^{-1}\Lambda\sum_{I\in\mathcal{W}(P)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}I\subset CI_{P}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\lesssim M\lambda^{-1}\Lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(P)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:meas-lower-bd2}}{\lesssim}AM\lambda^{-1}\Lambda\mu(P).

Recalling that
\Lambda
is an absolute constant and
\lambda\sim M^{-1}A^{-1}
finishes the proof.
∎

We are ready to conclude the proof of Lemma
11.1
.

Proof of Lemma
11.1
.

We want to show

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)}\mu(Q)\lesssim(AM)^{6}\mathcal{E}_{3}\,\mu(R).

Clearly,

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)}\mu(Q)=\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)\cap\mathsf{Evil}(R)}\mu(Q)+\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)\setminus\mathsf{Evil}(R)}\mu(Q)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:evil-est}}{\lesssim}(AM)^{4}\mathcal{E}_{3}\,\mu(R)+\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)\setminus\mathsf{Evil}(R)}\mu(Q),

so it suffices to estimate the last sum. Recalling (
11.7
), we have

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)\setminus\mathsf{Evil}(R)}\mu(Q)=\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)}\,\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{B}_{ext}(R)\cap\mathsf{Branch}(P)}\mu(Q)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:bad-in-branch}}{\lesssim}A^{2}M^{2}\sum_{P\in\mathsf{Top}(R)}\mu(P)\overset{\eqref{eq:top-est}}{\lesssim}A^{2}M^{2}\mathcal{E}_{4}\mu(R)=(AM)^{6}\mathcal{E}_{3}\mu(R).

∎

Putting together the estimates from Lemma
11.1
and Lemma
10.1
gives

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}(R)}\mu(Q)\lesssim(AM)^{6}\mathcal{E}_{3}\,\mu(R)=(AM)^{8}\mathcal{E}_{2}\,\mu(R),

so that Lemma
9.9
holds. Together with Lemma
9.8
, we get

\sum_{Q\in\mathsf{Bad}\cap\mathcal{T}(R)}\mu(Q)\lesssim(AM)^{9}\mathcal{E}_{2}\,\mu(R),

which establishes Lemma
9.2
. We showed earlier that Lemma
9.2
implies Proposition
9.1
, and that Proposition
9.1
implies Proposition
5.9
. Together with the work done in Subsection
5.3
, this completes the proof of our Main Proposition, Proposition
4.1
.

12.
Key geometric lemma

In this section we prove Lemma
11.3
which was crucially used in the previous section. For the sake of possible future applications we will prove a more general, self-contained result stated below.

Lemma 12.1
.

Suppose that
\alpha>1
and
\rho\in(0,1)
. There exist constants
0<c_{J}<1
,
0<c_{\lambda}<1
, and
\rho^{-1}<\Lambda\ll c_{\lambda}^{-1}
, depending only on
\rho
and
\alpha
, such that the following holds.

Suppose that
M\geq 1
,
A\geq 1
, and let
\lambda=c_{\lambda}M^{-1}A^{-1}
. Assume that

(i)

J\subset\mathbb{T}
is an interval with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\leq c_{J}M^{-1}A^{-1}
,

(ii)

E
is an Ahlfors regular set with constant
A
, and
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{1}|_{E}
,

(iii)

for some
z_{0}\in E
and
0<r<R
we have a subset
F\subset E\cap B_{J}(z_{0},R)
, and a
d_{J}
-ball
B_{0}\coloneqq B_{J}(z_{0},r)
satisfying

\mu(\Lambda B_{0})\leq M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r

(12.1)

and

X(z,J,\lambda r,\Lambda R)\cap F=\varnothing\quad\text{for all $z\in\Lambda B_{0}\cap E$.}

(12.2)

(iv)

there exists
x\in B_{0}\cap F
such that

X(x,\alpha J\setminus J,\rho r,r)\cap E\neq\varnothing.

Then, there exists an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi_{J}^{\perp}(F)\cap I=\varnothing,

\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\sim_{\alpha,\rho}\lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r
, and
\pi_{J}^{\perp}(B_{0})\subset\lambda^{-1}\Lambda I
.

First, let us show how Lemma
11.3
follows from this more general statement.

Proof of Lemma
11.3
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Lemma
11.3
hold. Let
J=0.5J_{R}
, and note that

d_{J_{R}}(x,y)\leq d_{J}(x,y)\leq 2d_{J_{R}}(x,y).

Let
\alpha=30
, and let
\rho
be the absolute constant we fixed in Lemma
6.1
. Let
c_{J}^{\prime}
,
c_{\lambda}^{\prime}
and
\Lambda^{\prime}
be the constants depending on
\alpha
and
\rho
given by Lemma
12.1
. Since
\alpha
and
\rho
were absolute constants,
c_{\lambda}^{\prime}
and
\Lambda^{\prime}
are also absolute constants.

We define the absolute constants
c_{J},c_{\lambda}
and
\Lambda
, whose existence is asserted by Lemma
11.3
, as

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle\coloneqq C\Lambda^{\prime},

\displaystyle c_{J}

\displaystyle\coloneqq c\Lambda^{-1}c_{J}^{\prime},

\displaystyle c_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\coloneqq c\,\Lambda^{-1}c_{\lambda}^{\prime},

where
0<c<1<C
are absolute and will be chosen in the course of the proof.

Let
M^{\prime}=C\Lambda^{\prime}M
and
A^{\prime}=A
. Let
r=\ell(Q)
,
R=C\ell(P)
for some absolute
C\geq 10
,
z_{0}=x_{Q}
where
x_{Q}
is the center of
Q
as in Lemma
6.1
, and
F=P
. Let us verify the assumptions of Lemma
12.1
with these choices, and with constants
M^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime}
.

Assumption (i) holds because
J\subset J_{0}
and by Proposition
4.1
(b)

\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{0})\leq c_{J}M^{-1}A^{-1}\leq c_{J}^{\prime}(M^{\prime})^{-1}A^{-1}.

Assumption (ii) is trivial.

Regarding assumption (iii), it is easy to see that

F=P\subset E\cap B_{P}=E\cap B_{J_{R}}(x_{P},50r(P))\\
\subset E\cap B_{J}(x_{Q},C\ell(P))=E\cap B_{J}(z_{0},R),

where in the second inclusion we also used that
x_{Q}\in Q\subset P\subset B_{P}
so that
d_{J_{P}}(x_{Q},x_{P})=d_{J_{R}}(x_{Q},x_{P})\lesssim\ell(P)
.

Observe that
B_{J}(z_{0},r)\subset B_{J_{R}}(x_{Q},50r(Q))=B_{Q}
. Thus,

\mu(\Lambda^{\prime}B_{0})\leq\mu(\Lambda^{\prime}B_{Q})\overset{\eqref{eq:meas-upper-bd2}}{\leq}C\Lambda^{\prime}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r\leq M^{\prime}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r

which shows (
12.1
).

The assumption (
12.2
) follows from (
11.1
) because
\Lambda^{\prime}R\leq\Lambda\ell(P)
and

\lambda\ell(Q)=c_{\lambda}(AM)^{-1}\ell(Q)\leq c_{\lambda}^{\prime}(AM^{\prime})^{-1}r=\lambda^{\prime}r,

if the absolute constants denoted by
c
are chosen small enough and by
C
are chosen large enough. This verifies assumption (iii) of Lemma
12.1
.

Finally, assumption (iv) holds by the assumption (ii) of Lemma
11.3
.

Thus, we may apply Lemma
12.1
to obtain an interval
I^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi_{R}^{\perp}(P)\cap I^{\prime}=\varnothing
,
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime})\sim\lambda^{\prime}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q)
, and

\pi_{R}^{\perp}(B_{0})\subset(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda^{\prime}I^{\prime}.

Without loss of generality, assume
I^{\prime}
is a dyadic interval. Since
z_{0}=x_{Q}\in P
, we get that
\pi_{R}^{\perp}(x_{Q})\in(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda^{\prime}I^{\prime}\cap\pi_{R}^{\perp}(P)
and so

\dist(I^{\prime},\pi_{R}^{\perp}(\overline{P}))\leq\dist(I^{\prime},\pi_{R}^{\perp}(P))\lesssim(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda^{\prime}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime})\sim\Lambda^{\prime}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q).

It is easy to show that, since
\pi_{R}^{\perp}(P)\cap I^{\prime}=\varnothing
, we have

\pi_{R}^{\perp}(\overline{P})\cap I^{\prime}\subset\partial I^{\prime},

and possibly
\pi_{R}^{\perp}(\overline{P})\cap I^{\prime}=\varnothing
.
It follows that we may find a dyadic descendant
I^{\prime\prime}\subset I^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime})\sim\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime})\sim\lambda^{\prime}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q)
and such that

3\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime})\leq\dist(I^{\prime\prime},\pi_{R}^{\perp}(\overline{P}))\lesssim(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime}).

(12.3)

Furthermore, we may find another dyadic interval
I^{\prime\prime\prime}
with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime\prime})=\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime})
,

\dist(I^{\prime\prime},I^{\prime\prime\prime})\lesssim(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda^{\prime}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime})

(12.4)

and such that

3\,\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime\prime})\leq\dist(I^{\prime\prime\prime},\pi_{R}^{\perp}(\overline{P}))\leq 4\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime\prime}).

(12.5)

To do that, we consider dyadic intervals
\{I_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
with
I_{k}=I^{\prime\prime}+k\cdot\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime})
. It follows from (
12.3
) that for some
|k|\lesssim(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda^{\prime}
the interval
I_{k}
satisfies (
12.5
), and we set
I^{\prime\prime\prime}\coloneqq I_{k}
.

Note that
3I^{\prime\prime\prime}\cap\pi_{R}^{\perp}(\overline{P})=\varnothing
. We define
I
to be the maximal dyadic interval with
I^{\prime\prime\prime}\subset I
and
3I\cap\pi_{R}^{\perp}(\overline{P})=\varnothing
, so that
I\in\mathcal{W}(P)
is a Whitney interval for
U(P)=\mathbb{R}\setminus\pi_{R}^{\perp}(\overline{P})
. It follows from (
12.5
) that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime\prime})\sim\lambda^{\prime}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q)

and at the same time
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime\prime})\sim\lambda^{\prime}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q)
. Recalling that
\lambda^{\prime}\sim\lambda
, we get that
I\in\mathcal{W}(P,\ell(Q))
if the constant
C>1
in the definition of
\mathcal{W}(P,r)
is chosen large enough.

It remains to show that

I_{Q}=\pi_{R}^{\perp}(B_{Q})\subset\lambda^{-1}\Lambda I.

(12.6)

Recall that
\pi_{R}^{\perp}(x_{Q})\in\pi_{R}^{\perp}(B_{0})\subset(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda^{\prime}I^{\prime}
, and observe that
\pi_{R}^{\perp}(x_{Q})
is the center of
I_{Q}
. We have

\displaystyle\dist(\pi_{R}^{\perp}(x_{Q}),I)\leq\dist(\pi_{R}^{\perp}(x_{Q}),I^{\prime\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\dist(\pi_{R}^{\perp}(x_{Q}),I^{\prime\prime})+\dist(I^{\prime\prime},I^{\prime\prime\prime})+\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:rgerh}}{\lesssim}\dist(\pi_{R}^{\perp}(x_{Q}),I^{\prime})+\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime})+(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda^{\prime}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime})

\displaystyle\lesssim(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda^{\prime}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I^{\prime})\sim(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda^{\prime}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I).

Taking into account that

\lambda^{-1}=c_{\lambda}^{-1}AM=c^{-1}C(c_{\lambda}^{\prime})^{-1}A^{\prime}M^{\prime}=c^{-1}C(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}

and
\Lambda=C\Lambda^{\prime}
, it follows that
\dist(\pi_{R}^{\perp}(x_{Q}),I)\lesssim cC^{-1}\lambda^{-1}\Lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)
. Since we also have

\mathcal{H}^{1}(I_{Q})\sim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J_{R})\ell(Q)\sim(\lambda^{\prime})^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\lesssim cC^{-1}\lambda^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(I),

we get (
12.6
) if
0<c<1
is small enough and
C>1
is large enough. This concludes the proof of Lemma
11.3
.
∎

We begin the proof of Lemma
12.1
. Fix
0<r<R
,
F\subset E\cap B_{J}(z_{0},R)
,
B_{0}=B_{J}(z_{0},r),
and
x\in B_{0}\cap F
as in the assumptions. For brevity, set
\pi\coloneqq\pi_{J}
.

Let
y\in X(x,\alpha J\setminus J,\rho r,r)\cap E
. Observe that
y\in CB_{0}
for some
C=C(\alpha)>1
, and also

\displaystyle|\pi(x-y)|

\displaystyle\sim_{\rho}r,

(12.7)

\displaystyle|\pi^{\perp}(x-y)|

\displaystyle\sim_{\alpha,\rho}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r.

(12.8)

We are going to find an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi^{\perp}(F)\cap I=\varnothing,

\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\sim\lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r
, and
\pi^{\perp}(x)\in\Lambda I
. After that it won’t be too difficult to show that
\pi^{\perp}(B_{0})\subset\lambda^{-1}\Lambda I
.

Let

t_{\mathcal{G}}\coloneqq\frac{\pi^{\perp}(x)+\pi^{\perp}(y)}{2}\in\mathbb{R},\\

and

\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\bigg\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ |\pi^{\perp}(z)-t_{\mathcal{G}}|\leq 2|\pi^{\perp}(x-y)|,\ |\pi(z-y)|\leq\frac{|\pi(x-y)|}{2}\bigg\}.

Note that by (
12.7
) and (
12.8
)
\mathcal{G}
is a tube of dimensions roughly
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r\times r
. Moreover, for
z\in\mathcal{G}

|\pi^{\perp}(z-x)|=\left|\pi^{\perp}(z)-t_{\mathcal{G}}-\frac{\pi^{\perp}(x-y)}{2}\right|\leq 3|\pi^{\perp}(x-y)|.

(12.9)

and

|\pi^{\perp}(z-y)|=\left|\pi^{\perp}(z)-t_{\mathcal{G}}-\frac{\pi^{\perp}(y-x)}{2}\right|\leq 3|\pi^{\perp}(x-y)|.

(12.10)

Let
N
be a big integer satisfying

N=\lceil C_{N}AM\rceil,

(12.11)

for some big constant
C_{N}=C_{N}(\alpha,\rho)
whose precise value will be fixed later on.

We divide
\mathcal{G}
into
2N+1
subsets
\{\mathcal{G}_{i}\}_{i=-N}^{N}
defined as

\mathcal{G}_{i}=\bigg\{z\in\mathcal{G}\ \mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\ \frac{(2i-1)}{2(2N+1)}|\pi(x-y)|\leq\pi(z-y)\leq\frac{(2i+1)}{2(2N+1)}|\pi(x-y)|\bigg\}.

Each
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is a tube of dimensions roughly
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r\times r/N
. Since
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\leq c_{J}M^{-1}A^{1}
, if we choose
c_{J}
small enough (depending only on
C_{N}
) we have
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)\ll N^{-1}
. In particular, for each
\mathcal{G}_{i}
with
\mathcal{G}_{i}\cap E\neq\varnothing
we have

\mu(3\mathcal{G}_{i})\gtrsim_{\alpha,\rho}A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r.

(12.12)

Definition 12.2
.

For each
\mathcal{G}_{i}
with
\mathcal{G}_{i}\cap E\neq\varnothing
we define
z_{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{i}\cap E
to be a point satisfying

|\pi^{\perp}(z_{i}-x)|=\inf_{z\in\mathcal{G}_{i}\cap E}|\pi^{\perp}(z-x)|.

There may be many such points, and we just pick one.

Definition 12.3
.

If
-N\leq i,j,\leq N
and
\mathcal{G}_{i}\cap E\neq\varnothing
, we will say that
\mathcal{G}_{i}

beats

\mathcal{G}_{j}
if either
\mathcal{G}_{j}\cap E=\varnothing
, or

|\pi^{\perp}(z_{i}-x)|\leq|\pi^{\perp}(z_{j}-x)|.

Definition 12.4
.

For
-N+1\leq i\leq N-1
we will say that
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is
nice
if
\mathcal{G}_{i}\cap E\neq\varnothing
and
\mathcal{G}_{i}
beats both
\mathcal{G}_{i-1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{i+1}
.

Observe that
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is nice if and only if
\mathcal{G}_{i}\cap E\neq\varnothing
and

|\pi^{\perp}(z_{i}-x)|=\inf_{z\in(\mathcal{G}_{i-1}\cup\mathcal{G}_{i}\cup\mathcal{G}_{i+1})\cap E}|\pi^{\perp}(z-x)|.

Lemma 12.5
.

If
C_{N}=C_{N}(\alpha,\rho)
is chosen big enough, then there exists
-N+1\leq i_{*}\leq N-1
such that
\mathcal{G}_{i}
is nice.

Proof.

Observe that
y\in\mathcal{G}_{0}\cap E
, and so
z_{0}
is well-defined.
If
\mathcal{G}_{0}
is nice, we are done. Otherwise, either
\mathcal{G}_{-1}
or
\mathcal{G}_{1}
beats
\mathcal{G}_{0}
. Without loss of generality assume
\mathcal{G}_{1}
beats
\mathcal{G}_{0}
.

If
\mathcal{G}_{1}
is nice, we are done. Otherwise,
\mathcal{G}_{2}
beats
\mathcal{G}_{1}
. Repeating this reasoning
N-1
times, we either find a nice
\mathcal{G}_{j}
, or we get that for each
j\in\{0,\dots,N\}
the intersection
\mathcal{G}_{j}\cap E
is non-empty. Since
\{3\mathcal{G}_{j}\}_{j=-N}^{N}
have bounded overlaps, it follows that

\mu(3\mathcal{G})\gtrsim\sum_{j=-N}^{N}\mu(3\mathcal{G}_{j})\overset{\eqref{eq:Gi-lower-bd}}{\gtrsim_{\alpha,\rho}}N\cdot A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r.

Hence,

\mu(CB_{0})\geq cNA^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r

for some constants
0<c<1<C
depending only on
\alpha
and
\rho
. Recalling (
12.11
), we get

\mu(CB_{0})\geq cC_{N}M\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r.

But this is a contradiction with (
12.1
), assuming
C_{N}\geq c^{-1}
and
\Lambda\geq C
.
∎

Let
i_{*}\in\{-N+1,\dots,N-1\}
be the nice tube
\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}}
found in the proof above, and set
z_{*}\coloneqq z_{i_{*}}
. Note that

|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|\leq|\pi^{\perp}(y-x)|,

(12.13)

and the equality holds if and only if
i_{*}=0
and
y=z_{0}
.

We define

\mathcal{B}=\{z\in\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}-1}\cup\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}}\cup\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}+1}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}|\pi^{\perp}(z-x)|<|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|\}.

(12.14)

Lemma 12.6
.

We have

\mathcal{B}\cap E=\varnothing.

(12.15)

Proof.

If
z\in\mathcal{B}\cap E
, then
|\pi^{\perp}(z-x)|<|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|
. At the same time

|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|=\inf_{z^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}-1}\cup\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}}\cup\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}+1})\cap E}|\pi^{\perp}(z^{\prime}-x)|

because
\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}}
is nice. Since
\mathcal{B}\subset\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}-1}\cup\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}}\cup\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}+1}
, this leads to a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 12.7
.

We have

|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|\sim_{\alpha,\rho}\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r.

(12.16)

Proof.

The upper bound is immediate because
z_{*}\in\mathcal{G}
and we have (
12.9
) and (
12.8
). Now suppose that the lower bound fails, so that
|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|\leq c\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r
for some tiny
0<c<1
. Recalling that
|z_{*}-x|\sim\dist(\mathcal{G},x)\sim_{\rho}r
we get

|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|\leq c\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r\sim_{\rho}c\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)|z_{*}-x|,

and so assuming
c
is small enough we get
x\in X(z_{*},J,\lambda r,\Lambda r)\cap F
, which is a contradiction with (
12.2
).
∎

Let

t_{\mathcal{Y}}\coloneqq c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda\pi^{\perp}(x)+(1-c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda)\pi^{\perp}(z_{*})\in\mathbb{R},

for some small constant
0<c_{\mathcal{Y}}=c_{\mathcal{Y}}(\alpha,\rho)<1
, and consider the tube

\mathcal{Y}=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}|\pi^{\perp}(z)-t_{\mathcal{Y}}|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|,\ |\pi(z-z_{*})|\leq\Lambda R/2\}

Note that
\mathcal{Y}\subset B(z_{*},\Lambda R)
, and also that for
z\in\mathcal{Y}
we have

|\pi^{\perp}(z)-\pi^{\perp}(z_{*})|=\left|\pi^{\perp}(z)-t_{\mathcal{Y}}+c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda(\pi^{\perp}(x)-\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}))\right|\\
\leq 2c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|\overset{\eqref{eq:z*x}}{\sim_{\alpha,\rho}}c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r

(12.17)

and

|\pi^{\perp}(z-x)|=|\pi^{\perp}(z)-t_{\mathcal{Y}}+t_{\mathcal{Y}}-\pi^{\perp}(x)|\\
\leq\tfrac{1}{2}c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|+(1-c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda)|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|<|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|.

(12.18)

Lemma 12.8
.

If
0<c_{\mathcal{Y}}=c_{\mathcal{Y}}(\alpha,\rho)<1
and
0<c_{\lambda}=c_{\lambda}(\alpha,\rho)<C_{N}^{-1}
are chosen small enough, then
\mathcal{Y}\cap F=\varnothing
.

Proof.

We will show that

\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathcal{B}\cup X(z_{*},J,\lambda r,\Lambda R).

Then, the claim follows from (
12.15
) and (
12.2
). Suppose the contrary, so that there exists

z\in\mathcal{Y}\setminus(\mathcal{B}\cup X(z_{*},J,\lambda r,\Lambda R)).

If
\lambda r\leq|z-z_{*}|\leq\Lambda R
, then
z\notin X(z_{*},J)
, which implies

|\pi^{\perp}(z)-\pi^{\perp}(z_{*})|\geq c\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)|z-z_{*}|\geq c\lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r,

but this contradicts (
12.17
), assuming
c_{\mathcal{Y}}
is small enough. So either
|z-z_{*}|\geq\Lambda R
or
|z-z_{*}|\leq\lambda r
. The former is not the case because
\mathcal{Y}\subset B(z_{*},\Lambda R)
.

So we have
|z-z_{*}|\leq\lambda r
. We will show that this implies
z\in\mathcal{B}
. First, let’s show that
z\in\mathcal{G}
. Observe that

\displaystyle|t_{\mathcal{Y}}-t_{\mathcal{G}}|

\displaystyle=|c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda\pi^{\perp}(x)+(1-c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda)\pi^{\perp}(z_{*})-0.5\pi^{\perp}(x)-0.5\pi^{\perp}(y)|

\displaystyle=|c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda\pi^{\perp}(x-0.5x-0.5y)+(1-c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda)(\pi^{\perp}(z_{*})-t_{\mathcal{G}})|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda\frac{|\pi^{\perp}(x-y)|}{2}+(1-c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda)|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*})-t_{\mathcal{G}}|\leq(2-2.5c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda)|\pi^{\perp}(x-y)|,

where in the last inequality we used the fact that
|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*})-t_{\mathcal{G}}|\leq 2|\pi^{\perp}(x-y)|
because
z_{*}\in\mathcal{G}
. Hence,

|\pi^{\perp}(z)-t_{\mathcal{G}}|\leq|\pi^{\perp}(z)-t_{\mathcal{Y}}|+|t_{\mathcal{Y}}-t_{\mathcal{G}}|\\
\leq c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|+(2-2.5c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda)|\pi^{\perp}(x-y)|\overset{\eqref{eq:z*y}}{\leq}2|\pi^{\perp}(x-y)|.

This shows that
\pi^{\perp}(z)\in\pi^{\perp}(\mathcal{G})
.

Now suppose that
c_{\lambda}\ll C_{N}^{-1}
, so that
\lambda=c_{\lambda}A^{-1}M^{-1}\ll N^{-1}=\lceil C_{N}AM\rceil^{-1}
. Then,

|z-z_{*}|\leq\lambda r\leq C\lambda|\pi(x-y)|\leq\frac{1}{2N+1}|\pi(x-y)|.

Using also the fact that
z_{*}\in\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}}
, we get

\pi(z-y)=\pi(z-z_{*})+\pi(z_{*}-y)\leq\frac{1}{2N+1}|\pi(x-y)|+\frac{(2i_{*}+1)}{2(2N+1)}|\pi(x-y)|\\
=\frac{(2(i_{*}+1)+1)}{2(2N+1)}|\pi(x-y)|,

and similarly

\pi(z-y)=\pi(z-z_{*})+\pi(z_{*}-y)\geq\frac{(2i_{*}-1)}{2(2N+1)}|\pi(x-y)|-\frac{1}{2N+1}|\pi(x-y)|\\
=\frac{(2(i_{*}-1)-1)}{2(2N+1)}|\pi(x-y)|.

This shows that

z\in\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}-1}\cup\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}}\cup\mathcal{G}_{i_{*}+1}.

Together with (
12.18
), we get that
z\in\mathcal{B}
.
∎

Lemma 12.9
.

There exists an interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi^{\perp}(F)\cap I=\varnothing,

\mathcal{H}^{1}(I)\sim_{\alpha,\rho}\lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r
, and
\pi^{\perp}(B_{0})\subset\Lambda\lambda^{-1}I
.

Proof.

Observe that
I\coloneqq\pi^{\perp}(\mathcal{Y})
is an interval centered at
t_{\mathcal{Y}}
of length

c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|\sim_{\alpha}\lambda\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r

Since

|\pi^{\perp}(x)-t_{\mathcal{Y}}|=|\pi^{\perp}(x)-c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda\pi^{\perp}(x)-(1-c_{\mathcal{Y}}\lambda)\pi^{\perp}(z_{*})|\leq|\pi^{\perp}(z_{*}-x)|,

we get that
\pi^{\perp}(x)\in C\lambda^{-1}I
. Since
\pi^{\perp}(B_{0})
is an interval of length
\sim\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)r
containing
\pi^{\perp}(x)
, this further implies that
\pi^{\perp}(B_{0})\subset\lambda^{-1}\Lambda I
assuming
\Lambda
large enough.

It remains to show that

I\cap\pi^{\perp}(F)=\varnothing

(12.19)

Recall that
\mathcal{Y}\cap F=\varnothing
and
F\subset B_{J}(z_{0},R)
. Thus, to show (
12.19
) it suffices to prove

(\pi^{\perp})^{-1}(I)\cap B_{J}(z_{0},R)\subset\mathcal{Y}.

Recall that
I=\pi^{\perp}(\mathcal{Y})
and
\mathcal{Y}=\pi^{-1}(\pi(\mathcal{Y}))\cap(\pi^{\perp})^{-1}(I)
. We are going to show that
B_{J}(z_{0},R)\subset\pi^{-1}(\pi(\mathcal{Y}))
.

By the definition of
\mathcal{Y}

\pi^{-1}(\pi(\mathcal{Y}))=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}|\pi(z-z_{*})|\leq\Lambda R/2\}.

Recall that
z_{*}\in\mathcal{G}\subset CB_{0}=B_{J}(z_{0},Cr)
. Thus, for any
z\in B_{J}(z_{0},R)
we have

|\pi(z-z_{*})|\leq|\pi(z-z_{0})|+|\pi(z_{0}-z_{*})|\leq R+Cr\leq\Lambda R/2

assuming
\Lambda
is large enough. This gives
B_{J}(z_{0},R)\subset\pi^{-1}(\pi(\mathcal{Y}))
and finishes the proof of (
12.19
). ∎

Appendix A
Reduction to parallel segments

In this section we prove that it suffices to prove Theorem
1.1
under the additional assumption that
E
is a finite union of parallel segments. Recall that in Theorem
1.1
we assume that

E
is Ahlfors regular with constant
A
,
\Fav(E)\geq\kappa\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
.

(A.1)

Lemma A.1
.

Suppose that there exist functions
\phi,\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that the following holds: if
E\subset[0,1]^{2}
is a finite union of parallel segments and it satisfies (
A.1
), then there exists a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
such that
\lip(\Gamma)\leq\phi(\kappa,A)
and

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap\Gamma)\geq\psi(\kappa,A)\cdot\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

Then, there is an absolute constant
C>1
such that for every
E\subset[0,1]^{2}
satisfying (
A.1
) there exists a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
such that
\lip(\Gamma)\lesssim\phi(C^{-1}A^{-1}\kappa,CA^{2})
and

\mathcal{H}^{1}(F\cap\Gamma)\gtrsim A^{-2}\psi(C^{-1}A^{-1}\kappa,CA^{2})\cdot\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

We begin the proof of Lemma
A.1
.

Suppose that
\phi,\psi\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
are as in the assumptions. Let
E\subset[0,1]^{2}
satisfy
\eqref{eq:Eassump}.
We need to find a Lipschitz graph covering a big piece of
E
.

Denote by
\mathfrak{D}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
the usual dyadic cubes in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and by
\mathfrak{D}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
the dyadic cubes with sidelength
2^{-k}
. For every
k\geq 0
let

\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)\coloneqq\{Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Q\cap E\neq\varnothing\}.

Note that for any
k\geq 0
the cubes in
\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)
are a covering of
E
.

Set

E_{k}\coloneqq\bigcup_{Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)}\partial Q,

where
\partial Q
denotes the boundary of a cube. For any
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
we have
\pi_{\theta}(Q)=\pi_{\theta}(\partial Q)
, and so

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E_{k}))=\mathcal{H}^{1}\left(\pi_{\theta}\bigg(\bigcup_{Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)}Q\bigg)\right)\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(\pi_{\theta}(E)).

(A.2)

In particular, we have
\Fav(E_{k})\geq\Fav(E)\geq\kappa\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
.

Observe that
E_{k}
may be written as a disjoint union
E_{k}=F^{1}_{k}\cup F_{k}^{2}
, where each
F^{i}_{k}
is a finite union of segments parallel to the
x_{i}
-axis. Taking into account that
\Fav(E_{k})\leq\Fav(F_{k}^{1})+\Fav(F_{k}^{2})
, we have
\Fav(F_{k}^{i})\geq\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
for some
i\in{1,2}
. Set
F_{k}\coloneqq F_{k}^{i}
.

Lemma A.2
.

The set
F_{k}
is Ahlfors regular with constant
\sim A^{2}
, and we have
A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{k})\lesssim A\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)
.

Proof.

We begin by proving Ahlfors regularity. Let
x\in F_{k}
. We will show that
A^{-2}r\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{k}\cap B(x,r))\lesssim A^{2}r
.

Assume first
0<r<C2^{-k}
. Since
F_{k}
is a union of segments of length
2^{-k}
, and the ball
B(x,r)
intersects at most
\lesssim 1
such segments, we immediately get

\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{k}\cap B(x,r))\sim r.

Now assume
C2^{-k}\leq r\leq\diam(F_{k})
. Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Q\cap B(x,r)\neq\varnothing\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}Q\subset B(x,r)\}.

Note that if
C>1
is chosen large enough, then every
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
is contained in
B(x,2r)
, and also every
Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)
intersecting
B(x,r/2)
is in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.

Observe that for every
Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)
we have

A^{-1}2^{-k}\lesssim\mathcal{H}^{1}(3Q\cap E)\lesssim A2^{-k}.

Since the family
\{3Q\}_{Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)}
has bounded intersections, it follows that

\#\mathcal{Q}\lesssim A2^{k}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(3Q\cap E)\lesssim A2^{k}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap B(x,2r))\lesssim A^{2}2^{k}r

and

\#\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\gtrsim A^{-1}2^{k}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(3Q\cap E)\gtrsim A^{-1}2^{k}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap B(x,r/2))\gtrsim A^{-2}2^{k}r.

Thus,

\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{k}\cap B(x,r))\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial Q)\lesssim A^{2}2^{k}r\cdot 2^{-k}=A^{2}r

and

\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{k}\cap B(x,r))\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial Q)\gtrsim A^{-2}2^{k}r\cdot 2^{-k}=A^{-2}r.

Thus,
F_{k}
is Ahlfors regular with constant
\sim A^{2}
.

Concerning the length estimate, observe that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)=\sum_{Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap Q)\leq A\sum_{Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)}2^{-k}\sim A\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{k})

and

\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)\sim\sum_{Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E\cap 3Q)\geq A^{-1}\sum_{Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)}2^{-k}\sim A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{k}).

∎

If follows from the lemma above that

\Fav(F_{k})\geq\frac{\kappa}{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)\gtrsim A^{-1}\kappa\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{k}).

Since each
F_{k}
is an Ahlfors regular finite union of parallel segments with large Favard length, we may use our assumptions on functions
\phi
and
\psi
to obtain a family of Lipschitz graphs
\Gamma_{k}
with
\lip(\Gamma_{k})\leq\phi(C^{-1}A^{-1}\kappa,CA^{2})
and

\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{k}\cap\Gamma_{k})\geq\psi(C^{-1}A^{-1}\kappa,CA^{2})\cdot\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{k})\\
\gtrsim\psi(C^{-1}A^{-1}\kappa,CA^{2})\cdot A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

(A.3)

By choosing a subsequence and slightly increasing the Lipschitz constants, we may assume that every
\Gamma_{k}
is a graph of a Lipschitz function
f_{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
over a fixed line
\ell
passing through the origin, and by applying a rotation, we may assume that

\Gamma_{k}=\{(y,f_{k}(y))\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}y\in\mathbb{R}\}.

Since
F_{k}\subset[0,1]^{2}
, we may also assume that
f_{k}
are bounded and compactly supported.

By applying the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem, we may choose a subsequence of
f_{k}
converging uniformly to another Lipschitz function
f\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\lip(f)\lesssim\phi(C^{-1}A^{-1}\kappa,CA^{2})
. Let
\Gamma
be the graph of
f
. To complete the proof of Lemma
A.1
it suffices to show that
E\cap\Gamma
is large.

Lemma A.3
.

We have

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma\cap E)\gtrsim A^{-2}\psi(C^{-1}A^{-1}\kappa,CA^{2})\cdot\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

Proof.

Fix
m\geq 0
and set

G_{m}\coloneqq\{x\in E\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\dist(x,\Gamma)\leq 2^{-m}\}.

Let
k_{0}\geq m+1
be so large that
\|f-f_{k}\|_{\infty}\leq 2^{-m-1}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
.
For any
k\geq k_{0}
set

\mathcal{F}_{k}\coloneqq\{Q\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(E)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}F_{k}\cap\Gamma_{k}\cap\partial Q\neq\varnothing\}.

Fix
Q\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
and
x\in 3Q
. Observe that

\dist(x,\Gamma_{k})\leq\diam(3Q)\lesssim 2^{-k}.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}
be such that

\dist(x,\Gamma_{k})=|x-(y,f_{k}(y))|\lesssim 2^{-k}.

Since
\|f-f_{k}\|_{\infty}\leq 2^{-m-1}
, we get

\dist(x,\Gamma)\leq|x-(y,f(y))|\leq|x-(y,f_{k}(y))|+|f(y)-f_{k}(y)|\\
\leq C2^{-k}+2^{-m-1}\leq 2^{-m},

assuming
k\geq k_{0}
is large enough. Since
x\in 3Q
was arbitrary, we get that
3Q\cap E\subset G_{m}
, and so

\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{m})\gtrsim\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(3Q\cap E)\gtrsim A^{-1}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{F}_{k}}2^{-k}\gtrsim A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(F_{k}\cap\Gamma_{k})\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:Fklarge}}{\gtrsim}A^{-2}\psi(C^{-1}A^{-1}\kappa,CA^{2})\cdot\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

Since
G_{m}
is a decreasing sequence of sets, we finally get that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma\cap E)=\mathcal{H}^{1}\bigg(\bigcap_{m\geq 0}G_{m}\bigg)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathcal{H}^{1}(G_{m})\gtrsim A^{-2}\psi(C^{-1}A^{-1}\kappa,CA^{2})\cdot\mathcal{H}^{1}(E).

∎

Appendix B
Constant dependence in Theorem
3.17

In this section we explain briefly how to obtain Theorem
3.17
, which is a version of
[
MO18
, Proposition 1.12]
with explicit parameter dependence. We repeat the statement below.

Theorem B.1
.

Assume that
\rho=1/2
,
E\subset B(0,1)
is an Ahlfors regular set with constant
A
,
\diam(E)=1
, and
F\subset E
is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-measurable with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)\geq\tau
. Suppose that
J\subset\mathbb{T}
is an interval with
\mathcal{H}^{1}(J)=\alpha\leq c_{J}
for some absolute
0<c_{J}<1
, and that for every
x\in F
we have

\#\mathsf{Bad}_{F}(x,J)\leq M_{0}.

Then, there exists a Lipschitz graph
\Gamma
with
\lip(\Gamma)\sim 2^{M_{0}}\alpha^{-1}
and

\mathcal{H}^{1}(F\cap\Gamma)\geq(c\alpha A^{-2}\tau)^{2^{M_{0}}},

(B.1)

where
0<c<1
is absolute.

The key technical proposition used in the proof of
[
MO18
, Proposition 1.12]
is
[
MO18
, Proposition 3.2]
stated below.

Proposition B.2
.

Suppose that
F\subset E
and
J\subset\mathbb{T}
are as in Theorem
B.1
. Assume that we have

\#\mathsf{Bad}_{F}(x,J)\leq M\quad\text{for all $x\in F$.}

Then, there exists a compact set
K\subset F
satisfying

\#\mathsf{Bad}_{K}(x,0.5J)\leq M-1\quad\text{for all $x\in K$}

and

\mathcal{H}^{1}(K)\gtrsim_{\tau,\alpha,A}1.

(B.2)

Claim B.3
.

Careful inspection of the proof of
[
MO18
, Proposition 3.2]
shows that in (
B.2
) we actually have

\mathcal{H}^{1}(K)\geq c_{0}\alpha A^{-2}\tau^{2}

(B.3)

for some absolute
0<c_{0}<1
.

Proof.

The relevant argument is contained on pp. 1065–1068 of
[
MO18
]
. The set
K
is obtained by an iterative algorithm summarized on p. 1065, and which involves Stopping Conditions 3.3 and 3.4. Over the course of the algorithm sequences of disjoint sets
D^{0},\dots,D^{k}
and
S^{0},\dots,S^{k}
are defined.

If the algorithm stops due to the Stopping Condition 3.4, then it is shown that

\mathcal{H}^{1}(K)\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)/4\geq\tau/4,

which is much better than (
B.3
).

The alternative is that the algorithm stops due to the Stopping Condition 3.3, which claims that the sets
D^{0},\dots,D^{k}
and
S^{0},\dots,S^{k}
defined over the course of the iteration satisfy either

\sum_{i=0}^{k}\mathcal{H}^{1}(D^{i})\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)/2\quad\text{or}\quad\sum_{i=0}^{k}\mathcal{H}^{1}(S^{i})\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)/2.

(B.4)

In both cases, we set
K=\bigcup_{i=0}^{k}S^{i}
. If the second estimate of (
B.4
) holds, then using that
S^{i}
are disjoint we get

\mathcal{H}^{1}(K)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\mathcal{H}^{1}(S^{i})\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)/2\geq\tau/2,

which is again much better than (
B.3
).

The remaining alternative is that
\sum_{i=0}^{k}\mathcal{H}^{1}(D^{i})\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)/2
. Luckily, the sets
S^{i}
satisfy

\mathcal{H}^{1}(S^{i})\gtrsim_{\tau,\alpha,A}\mathcal{H}^{1}(D^{i})

(B.5)

see the last displayed inequality on p. 1067, and so

\mathcal{H}^{1}(K)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\mathcal{H}^{1}(S^{i})\gtrsim_{\tau,\alpha,A}\sum_{i=0}^{k}\mathcal{H}^{1}(D^{i})\geq\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)/2\geq\tau/2.

(B.6)

It remains to make the estimate (
B.5
) explicit.

The set
S^{k}
is defined in the middle of p. 1067 as
S^{k}\coloneqq B(x_{k},r_{k})\cap F^{k,M}
. The sets
F^{k,M}
are defined in (IV) on p. 1065. The point
x_{k}\in F^{k,M}
satisfies

\mathcal{H}^{1}(S^{k})=\mathcal{H}^{1}(B(x_{k},r_{k})\cap F^{k,M})\geq\epsilon r_{k}

(B.7)

for certain
\epsilon=\epsilon(\tau,A)>0
fixed in the Stopping Condition 3.4. The radius
r_{k}
is defined as
r_{k}=c2^{-j_{k}}
for certain
c=c(\alpha)
fixed on p. 1068, where
j_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
is one of the “bad scales”. One can easily check that
c(\alpha)\sim\alpha
.

Concerning
\epsilon
, its definition in the Stopping Condition 3.4 invokes
[
MO18
, Lemma 2.1]
. Together with the (very short) proof of that lemma it is clear that one may take
\epsilon\sim A^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)
.
§
§

§

Literally using
[
MO18
, Lemma 2.1]
yields a worse bound, but a modified version of
[
MO18
, Lemma 2.1]
, where the assumption
\mathcal{H}^{1}(E)\geq c>0
is replaced by
\diam(E)=1
(which is what we assume in Proposition
B.2
), gives the claimed estimate. Modifying the proof is trivial.
Thus, (
B.7
) becomes

\mathcal{H}^{1}(S^{k})\gtrsim A^{-1}\alpha 2^{-j_{k}}\mathcal{H}^{1}(F)\geq A^{-1}\alpha\tau 2^{-j_{k}}.

(B.8)

On the other hand, the set
D^{k}\subset F\subset E
is contained in a single ball of radius
\lesssim 2^{-j_{k}}
, see the bottom of p. 1067. Thus,
\mathcal{H}^{1}(D^{k})\lesssim A2^{-j_{k}}
. Together with (
B.8
) we get

\mathcal{H}^{1}(S^{k})\gtrsim A^{-2}\alpha\tau\mathcal{H}^{1}(D^{k}).

Recalling the computation (
B.6
), we finally arrive at

\mathcal{H}^{1}(K)\gtrsim A^{-2}\alpha\tau^{2},

which is what we claimed in (
B.3
).
∎

Having Proposition
B.2
and (
B.3
) at our disposal, it is easy to get Theorem
B.1
by applying Proposition
B.2
iteratively.

Proof of Theorem
B.1
.

Set
K_{0}=F
. Applying Proposition
B.2
with
K_{0}
produces a set
K_{1}\subset K_{0}
with
